outrage, outrages would not exist; and that in consequence it is the
victims who are the authors of the mischief and the really guilty
persons. They obeyed the law under their hand of 1816, without dreaming
of the triumphant application which would be made of it against
religious liberty by their successors some sixty years later. Order was
maintained by them, and the liberties of a contemptible minority were
protected. Our ancient magistrates consulting neither their sympathies
nor their antipathies, nor even their sincere convictions concerning
what they believed to be for the public good, held it to be a matter of
honour and of conscience to fulfil the first duties of their position,
that of causing the rights of all, including those of the weakest
portion of the community, to be respected. A party Government, on the
contrary (such as is at present in power), does not scruple, in order to
excite and maintain the zeal of its present supporters, to cultivate
irritating questions, and even to create them if necessary. It is ready
to gratify the passions of the people on any question where there is a
chance of making use of them in their own interests, against persons
whose insignificant number makes them in no way dangerous. Herein we
find the distinction between a Government which is truly National and a
Government by a party.”

To return to the legal validity of the decree of the 2nd February;—not
only is there no law upon which it could have been substantially based,
but it actually violates in the most open manner the Constitutional
Guarantees which lie at the foundation of the political life of the
country. Article 2 of the Constitutional Law of the 26th August, 1868,
pronounces: “Liberty of worship is guaranteed throughout the whole
extent of Swiss territory. All forms of worship have an equal right to
the protection of the State.” This law, accepted by the people, is
binding not only upon the executive, but on the legislative power
itself. The duty of the Council of State was to have enforced respect
for it at all costs. The Council of State preferred to violate it, and
in doing so has by the same blow struck at the principles of the
inviolability of domicile, of freedom of speech, and freedom of public
meeting.

The following remarks on the subject appeared in the _Journal de Genève_
of February 3, 1883:—

  “We published yesterday a proclamation of the Geneva Government
  closing the meetings of the Salutists; to-day we receive the text of a
  proclamation of the Government of Neuchâtel on the same subject. These
  two proclamations present a great contrast the one to the other. That
  of Neuchâtel is founded on the text of the Constitution, which
  guarantees freedom of public meeting and freedom of worship. It
  reminds the authors of the disturbances, of the law against such
  outrages, and threatens punishment if they disobey. The Genevese
  Government, on the contrary, entirely ignores the Constitution. It
  does not contain a single word of blame for the individuals who for a
  fortnight past have been disgracing our town, breaking up meetings
  held in private houses, attacking inoffensive people in the streets,
  and perpetrating every kind of violence. It reserves its severity for
  the victims of these acts of violence, for the foreigners or Genevese
  citizens alike who have been attacked, insulted, hunted, and
  maltreated by bands of roughs who have remained, and evidently will
  remain, unchecked and unpunished. The victory which these bands of
  roughs have obtained over our laws is decisive and complete. The
  Government whose duty it is to enforce respect for the laws has
  lowered its flag in the presence of these rioters, and has made itself
  the executor of their will. Truly we blush for shame in reading this
  proclamation and in comparing the language of our Genevan Council of
  State with that of Neuchâtel. Is it possible that on this question in
  which are involved freedom of speech, and of meeting, individual
  liberty, the rights of citizens to go where they please, to listen to
  whom they please, and to be free from attacks in the streets, our
  Government has nothing to say? It seeks a miserable excuse for the
  disturbances in the text of an advertisement of the meetings which was
  previously submitted to its own judgment and the placarding of which
  it authorised. This advertisement was a very small affair indeed to
  justify not only the interference of the mob, but an assault by our
  Government itself on liberty of opinion.... If it presented the
  character which they now attribute to it, it was in the power of the
  police to have interfered, and everyone would have been satisfied with
  its decision. But six weeks have passed since it appeared, and if it
  was so dangerous as it is now asserted for the public peace, it is
  rather late to condemn it only to-day. All that has occurred confirms
  our suspicions on the more than equivocal part which the police have
  played since the commencement of these events, and proves almost to a
  certainty its complicity with the rioters. If from the first the
  police had seriously and sincerely wished to hinder the disturbances,
  they had abundant power to do so. They might then have brought into
  play Article 9 of the law of March 11th, 1816, but instead of this
  they tolerated and encouraged the rioters with the motive of reaching
  the end which M. Héridier confessed in the Grand Council he had in
  view, namely, of driving out of our midst people whose religion does
  not please him. Doubtless their religion does not please; but what
  merit would there be in tolerating it if it entirely pleased us? and
  what motive would there have been for inscribing this guarantee in our
  Constitution if it were only meant to be applied to cases in which it
  would be manifestly useless? The game is played! but now we must see
  that we do not any longer deceive foreigners, who, in the faith of our
  Constitution, may imagine that Geneva is a country of religious
  liberty. It will be necessary to change the text of the articles of
  the Constitution in which this mention of our rights is found. It must
  in future run thus:—‘Freedom of meeting is guaranteed; nevertheless,
  persons who know how to persevere in making sufficient noise, coupled
  with abuse and personal assaults, shall always be able to obtain from
  the Government the closing by the Police of any meeting whatsoever,
  even in a private house, which happens to displease them.’ Such is the
  _régime_ which the manifesto of the Geneva Government has inaugurated.
  There is not much in it to make one proud of one’s country. It is the
  true _arbitraire_, and the most wretched form of it, namely, that
  which is born of weakness and cowardice.”

On the 8th February, after having spent some time quietly at the house
of M. Lenoir in the neighbourhood of Geneva, Miss Booth was summoned to
the Police Court and subjected to a lengthy cross-examination.
Numberless questions were asked of her, and afterward of Miss
Charlesworth, which were of a personal and utterly unjustifiable nature,
some of them being of a significance better understood perhaps by men of
the character of their questioners than they could be by refined
Christian ladies. The following day Miss Booth was again called before
the Police, and was then suddenly requested to produce the account books
of the Salvation Army in Switzerland. Little imagining that this was the
point upon which was to be based her illegal expulsion from Geneva, Miss
Booth frankly and unsuspectingly replied: “It is impossible for me to do
so, as I am not the treasurer, and my secretary who has the books is now
absent from Geneva. But,” she added, “you shall have them as soon as she
returns.”

It was now that the monstrous charge was made against Miss Booth of
having broken a cantonal law _which has no existence_, but which was
improvised by the Chief of the Department of Justice and Police in order
to afford an excuse for the complete suppression of the Salvation Army
in Geneva. It is with shame and grief that the chronicler of these
events feels obliged to publish the plain fact that M. Héridier
falsified a cantonal law of Geneva, apparently deliberately, since the
falsification was repeated at a later date as we shall presently see, in
order to get rid of this person whose Christian courage and unflinching
attacks on the sins of the day appear to have excited his anger or his
fears. In the absence of recorded facts we are left to imagine what may
have passed in the minds of some of the authorities of Geneva during
those few days between the closing of the meetings and the accusation
and expulsion of Miss Booth. Readers of this story will naturally
ask—“What had occurred in the interval?” They have an answer from the
Federal Council itself, which when citing these circumstances in the
month of July following, declares that Miss Booth had during that period
gone into retirement, and that she had herself advised other officers of
the Army to abstain from any act which might seem like a contravention
of the decree forbidding the meetings. Much, however, had evidently
occurred in the hearts and consciences of certain persons at Geneva. It
is no doubt convenient to throw off the guilt of such miserable
transactions as we are now obliged to criticize upon the ignorant and
misguided mob. But the act which is now under consideration was not that
of the people. It appears to have been conceived in the private chamber
of some unhappy being whose own life and character may have been
presented to himself in a new light by the severe teaching of the
Salvationists, and who, lacking the courage to face the truths announced
by them, took the ill-starred resolution to get rid of them by whatever
means.

M. Héridier having found it necessary to make diligent search for some
charge against Miss Booth, appears to have ransacked the Penal code and
the police regulations of the city for this purpose. He finds Article 27
of the police regulations concerning the discipline to be applied to
foreigners, which runs thus: “If subsequent to the granting of a
permission to reside in the canton, the Department of Justice and Police
learns that there has been anything which could have been laid to the
charge of the foreigner (to whom the permission was given) in the matter
of facts which, had they been previously known, would have caused him to
refuse that permission, the said permission may be withdrawn.” M.
Héridier recollects to have heard that collections of money had been
publicly made at the close of Salvationist meetings, and he lays his
hand on the cantonal law of March 4, 1879, concerning collections made
“from house to house.” This appears susceptible of being made to serve
the purpose in view. The title of the law is perhaps a little
embarrassing; it is this: “Règlements de la Police sur les collectes à
domicile.” Nevertheless he makes use of it.

M. Héridier then summons Miss Booth to appear at the police office. She
is charged with having broken a law which has no existence, and is
expelled from the canton. A double charge was now made against her,
which was brought up in the replies to her two appeals; not only had
she, according to M. Héridier, infringed the regulation concerning the
collection of money, but she had refused, they said, to show her
accounts when asked to do so. The demand to show her accounts was in
itself an illegal one, and therefore had she refused distinctly to
comply with it, she would not have been guilty of any illegal act. At
the time, Miss Booth, probably not knowing the text of the regulation
cited against her, supposed that her questioners were acting legally in
demanding to see the account books. She therefore, as we have seen,
offered to satisfy them in that respect as soon as she received the
books. In order to be doubly sure of these circumstances, I wrote to
Miss Booth early in July, and asked her the simple question, “Did you,
or did you not refuse to show your accounts?” The circumstances which
immediately prompted me thus to write to her was an interview which I
had on the 25th of July, with the Chancellor of the Confederation in the
Federal Palace at Berne. I had called to ask if he would oblige me by
letting me have a copy of the printed reply of the Federal Council to
Miss Booth’s appeal, as our Ambassador at Berne informed me that he had
sent his copy to the Foreign Office in London. In the course of my
conversation with the Chancellor I mentioned the matter of the accounts.
He then stated abruptly that Miss Booth had positively refused when
legally required to do so, to submit her accounts to the Genevese
Department of Justice and Police. Knowing to the contrary, I replied:
“Pardon me, sir, she was willing to show them, but time was not given
her to do so.” The Chancellor with some indignation then asked: “Have
you come here to dispute with me on a matter of fact?” That not being
the object of my visit, I retired, and having written to Miss Booth I
received the following from her:—“On the 8th and 9th of February I was
summoned to the Hotel de Ville to be cross-examined. Among many other
questions I was asked to produce on the spot the accounts of all money
received and spent in Geneva, or to show them at the house where I was
staying. I told them it was impossible for me to show them then and
there, as the friend who kept them was absent from Geneva and had the
books with her. I added that for myself I had nothing to do with the
keeping or the immediate expenditure of the money, neither could I
answer their questions as to the amount received by the collectors, as I
did not know what it was. My examiner said, ‘Very well, Mdlle., _you
refuse then to show your accounts_;’ and he was about to write that
down. ‘No,’ I replied, ‘_I do not refuse to show the accounts_, but they
are not in my hands; I ask time to recall my secretary, when you can see
them.’ It was with the utmost difficulty that I prevented him from
writing down that I had definitely refused to show the accounts, which
he appears to have done notwithstanding. It is our rule to publish our
accounts, audited by some one outside the Army, annually. But in this
case of Geneva I made an exception and told my examiner that they would
be published at the end of the first three months, and then all the
world could see them. On the 12th I was cross-examined again, M. Lenoir
being present. Being again asked for the accounts, I told them that the
day previously, the 11th, they had expelled Miss Charlesworth, and that
my secretary was still absent. Miss Charlesworth had the petty cash-book
and had taken it with her. M. Lenoir, banker, M. Pictet, lawyer, who are
friends of justice, bitterly lamented this action which their native
country had taken. They, together with M. Audeoud, also a lawyer, and M.
Naville, all assured me that such a thing had never been done before,
that the police authorities had no legal right at all to ask me such a
question, and that I was not in any way bound to answer them. That same
day, the 12th, I was expelled, and left Geneva before six o’clock that
night.

 (Signed) “CATHERINE BOOTH.”

In consequence of the publication in the Government organ, the
_Genevois_, of a number of anonymous letters, some of which were of a
shameful and slanderous character, and one of which contained the
statement that the Salvation Army were robbing the country and had
already forwarded enormous sums of money to London, the officers had
publicly announced that they would publish their balance-sheet of
receipts and expenditure at Geneva, at the end of the quarter instead of
at the end of the year, as was their usual custom. Every collection had
been carefully registered in their books.[9] It was further stated by
Miss Booth when she was questioned, that all the accounts of the Army
were under the inspection of a well-known firm of public accountants in
London, under whose signature they were published. The conduct of the
Geneva authorities in demanding to have the account books of the Army
submitted to their scrutiny is, besides being illegal as judged by their
own laws, a breach of the “Treaty of Friendship, Commerce and
Establishment of 1855” between Switzerland and England. Article 3 of
that Treaty runs thus:—

“The dwellings and warehouses of the citizens or subjects of either of
the two contracting parties, in the territories of the other, and all
premises appertaining thereto, destined for purposes of residence or
commence, shall be respected. No search or examination of such dwellings
and premises, and no examination or inspection of the books, papers, or
accounts of the respective citizens and subjects shall be arbitrarily
made; but such measures shall be executed only in conformity with the
legal sentence, warrant, or order in writing of some Tribunal or
Magistrate having competent constitutional or legal jurisdiction.”

I could multiply testimonies to the illegality of these proceedings, but
will only cite one more, that of a Swiss lawyer, given in reply to a
question which I asked him in writing:—“The Geneva Government,” he says,
“and subsequently the Federal Government, have cited against Miss Booth
a Cantonal regulation of the 4th March, 1879, upon public collections of
money. Now no such regulation exists in the Canton of Geneva; for the
regulation of the 4th March, 1879, is entitled, ‘A regulation concerning
house to house collections of money,’ and contains no article whatsoever
on the subject of collections made in either private or public meetings,
which are placed on a footing of complete liberty. There is no Genevese
regulation which bears the title of ‘Regulation concerning public
collections;’ consequently we are driven to the conclusion that this
title was invented for the necessity of the case in hand.”

In regard to the collections of money, it is to be noted that the
collections in Geneva _had been made for several weeks previously to the
29th January, at which date the Department of Justice and Police had
given Miss Booth an official permission to reside in the Canton, and
that those collections had been made in public, under the eyes of the
police, during all those weeks previous to the illegal order to stop the
meetings. No question whatever as to their legality was raised at the
moment when Miss Booth received in exchange for her passport the
permission to reside in the Canton._

On the 11th of February, Miss Charlesworth was expelled from the Canton.
The grounds of her expulsion and the circumstances connected with it
will be given later. At half-past twelve on the following day (the 12th)
Miss Booth received the warrant of expulsion, commanding her to leave
the Canton before 6 o’clock the same day upon pain of imprisonment. The
officer who delivered to her the warrant, turned back after having left
the door, to impress upon her the fact that if she did not leave before
6 o’clock she would incur the penalty of imprisonment. It is worthy of
remark that common felons are allowed twenty-four hours’ notice of
expulsion, whereas this lady was only given five and a-half hours’
notice.

I have now passed in review the three illegal or immoral acts of which
the authorities at Geneva were guilty since the entrance of the
Salvation Army into their city, namely:—1. The closing of the meetings.
2. The falsification of the police regulation concerning collections of
money. 3. The expulsion of the officers.

Those who took part in these events, Swiss and English alike, are
anxious that the public should, however, clearly understand that these
illegal acts were those of a mere handful of men openly at war with all
religion, and that such a course of conduct is as repugnant to the minds
of all the true Swiss citizens as they can be to our own countrymen.
Many indignant protests have been made against these measures both in
the Swiss Press and in numerous pamphlets published in Switzerland, of
which my space allows only meagre extracts.

I give the account of Miss Charlesworth’s arrest and expulsion in her
own words:—

“On Saturday, the 10th February, I was summoned to appear at the Police
Court. M. Lenoir, a gentleman of Geneva, accompanied me, and asked
permission to go with me before the officials who had instructions to
cross-examine me. I wished to have a friend present, being a minor, and
not knowing the language perfectly. We waited half-an-hour while Mr.
Cuenod[10] tried to induce M. Héridier to allow M. Lenoir to go with me.
M. Héridier persisted in refusing, and I was conducted into a little
office in the remote part of the building. Here I was kept from 3.30
till 7.30, being questioned sometimes by three, and sometimes by five
officials. After asking my age, birthplace, nationality, &c., they began
to speak on personal subjects, about which I know they had no right to
ask me. They asked me whether, being a minor, I had my father’s
permission to be in Geneva with Miss Booth. I answered that I had, and
that Miss Booth had letters in her possession to prove it. Nevertheless,
the chief charge subsequently adduced for my expulsion was that I
produced no authorization from my parents. I was asked how I lived,
whether we received much money from the Army for our board, what sort of
food we had, &c. I answered that I received nothing from the Army. I was
asked if I had a passport and a permission to reside. I answered that my
passport was deposited at the Town Hall in exchange for my permit of
residence. The inspector, who had been told I had no papers, was enraged
at this, and did not try to conceal or restrain his passion. He sent for
another official who found my passport and proved my papers to be all
right. I was asked many questions about the meeting I had attended at
Cologny. They said I had done so contrary to the decree. I answered that
I was invited by letter, and I went to the meeting as a guest, and on
being asked, I spoke and prayed. Others also spoke who had nothing to do
with the Army. The police detectives went to this private meeting; they
slipped in after it had begun, and not having been asked at the door
whether they were invited guests, they reported it to be a public
meeting. I protested that being only guests we could not be responsible
for the door-keeping, and that we knew none of those invited. They made
a number of charges against us with respect to this meeting, which I
contradicted. I was asked how I dared to wear my uniform in a meeting
after having been told of the law forbidding religious dresses. I
answered that the law only specified that religious costumes were not to
be worn in the public highway, and that it could not be applied to
private houses or a private gathering in the kitchen of a private house.
Moreover, I wore no uniform, but an ordinary dress.... I was at last
allowed to leave the office where I had spent four hours. We were told
to appear the next morning, but on leaving we recollected it would be
Sunday, and we wrote a letter saying we could not conscientiously go on
that day.[11] At half-past twelve on Sunday I received an official
warrant, which informed me that I must be out of the Canton in five
hours.... I was sent away alone; for they had taken care to hurry off
the French and German officers without giving them time to communicate
with their comrades. The warrant stated that I was expelled for (1)
having disobeyed the proclamation of the Cantonal Council suspending
temporarily the exercises of the Salvation Army; (2) for having produced
no authorisation from my parents giving me leave to reside in Geneva,
and (3) for having refused to present myself on the Sunday.”

Let us suppose this case reversed, and imagine a young Swiss lady of
seventeen suddenly called up before the police authorities in London, no
other lady, no friend, no witness, allowed to be present, taken to
Scotland Yard, and into a small room at the remote end of the building,
cross-questioned alone by five men in a foreign language (her only crime
being that she had accepted an invitation to a prayer meeting in a
private house), and expelled on a Sunday within five hours’ notice. It
is difficult to imagine such a possibility.

During the first cross-examination, Miss Charlesworth had said that Miss
Booth had in her possession letters from her father authorising her to
reside in Geneva, but she was not asked to produce these letters then or
at any subsequent time. On the Sunday, however, she was suddenly and
summarily expelled, one of the grounds of her expulsion being that she
had never shown these letters. She was sent off (as has been said)
alone, the authorities having taken care a few days previously to expel
the officers of the Salvation Army who had been her companions, and in
some sort her protectors. She was thus also forcibly separated from Miss
Booth, her constituted guardian, and from all her other companions, and
this in spite of her own earnest protests. No words of mine surely are
required to increase the feeling on the one hand of pity and on the
other of indignation which must arise in the minds of English readers on
perusing this narrative. One cannot fail, also, to be struck with the
cowardice (for it is impossible to give it a gentler name), manifested
by the authorities in availing themselves of the moment when Mr.
Clibborn, the business head of the Salvation Army work, was absent at
Berne, where he had gone to claim the protection of the Federal Council
on their behalf, in order to attack these gentle and unoffending girls.
When Miss Charlesworth was called up the second time, Miss Booth wrote a
letter of remonstrance, saying she did not think she ought to be
submitted to a second cross-examination. The officials, however,
overcame Miss Booth’s objection by insisting that M. Héridier himself
wished to see her personally. She did not see M. Héridier, but only a
number of his subordinates.

The following is the letter referred to by Miss Charlesworth when
questioned as to whether she had her father’s permission to reside
abroad. Miss Booth received it from the Rev. S. Charlesworth in reply to
one from herself asking if he would permit his daughter to stay in
Geneva during her projected visit to England:—

                                                      “January 31, 1883.

  “I will leave entirely to your judgment and the convenience of your
  arrangements whether my child remains in Geneva or at Paris during
  your absence. Let her be where she will be most helpful to you and
  most useful in the Lord’s service. If you intend to return to Geneva,
  her remaining there will save both the fatigue and the expense of a
  long double journey.

 (Signed) “SAMUEL CHARLESWORTH.”

The following letter, written by Mr. Charlesworth, seventeen days later
to the British Ambassador at Berne, further confirms the fact that Miss
Charlesworth was in Switzerland with her father’s consent. (She never
took the character in Switzerland of an officer in the Salvation Army;
but, in accordance with her father’s wish, accompanied and aided Miss
Booth as a friend.)

 “Clapham Common, London, 17th February, 1883.

  “SIR,—At the request of Miss Booth and of my daughter, Maud Elizabeth
  Charlesworth, I write to mention to your Excellency, that my daughter
  has accompanied Miss Booth to Switzerland with my full concurrence.
  She will be 18 years of age next September.

  “I am anxious to have it understood that my daughter is not officially
  connected with the Salvation Army, but is only with Miss Booth as a
  young friend and visitor, taking much interest in the work carried on
  amongst the poor in England and on the Continent.

  “I have the honour to be your Excellency’s very obedient servant,

 (Signed) “SAMUEL CHARLESWORTH.”

Even had her father’s authorisation been wanting, neither this nor the
fact of her age afforded just grounds for Miss Charlesworth’s expulsion,
as the Department of Police had given her the permit of residence with a
full knowledge of her age and without asking her to produce her father’s
authorisation. It is certain there are many young persons in Switzerland
of both sexes who live and travel in the country without ever being
asked to produce an authorisation from their parents.

The Geneva correspondent of the _Times_ (February 19, 1883) published a
letter from Miss Charlesworth on the subject of her appearance before
the police, in a paragraph headed “A Lamb among Wolves.” This letter was
written by Miss Charlesworth to a private friend in England, and was
never intended by her for publication. She was much surprised to see it
in the _Times_. She had handed it to some one who asked her in a
friendly manner for some details, saying, “You can gather something from
this.” No serious blame, however, in regard to this mistake, need be
imputed to the Geneva correspondent of the _Times_, who had generally
represented in their true light the injustices committed by the Genevan
authorities at that time. This letter, having once become public, may
naturally take its place in the present narrative. The _Times_
correspondent prefaces it with the remark:—“Miss Charlesworth’s artless
narrative is both interesting in itself and valuable for the vivid light
which it throws on the ways of Genevan justice—to foreigners. According
to the _Journal de Genève_, M. Héridier, Councillor of State charged
with the Department of Justice and Police, was present at Miss Booth’s
and Miss Charlesworth’s examinations, but behind a curtain.”

  “‘On Saturday afternoon,’ says Miss Charlesworth (who, it may be well
  to mention, is only seventeen years old), ‘a man came to the house of
  M. Lenoir, where we were staying, and said that Maud Charlesworth,
  aide-de-camp to Miss Booth, was to go at once and see the Chief of
  Police at the Hôtel de Ville. I went at once, taking M. Lenoir with
  me, as I did not like to go alone. When we arrived, M. Lenoir sent in
  to ask the Chief of the Police, M. Héridier, if he might be allowed to
  accompany me. We waited half-an-hour, and then the answer was that I
  must go alone. So there was nothing else for it, and I had to follow a
  savage-looking magistrate up stairs into a small and very hot office,
  where I was asked to sit down. I suppose they thought that this
  exceedingly cross-looking officer was not enough to question me, for
  two others, with equally unsaved-looking faces, came in to help him. I
  had been with Katie (Miss Booth) both the times when she had to appear
  before the police, so I was quite prepared for the sort of questions
  they were going to ask me. The last thing Katie said to me was, “Do
  not sign anything,” and I answered that I would sooner let my right
  hand be cut off; and when I got into that little room I made up my
  mind that when they came to the end and asked me to sign I would
  refuse unless they would allow M. Lenoir to come up and read the paper
  through.’

  “‘Well, they began and asked me about the private meeting at which I
  had been present. They said it was a public meeting, because three
  detectives had got in without being asked at the door for their cards
  of invitation. I denied the false statement, and made them write down
  my answer plainly. The point on which I laid the most stress was that
  we, the four Salvationists, had been invited to a private meeting in a
  private house, to which others (strangers to us) had also been
  invited; that we spoke, prayed, and sang, as did others who did not
  belong to the Salvation Army, and that if people who were not invited
  made their way in it was not our fault; we were only guests. Then they
  asked me how I dared to wear my uniform at the meeting when I had been
  told of the law forbidding the wearing of a religious dress. Now, I
  know this law by heart. It says that no one is to wear a religious
  dress on the public highway. My answer was that I did not think the
  words “public highway” could apply to the kitchen of a cottage in
  which a private meeting was held. I must here tell you that my
  questioners, or rather persecutors (of whom most of the time there
  were five) were very unsaved and all possessed very quick tempers.
  Their object was evidently to frighten me so as to make me answer
  unwisely, and catch me in my speech. But they were disappointed, for
  they had never had to do with a Salvationist before, and could not
  make out why I was so calm and answered so clearly. They were also
  disappointed to find that I understood their language, and no matter
  how fast they read I was always ready with an answer. Every now and
  then one or another went into a passion. But the worst was to come.

  “‘Have you got a passport or “leave to stay” in Geneva;’ asked one of
  the crossest of the examiners (with whom I was now quite alone); and I
  could see by his manner that he thought that I had not got my papers.
  I answered that I had my leave to stay; and that my passport was in
  the hands of the police. You should have seen the rage he got into. He
  rose, threw down his chair, stamped out of the room, shouted for some
  under officer, and asked the man what he meant by having said that I
  was not provided with a passport. This man also lost his temper, went
  off to look for the passport, and in a few minutes returned and saw I
  was quite right, that they had my passport and I my ‘leave to stay.’
  The inspector then flew into a greater rage than before, and scolded
  the man who had misled him. When he was more composed he continued.
  But now he came to personal questions, which I told him he had no
  right to ask, and I inquired what law authorised him to ask them. He
  said that was not his affair; he had been told to ask these questions,
  and I must answer. He asked me if I had my father’s leave to remain at
  Geneva, and when I said “Yes,” he wanted letters to prove it. I asked
  him how he dared to doubt my word, and told him to write down that
  Miss Booth had letters from my father authorising me to stay. A little
  later he said that I had prayed in a private meeting according to the
  form of the Salvation Army. I insisted that the Salvation Army had no
  form of prayer, and asked him in what way their prayers differed from
  other prayers. He said they differed very much, but he could not tell
  me how; he repeated that we had a form of prayer, and began to storm
  and rave so loudly that an inspector ran in from the next room,
  saying, “Gently, gently, there is somebody outside.” ... At last,
  after a great deal more questioning my paper was finished. I knew all
  my answers were true, and that there was no harm in putting my name to
  it; but then I remembered my promise to Katie, so I refused, unless I
  might go down and fetch M. Lenoir, and I said I would not sign the
  paper until he had read it. Of course they raved at me, but it had no
  effect; so they went for M. Lenoir, but unfortunately he had gone
  away, as they came back in triumph to tell me. I still refused to
  sign, and said I would not sign until Captain Bouillat (a member of
  the Salvation Army) had read it through. They were angry, and tried to
  frighten me, all talking as fast as they could at the same time. Then
  they said they would read the paper all through again, which they did
  three times; but nothing could move me. I said I would go with Zitzer
  and fetch Bouillat. They answered that I might go, but not with Zitzer
  (a Salvationist, who was waiting to be examined), but I said I could
  not think of such a thing—that it would be very improper for a young
  lady to walk through the streets after dark, especially as I knew
  there was a plot on foot to do us harm.’ In the end two gendarmes were
  sent for Bouillat; I and Zitzer waiting meanwhile in the little hot
  office.

  “‘All at once it struck me that we would have a prayer meeting.
  “Zitzer,” I said, “we will pray. Let us go down on our knees and pray
  for these people, for if ever we wanted the Lord with us, it is now.”
  So down we went, and prayed out loud for about ten minutes, and it did
  us good. The inspector was much surprised; he cleared his throat,
  grunted, and finally got up and went to the door of the outer office.
  Then four of the men came back. I said to Zitzer that we would tell
  the correspondent of the _Times_, and that I wondered what the English
  would think of the way in which their countrywomen were being treated.
  This was overheard, and seemed to make an impression, for two men came
  and said I was quite free to go if I liked, or if they could fetch me
  anything they would do so. I wanted to fetch Bouillat, but when I
  found that I should either have to go alone or walk between two
  policemen I preferred to wait. At length Bouillat came, and on his
  recommendation I signed the deposition.

  “‘The great fun was that all these cross magistrates and inspectors
  were kept from their dinners. So were we; but, as I told them, that
  was a very secondary consideration to us. We left that office at
  half-past seven, singing, “Glory to His Name.” I had been there four
  hours. The whole town knew it. A Swiss lawyer at once took all that
  had passed down in French, because he was so indignant.

  “‘On Sunday I received a paper which told me that before 6 o’clock I
  was to be out of the Canton, because, first, I had broken the law by
  speaking in a public meeting (lie No. 1); secondly, because I had
  nothing to show that my parents consented to my being with Miss Booth
  (lie No. 2); thirdly, because that morning I had not appeared when
  sent for by the police. (We sent a letter to say that we could not go
  on Sunday.)

  “‘Before the man who brought this letter went away I made him tell me
  who else was expelled, and I found that Bouillat, Zitzer, and Emile
  (all foreigners) had shared the same fate. We sent for them to come up
  that we might arrange where to go, but they did not come, and we found
  that they had been fetched out of their room, put into a cab with a
  policeman, and driven away without a moment’s notice. So these three
  are gone, I know not whither, and Miss Booth sent a Swiss lass with
  me, as, of course, I could not go alone.’”

Some of the Geneva journals stated that on the Sunday on which Miss
Charlesworth was expelled, several—probably a majority—of the Genevan
Government had gone to the country for the day, or were otherwise
absent. There is some ground for believing that there are persons in
that Government who from the first have disapproved of the harsh and
unlawful measures for which M. Héridier had made them responsible by
these expulsions. The _Journal de Genève_, after an ironical notice of
the rapid interrogations and sudden expulsion at the same moment of the
other officers of the Salvation Army—German and French—and of M.
Héridier’s secret attendance at all these examinations “behind a
curtain, after the manner of all grand inquisitors,” adds: “The public
interest was lately awakened in the matter of a Russian prince who was
expelled our country. To-day it is the case of two young foreign girls,
of honourable character, whose only crime is that they have held
religious services of a rather eccentric nature. They have complied with
every police rule, and so far from attempting to break the Cantonal
decree, they have requested to be more definitely instructed concerning
the actions they were to avoid. No answer is given to them, and they are
expelled from our HOSPITABLE Genevese soil at six hours’ notice! The
_Genevois_ (the Government organ) congratulates the Council of State on
its ‘energy.’ It has ‘saved the country; Vive Dieu!’ And what courage
did it not require to accomplish such a _coup_!! We find all this
contemptibly ridiculous, shameful, and opposed to all our habits and
traditions. For never have foreigners—young girls, almost children one
may say—been treated in such a manner among us—in the way in which
sharpers and swindlers and women of bad character are treated! By whom
are we governed? Into whose hands have we fallen? And what new
experiences of madness and folly shall we be called upon to take part
in?”

Another journal wrote:—

“When M. Héridier has a motive for attacking our modern liberties he
falls back upon a law or regulation of 1816. His political creed goes no
further back than that date. Like the ultra-Royalists of the Restoration
he ignores all that succeeded the regime of the Constitution of 1814. We
do not, however, believe that even in 1816 the Government of our
_Seigneurs_, the Syndics, would have dared to proclaim the principle
that the violence of the one party is a justification for the
suppression of the rights of the other. And we should like to ask
whether the majority of the Council of State who follow M. Héridier
perfectly understand that in suspending the meetings of the Salvation
Army and expelling its leaders they did not attack only a few foreigners
who were here without protection in the midst of us, but that they
attacked at the same time the right which every citizen has of using his
faculties and liberties as he pleases within the limits of the laws.
According to the system now inaugurated the employment of this right is
absolutely at the mercy of those who may be displeased by the use made
of it at any given time.”

The following letter, addressed to citizens of Geneva by a Swiss
gentleman residing in London, indicates some of the secret springs of
action whence the unjust expulsions and other events just recorded took
their rise:—

  “In opening the _Times_ of this morning, my attention was directed to
  the correspondence from Geneva, followed by a telegram, which opened
  my eyes in a painful manner. For the first time in my life I felt
  ashamed of being a Swiss. I said to myself, If my people are
  autonomous, if every man can make his opinion felt, how can such an
  infamy be perpetrated? Is it possible that two ladies have been
  banished from your midst? And why? Have they broken any law? I will
  speak boldly. It is not for this that you have driven them out
  ignominiously. I can believe many things, but not this. You have
  driven them out, or permitted them to be driven out, which comes to
  the same thing, because you felt impelled to get rid of them, and
  because you dared not burn them, as would have been done in the old
  times. You have driven them out because you felt that they have had
  more real power, more noble virility than all of you put together;
  because you could not drive them away by ridicule and mockery, that
  weapon which you have hitherto considered all-powerful, nor even by
  brutal violence. I picture to myself these young girls leaving
  all—father, mother, family, country—in order to go forth into the wide
  world. And for what? Was it in order to make a little money? I cannot
  understand how people calling themselves men dare to insult them by
  such an insinuation, which would be ridiculous if it were not so
  basely cruel. Was it their object to make a noise in the world? Was it
  notoriety, or the desire of praise, or to have some adventures to
  record when they returned home? No, you know perfectly well it was not
  so. Your conscience tells you it was not so. These girls’ souls were
  filled with one single thought, with one immense desire, which
  communicated to them an unquenchable zeal, a courage which nothing on
  earth can shake, a dignity which you may endeavour in vain to lower.
  What they desired with their whole souls was and is _the eternal
  salvation of their persecutors_. It was for you, citizens of Geneva,
  when you yourselves were wholly indifferent about it, that they
  sacrificed themselves from day to day, for you who have heaped upon
  them ridicule which they have borne without complaint, and calumny and
  insults of such a nature as it seems it has been the lot of the
  Genevese only to invent. And it was not in order to gain you for the
  Salvation Army that they entreated you to have pity on yourselves. You
  know well that they have fought with the energy of despair, that they
  are ready even at this moment to give their lives, because the one end
  of all their efforts and sufferings and prayers is to save you from
  eternal ruin, you who wish to root them out not only from Geneva, but
  out of the world of the living. You say you do not believe in eternal
  ruin. But if it were even proved that there is no such thing as a
  future punishment, would this be a reason for reversing the Christian
  rule which you cannot but admire, and for saying, ‘Hate your friends,
  curse them who bless you, do evil to them who love you, persecute and
  despitefully use those who pray for you?’ May there not be in this
  fury which possesses you a sign that you are not quite so sure about
  your own beliefs, and that this makes you dislike those who come to
  trouble your mental security, to awaken certain doubts, and to cause
  some still living chords in your deadened consciences to vibrate? You
  say you do not believe in a Saviour; but is this any reason why you
  should furiously persecute those who come to announce to you that
  perhaps there is still a Saviour ready to receive even you? Is it not
  true that you feel instinctively that the Saviour of whom you speak
  wishes to deliver you from the power of sin, and that in your secret
  hearts you do not wish to give up sin. You would be very glad to
  escape eternal ruin if there is such a lot in store for you, but none
  the less do you desire to escape from the dreadful necessity of giving
  up the enjoyment of vice, and consequently you are furious with those
  who come to say to you ‘make your choice.’ But perhaps you will reply
  ‘No, it is not that; what we reject is not the Gospel, it is the
  Salvation Army and its eccentricities.’ You speak falsely. If certain
  women had brought to you heaps of gold, of treasures, not invisible,
  but palpable and tangible, in good ringing metal, would you take
  offence at the colour or the shape of their dresses? Would you not be
  ready to listen to their harangues, their prayers, their singing, to
  excuse their English accent, and to bear with all imaginable
  eccentricities in order to have your share of that treasure? But here
  are handmaidens of the Lord who offer you neither silver nor gold,
  which they neither possess nor wish to give, but who hold out to you
  that which they have to give, the glad tidings of Salvation. This they
  offer you gratuitously, with joy, with love, with pity, and what have
  you done in return? You have endeavoured to crush them; you have
  expelled them ignominiously. Yet you have succeeded only in bringing
  shame upon yourselves. You have shown to the world that Switzerland,
  the cradle of modern freedom, may very soon become its grave.

  “But no! a thousand times no! It is not too late. _Post tenebras
  lux._[12] Citizens of Switzerland, if you have any drop of Swiss blood
  left in your veins, if you still have in you the hearts of men, wake
  up and act!”

On Miss Booth’s arrival very late on the 12th February at Lausanne, she
went with two other ladies to an hotel, which out of consideration for
the proprietors I shall not name. Having in a state of extreme
exhaustion mounted the stairs to the fourth story, the porter who was
carrying their luggage was called to from below by the proprietor
through the communicator, when Miss Booth and her companions heard the
following words: “Send those people down at once.” The porter asked why,
and remonstrated. The answer was, “They are Salvationists; you must send
them down at once, we will not have them here.” Therefore, Miss Booth
and her companions, almost fainting from suffering and exhaustion, were
expelled the hotel. They were, however, hospitably received at the Hotel
des Trois Suisses, whose proprietor has never allowed blind popular
prejudice to prevent him from showing kind hospitality all along to the
Salvationists.

In a letter to her friends on these events, Miss Booth said: “Everybody
considered that the decree forbidding the ‘_exercises_ of the Army’
referred to public meetings, and as every Swiss person’s house is
inviolable according to the law, our officers accepted private
invitations to take part in private prayer meetings called by Swiss
citizens. But here also persecution followed us. Detectives dogged our
every step. Spies watched our every movement. On Thursday evening I was
suddenly called up by the police. My comrade, Miss Charlesworth, was
sent for a day later and questioned for four hours by a number of
men—sometimes three and sometimes five taking part in the
cross-examination. A gentleman of Geneva who has proved a true friend
from the first, protested that it was neither seemly, humane, nor just,
that a young lady of seventeen, a subject of another nation, and not
possessing a perfect knowledge of the language, should be subjected to
such a cross-examination without the presence of a witness or anyone to
represent her father.

“Colonel Clibborn had gone to Berne to see the President of the Republic
and the British Ambassador, and to claim protection.

“At one o’clock on Sunday an official appeared with a document expelling
Miss Charlesworth. Captains Zitzer and Bouillot, and Lieutenant Emile
were suddenly taken up by the police and hurried off to the frontier....

“Next day I was expelled. On the way out of the town some fifty of our
brave soldiers who had learned the fact met us on the road. Never shall
I forget the scene! There were many tears and prayers. But I told them
it was but the old story—the devil defeating himself, and that all his
efforts and all the power of hell combined could not stop the work; that
God had begun to work; that this fire would only purify and strengthen;
that justice and right would triumph and we should return. Meanwhile I
charged them to carry out the war boldly as true Swiss soldiers. Then
all broke out into a ringing hymn, and sang until their faces shone
again.”

Miss Charlesworth, who had gone to Coppet, joined her friend again at
Lausanne.

 CHAPTER III.


Leaving the banished Salvationists for a moment, I go back in my
narrative in order to bring before the reader some slight picture,
imperfect as it must be, of the great spiritual work which was
accomplished without intermission in Geneva during the heat of the
persecution. This might be illustrated by hundreds of personal
testimonies and incidents, from among which I can only select a few.

In reply to a request which I made to Colonel Clibborn to give me some
information concerning the motives which led him and his companions to
go to Geneva, and concerning the beginning of the work there, he wrote
to me as follows:—

“You say you have all the newspapers, secular and religious, published
in the early part of the year, and you have therefore sufficient record
of the sad and dark side of the history—the triumph of the powers of
darkness. I shall, therefore, as you ask, try to give you some slight
details of the brighter side, showing the glorious operation of the
spirit of God and the triumphs of the Kingdom of Light. I feel sure that
the facts which I state will be found to be in harmony with the
information you have received from your numerous acquaintances in
Switzerland during your visit this summer. You ask how we entered the
country.

“A Swiss citizen of note said to me not long ago in conversation:—

“‘When the Army first came to Switzerland I thought it had made a
mistake, and that we did not need it; but as I have watched late events
my opinion has entirely changed. I now believe the Army was sent of God,
and at the chosen time. It _was_ needed not only in order to reach the
masses, but to show us the actual state of our country. We could not
have believed that such an awful state of godlessness and cruel
wickedness existed under the surface, and that infidelity and vice had
made such frightful progress. But above all has been the terrible
revelation of the real state of our so-called Christianity. Yes, our
eyes have been opened, and it is high time. Many of us are humbled in
the dust, acknowledging the hand of God in the awakening sent to us. But
also many refuse to do so and are hardening their hearts. There is no
doubt the Army has already wrought a great and needed revolution in the
minds of a great mass of Christians with reference to vital aggressive
religion.’

“Had this gentleman known all the circumstances which combined to direct
the march of the Army to Geneva he would have found further confirmation
of his opinion.

“Desiring to extend its work into the Provinces of France the Armée du
Salut had searched successively three Roman Catholic towns for a hall,
without succeeding in finding any that was suitable. Meanwhile warm and
pressing invitations had come from various parts of Switzerland. The
following is an extract from one of them which appeared in the French
organ of the Salvation Army in October of 1882:—‘I wish to tell you that
your paper _En Avant_ makes me see more and more clearly how much
Christians need to come out of their state of langour in order to become
true soldiers, and to fight as you do. Believe me that I indeed make it
a subject of prayer before the Lord. My friends join me in assuring you
of our fullest sympathy with your great and glorious work; we unite in
asking the best blessings of God upon this war which you have
undertaken. Our greatest wish is to see you soon cross our frontier. God
grant that we may ere long clasp hands!’”

On Monday, the 4th December, Colonel Clibborn came to Switzerland to
“view the land,” as the Army phrase it, intending to spend a week in
reconnoitring Geneva and two other towns, hold several explanatory
meetings, and, if possible, hire and commence the fitting up of a hall.
On the day after his arrival he received a message, saying that several
pastors wished him to meet them. They seemed uneasy at the arrival of
the Army, and asked that it should not come to Geneva, or at any rate
that it should postpone its arrival for some months, as they intended
after the approaching _fête_ to have several special meetings weekly,
such as had just been held by Pastor Theodore Monod with much blessing.
The present time seemed “inopportune.” He asked if there was not
abundance of work for all among the masses of their city, as in every
other city of Europe. Could they say, he asked, that enough was being
done for these masses, or that even one-third of them were reached by
the Gospel message. In advising the Army to stay away from Geneva, he
continued, were they prepared to take upon themselves the responsibility
before the Judgment Throne in the last great day, of all the souls which
the Army might be the means of saving, out of the 40,000 people in the
city who _never entered any place of worship_. He added that he did not
see what they had to fear from the arrival of the Army. In England it
had been said that it had helped the churches and congregations in which
there was spiritual life. _Life_ could not hinder, hamper, or destroy
_life_. They had opportunity and external advantages vastly greater than
the handful of officers would have who might enter the city. _They_ were
in great numbers, they knew the city, the language, the people; they
had, besides all their churches, a number of halls, among which were
four capable of holding respectively 300, 500, 600, and 3,000 people.
Surely there were men, means, and places enough to set the city in a
blaze (spiritually speaking) in a few weeks if they were all baptized
with the Holy Ghost; and so to outstrip anything the Army could do, or
to counteract anything in its influence which they might consider
harmful.[13] After this conversation the pastors parted from Colonel
Clibborn with expressions of kindness and goodwill.

Colonel Clibborn continues:—“I proceeded to give three lectures upon
‘The aims, methods, and organization of the Salvation Army;—incidents of
the work, women’s ministry, &c., &c. These meetings were instrumental to
the awakening and conversion of many souls. I was a second time asked to
meet a number of pastors and others. Many questions were put, and many
objections were made to the Army and its methods. All these I patiently
endeavoured to meet, but said that I did not at all expect to be able to
satisfy them, nor was it necessary that they should agree with all the
Army did. There were ‘differences of administration and diversities of
operation’ in the divine economy. I explained that the Army having
brought into prominence some great principles which had lain
comparatively in the dust for centuries, it was not to be wondered at
that strong opposition arose where these principles ran counter to
existing religious traditions and customs. We consider the prophesy of
Joel to be the key-note of the New Testament Dispensation, having been
quoted by the apostle Peter on its inaugural day. The great design of
the Army in its organisation and methods is to give the fullest scope to
the accomplishment of that prophesy, which expressed the Divine
intention that the Spirit should come upon ‘_all flesh_,’ and that
_all_, young as well as old, women as well as men, uneducated as well as
learned, should, under the energising, vitalising power of that spirit
take part in the war for the salvation of the world.[14] One great aim
of the Army is therefore that _all_ the gifts of _all_ its converts may
be called out at the earliest moment, and have the fullest scope for
exercise;—that, as in the good old days, the fisherman, the artizan, or
the seamstress, may become the evangelists of their fellow workmen or
women, without having to spend three or four years in college; that the
Salvation Army evangelist might, like David, employ any and every
instrument in praising his God, after his day’s work, and find joyful
recreation in leading his fellows to the fountain of Life; that like the
early Christians he might be ‘instant in season and out of season,’
exhort his fellow men ‘daily,’ go out into the streets and lanes and
compel them to come in, and in his service for his Lord have the fullest
opportunity to rise into the highest position of usefulness for which
his gifts might qualify him, and thus become a blessing to thousands.

“The prevalent religious customs on the contrary restrict the joy of
preaching the Gospel to the _few_—to _educated_ persons of a certain age
and of one sex—thus excluding more than half the human race.

“The Army was not the result of any preconceived plan, or of careful
invention, but simply the gradual development of these and other great
principles in active operation. The chief instrument which God had used
in its formation,—Mr. William Booth,—had worked as an evangelist among
the masses in all parts of England from seventeen years of age. Wholly
consecrated to God, his efforts had been blessed to an extraordinary
degree. Years of devoted labour among the working classes had gained him
a profound acquaintance with their ideas, habits, likings, and
antipathies, and had shown him that the prevailing modes of
evangelisation were but imperfectly adapted to the end in view. They
left a great section of the population comparatively untouched, and the
good that was done was, he conceived, in great measure lost through the
want of a system in which it could be perpetuated and multiplied by
engaging in the work those who had been already won. God had granted to
him a willingness to unlearn and to abandon any preconceived idea or
theory, no matter how dear, which did not seem to conform to the great
original Pentecostal idea, or to the stern facts realised in practical
labour among the people.”

The following quotation from the writings of one well acquainted with
the Army’s works from the first, may throw light on its origin:—

  “Feeling a special call to Evangelistic work, Mr. William Booth
  resigned in 1865 his position and salary as a minister, and casting
  himself upon God, he began his work in the east end of London.
  Determined to follow the Spirit of God in all its leadings and at all
  costs, that he might be guided into the solution of the great problem
  of how to reach the masses with the Gospel, he commenced alone, Bible
  in hand, in an open space beside a crowded thoroughfare. The men and
  women of that poor quarter who grouped around him each day in the open
  air were invited to follow him to a little hall—thus began the
  processions. Transformed in character, under the powerful operation of
  the Spirit, these men, testifying publicly, in their rough dress,
  straight from their work, became the Evangelists of their
  fellow-workmen. The masses hated both the name and sombre garb of
  “religion,” but _Salvation from sin_ was a practical thing which
  workmen or the loafers could understand and appreciate when manifested
  daily in the _changed lives and homes_, and happy testimony of their
  former mates or _pals_.

  “The path thus entered upon was of necessity often a solitary and
  rugged one, beset with stupendous difficulties. Each new departure or
  novel method to attract the godless masses generally brought with it
  the estrangement of some of the friends who gave the work their
  pecuniary aid. Misrepresentation, misunderstanding, imputation of
  selfish or vainglorious motive succeeded. Nights of earnest prayer and
  painful thought and moments of extreme financial difficulty marked the
  track. But the cry of dying millions ever impelled him forward, and
  for seventeen years God had never failed to keep his promise, though
  faith was often tried to the utmost.

  “Experience had convinced him of the true principle of evangelisation,
  and that in face of the great degraded masses a central direction in
  each meeting and in each department of work was an indispensable
  necessity to united and successful effort. As the artisan preacher
  dared not assume the name of clergyman or clergywoman, “captain”
  sprang into use as the easiest name by which to designate the leader
  of a meeting—a word very constantly used among the people to designate
  a leader—whether of a ship’s crew, a troop of soldiers, a fire
  brigade, or a pit gang. ‘General Superintendent of the Christian
  Mission’ was gradually abbreviated into ‘General.’ ‘Headquarters’
  became substituted for ‘Central Office.’ The command of the Master,
  ‘Go ye out into the streets and lanes and compel them to come in,’ had
  in its practical working created the processions. The necessity of a
  head to the procession, visible from a distance in a crowded
  thoroughfare, had developed the flags. The principle of the employment
  of all the gifts of all the converts had developed the bands of all
  kinds of instruments. To the common people the drum did not seem less
  suitable or reverent as a call to prayer than the bell in the steeple.

  “And finally, the Christian Mission, which had been described in one
  of its reports as a volunteer _army_ of men and women banded together
  in efforts for the salvation of their fellow-creatures, adopted the
  name of the Salvation Army and rapidly assumed in all its various
  departments that form which has been in all ages found to be the best
  for the prosecution of a war. The simple uniforms were adopted not
  only as a guarantee of plainness of dress and as a safeguard against
  changes of fashion, but as a means of continually bringing the idea of
  _salvation_ before the eyes of the public, and of giving it the
  opportunity of narrowly watching the movements of Salvationists.”

“Sunday the 10th of December,” wrote Colonel Clibborn, “was a great day
of _fête_ in commemoration of a victory over the Catholic Savoyards some
two hundred years ago. It was the great annual carnival of the city in
which the greater part of the population turned out into the streets in
the masquerade, and the merrymaking was kept up for a week.

“The walls announced in great placards that according to the usual
custom Pastor —— would give the popular lecture in the Great Reformation
Hall, upon ‘the Escalade,’ on Sunday afternoon. On enquiry I ascertained
that the hall would be unused all the rest of the day and all the
following week on account of the _fête_. I was also told that it was
largely used for fashionable concerts. This splendid hall, which seats
2,700 people, was built for evangelistic meetings, one-third of the
money having been subscribed in England.

“The thought of that grand building remaining silent and empty during
the Sunday evening and during all those days of the _fête_ while the
giddy multitude thronged the streets as in Vanity Fair, filled my heart
with the intense desire to do something for the Lord at such a moment,
when the emissaries of Satan would be let loose. It was said that it
would be dangerous to hold a meeting that Sunday night, as the bands of
masqueraders would probably try to enter and upset it, and that so
entirely was that part of the city under the reign of the carnival that
night that respectable people would not like to attend. The Committee,
however, granted me the hall for the Sunday and Tuesday.

“Seeing that there would be many attractions of a worldly kind in the
streets that evening, I endeavoured to make my placard particularly
attractive by adding the statistics of the Army! And in order to be sure
that all should understand that it was a _spiritual_ war which was in
question, I concluded it with the text, ‘Thy kingdom come, Thy will be
done on earth as it is in heaven.’

“In the afternoon the hall was crammed to its utmost standing capacity.
As the aged Pastor told the oft-told story of how the Catholic Savoyards
had attempted to enter the Protestant city in the night; how the alarm
had been given and how they had been caught and killed; how old Madame
——, of unfading memory, had dropped the iron pot of boiling soup upon
the head of the leader,—loud and prolonged applause rang through the
building. Then a number of young men in white gloves stepped forward on
the platform and sung a song composed for the occasion, and which
praised God for the deliverance of their city out of the hand of the
enemy upon that day so many years ago.

“I seized the opportunity, while they were getting into choral order, of
handing a note to the Pastor, requesting him to announce my meeting in
the evening; he tore it to pieces before the whole audience with a
disdainful gesture. The great sacrifice made on Calvary was not a worthy
subject to follow ‘the Escalade’ on that Sunday evening in the
Reformation Hall! About eight hundred however, came in the evening.”

The earlier meetings held in the Reformation Hall were crowded
generally, and always accompanied with that fulness of blessing which
had been asked in prayer, and was expected by those who asked. The
following is the testimony of a Pastor, concerning one which he
attended:—

  “I should like to give you my impressions of the Conference at the
  Reformation Hall, last Wednesday, on the subject of the work of the
  Holy Spirit. Those who were present soon became aware that the speaker
  was not about to give them a theological dissertation; but they felt
  that they were in the presence of a solemn reality, so much so as to
  recall the words: ‘Put thy shoes from off thy feet, for the place
  whereon thou standest is holy ground.’ I shall make no analysis of the
  discourse to which we listened. It had indeed nothing in it of an
  exceptional nature, and, reported verbally by another not so fully
  penetrated with the spirit of the subject, would have no remarkable
  effect. But vivified throughout by the vital and profound convictions
  produced by the Spirit of God itself, the word spoken possessed a
  force which went direct to many consciences; one may say, it ran
  throughout the meeting like a volley of artillery. We well know that
  quite ordinary words spoken under such circumstances may strike to the
  soul like a sharp arrow. To this end, they must proceed from a heart
  entirely consecrated and filled with the Holy Spirit. How shall I tell
  you of my own experience as I left this meeting? I felt as one broken,
  burdened, annihilated. Never did I experience so deep and intense a
  feeling of my own complete misery and of the need that I had of the
  help of the Holy Spirit. I am convinced, if we were filled with that
  Spirit, He would work miracles in us and all around us. Why not? The
  promise is distinct, and the command positive—‘If ye being evil know
  how to give good gifts unto your children, how much more shall your
  heavenly Father give the Holy Spirit to them that ask Him?’ And the
  command is, ‘Be filled with the spirit.’ We are constantly praying for
  the Holy Spirit, but we do not receive it; there is something amiss in
  our prayers. We ask, perhaps, as certain professional beggars ask, who
  go from door to door begging bread, but who, well fed at home, do not
  for a moment care whether their petition is rejected or not. It is
  very different with those who ask as shipwrecked people crying for
  help, as they who are dying of hunger and thirst, beseeching a morsel
  of bread or a cup of water, or as miners buried under the earth by
  some terrible accident, sighing for air and light. We must ask with
  conviction, with faith, with constancy. It was only after nine
  consecutive days of unceasing prayer that the Apostles received this
  gift. So long as our prayers are unbelieving or languid they will
  remain sterile, and ourselves useless, unprofitable servants, however
  great our outward activity.

  “To return to our brothers of the Salvation Army. May God keep and
  bless them! Let us pray much for them; for it is exactly those who are
  walking upon heights who are the most in danger of falling.”

A record of the struggle in Geneva would hardly be complete without
allusion to the subject of the famous placard which gave offence to
many, and which has been frequently discussed as among the doubtful
means employed by Salvationists to attract to their meetings those
classes of the people who are strangers to all religion and indifferent
to any appeal addressed to them in the ordinary manner. This placard
announced that at a meeting about to be held the “_devise_” of the
Salvation Army, “_Sang et Feu_” would be explained; and that the
_Maréchale_ would open the attack. The promise here made was fulfilled,
simply by an earnest address, first, on the words, “the blood of Christ
Jesus His Son cleanseth us from all sin;” and secondly on those others,
“He shall baptise you with the Holy Ghost and with fire,” bringing into
view the full Salvation offered to all—absolution from the guilt of sin,
and inward purification. I was present at a conference of friends in
Switzerland, where the expediency of such a method of attracting the
attention of masses was warmly discussed, and will endeavour to
reproduce the impression which that discussion left on me. On the one
hand, it was argued that such an announcement was calculated to
“scandalize” the serious, the reverent, the refined and sensitive
portion of the public, and thus must have done harm; on the other hand,
the facts which followed the putting out of the placard were simply
stated. These facts said more, it seemed to me, than any arguments.
“True,” said Miss Booth, “this announcement seemed to you startling and
perhaps bordering on the profane; but, fellow-Christians, our mission is
not directed to _you_, but to a class of people whose views, tastes,
ideas and habits are wholly different from yours. Now, what did that
placard accomplish? It did for us exactly what we hoped. It brought up
from the depths, so to speak, from the cellars and slums and hells of
your city, a vast crowd of beings whom no ordinary call would have
reached, whom no ordinary evangelizing efforts have yet won. They were
struck by the words; seized with wonder and curiosity; even amusement
was awakened; and they came with a great rush to see this novelty—to
hear what these strange words might mean. They came with every motive
except the right ones; but they came; and, thanks to our God, many of
these demoniacs are now sitting clothed and in their right minds at the
feet of Jesus. If there had been any other way, not offensive to the
more refined classes, by which this mass of human beings could have been
brought together, we would have adopted them: but other means had
failed.”

I give the two views of the matter. Probably those who condemned the
placard will continue to do so. Yet, after all, there is that mass of
people. We cannot cart away _human_ rubbish as we do refuse from a
quarry or a coalpit. It will not lie passive where we place it, nor
remain a harmless detritus beneath and behind our streets, in the
cellars and dark quarters of whose existence we are scarcely aware. That
rubbish is palpitating with life—with human, terrible, infinite,
unquenchable life. There are hearts down there, hidden in those
cheerless rooms, squalid offices or unwholesome kennels—hearts that
beat, that love and hate and hunger and break; there are brains that
work and invent and contrive to the imminent peril of society;—souls
without hope, and lips that pour forth blasphemy.

You cannot reach these, Christian people, by the same appeals which are
understood by civilized men and women; and will you not bear a little
what wounds your sense of propriety, or even of what seems to you
irreverence, in order to achieve the task of furnishing yourselves with
the opportunity of reaching these masses? “Let all things be done
decently and in order,” is a wise and useful counsel which, like many
others, is applied sometimes at the expense of other truths. Decency and
order and reverence are things unknown, in connection with the worship
of God, or in any connection, to many of the persons of whom we are
speaking. They may please and attract you; they are a necessity to you;
but to reach those lower regions, what is wanted is rather a harsh
trumpet call, an electric flash, the sound “as of a rushing mighty
wind.” And as to Christians themselves! Can we say that they also do not
need at times these disturbing influences to shake them out of the
security or inactivity into which they may have lapsed in the midst of
all their privileges, of all that is decent, orderly, reverent, and
beautiful in ritual and religious observance?

The circumstances which preceded and followed the incident of the
publication of the objectionable announcement may be briefly recounted.
Miss Booth had addressed a meeting attended by a large number of the
better-class people of Geneva, members of churches, and others,
generally seriously disposed. An impression was made, and many tears
shed during her address. But on leaving the meeting, troubled thoughts
arose in her mind. “I am not satisfied,” she said, “this is all very
well; but these are not the persons we came to Geneva to seek. We _must_
reach that other world of human beings by whatever means.” And thus the
idea arose of an announcement which would bring forward those beings.
The expedient was successful. A meeting was held in the Casino Hall on
the 22nd of December. _That night the powers of light and darkness met
with a crash that shook the city._ On this occasion Miss Booth, standing
at an open window looking upon the street where the crowds had gathered,
called her companions to look down upon a motley gathering of human
beings, the like of which she had not seen even in Paris or London, “a
howling, biting, cursing mob,” impossible to describe. Some imagined the
announcement was a challenge to a physical conflict; others that Geneva
was to be attacked, that blood was to be shed, and the city set on fire!
An old woman, it is said, expressed to one of her friends the hope that
the taxes would not be raised by the invasion of this army which was
spoken of.

The hall was crowded to its utmost capacity a quarter of an hour before
the time, and the broad stone staircase was soon filled with a mass of
people crushing for entrance. A band of students in coloured caps had
come early and taken possession of the front of the galleries and other
prominent positions in the hall. It was soon evident that an organised
disturbance had been planned by them. The first hymn was interrupted by
cries and ribald songs, which continued, almost drowning the prayer
which followed.

But through and above the storm many a burning word or earnest
invitation was launched forth in the All-powerful name, which found its
way straight to hearts and consciences. The little band on the platform,
confident in God, remained perfectly calm, delivering in the intervals
of comparative stillness, the message of salvation with the power of the
Spirit of God. The tempest began to be assuaged; by nine o’clock victory
was evidently on the side of the Salvationists. Many who at first had
been the most wild and noisy in the disturbances were listening
attentively. Words of life, cutting through the uproar, had reached
their hearts. Certain citizens of Geneva who were present have said that
they never fully realised before that night the reality of the conflict
between the powers of light and darkness. The leaders of the meeting
appeared as persons girded with divine armour, wielding the sword of the
spirit in such a way that the fiercest shafts of ridicule opposed to
them by the bands of mocking students and others fell harmless to the
ground. Now, at last, the invitation is given out to those who desire
salvation, to come forward. A strong faith and a holy boldness indeed
were needed for this letting down of the net into such troubled and
raging waters. One after another bold scoffers of half an hour ago left
their places, some trembling visibly under the power of that divine
influence which had subdued them, and under the sense of personal guilt;
and as they kneeled down (for the first time perhaps in their lives)
there rose sweet and low, the beautiful hymn:—

 Reviens, reviens, pauvre pécheur!
 Ton Père encore t’attend.
 Veux-tu languir loin du bonheur
 Et pécher plus longtemps?

 _Chœur._ O! reviens à ton Sauveur
 Reviens ce soir,
 Il veut te recevoir,
 Reviens à ton Sauveur.

The power of music in aiding a holy cause is beginning to be better
understood. Many hearts have been and are daily moved, stirred to godly
sorrow, or to hope, by the message of peace and love conveyed in song. I
have been struck by the effect produced in such meetings by the words of
a hymn begun so softly that one could scarcely mark the moment at which
the silence was broken by the first word, and gradually rising in
clearness and power until it swelled into a full chorus. St. Augustine
records the fact that the first sense of relief to his burdened soul,
bowed down under the crushing sense of sin, came to him through the
sweet singing of a little child in a garden,—“Take up the book, take up
and read.” He obeyed the angelic summons, took up the book, and read
those words which opened for him the gates of the Kingdom of Heaven.

To return. Now a divine influence seemed to steal over the meeting,
hushing the crowd into a profound silence, while the pleading words of
the chorus were repeated again and again. As the last verse was sung,

 Reviens prodigue à la maison,
 Reviens, ne sois pas sourd,
 Après la mort plus de pardon,
 Le jour de grâce est court—

the faces of many young and careless men betrayed signs of deep
distress. A part of the audience now began to disperse. A tall man, a
German workman, approaches the platform, and, with tears flowing fast,
cries in broken French, “O pray for me, pray for me. I see my sins
to-night; I am lost; I know not what to do; I was too cowardly to come
