forward; I feared to be laughed at. Will you not pray for me?” A few
nights afterwards this man came out and gave himself to God. His
testimony in the subsequent meetings was sublime; he has stood forward
on the platform of the Reformation Hall, and told how he had been
plunged in every abyss of degradation—how he formerly drank to a
frightful excess—played cards till the small hours of the morning with
debased comrades, and how his life had become a burden to him through
sin. Now all was changed. The light of a glorious Salvation shone upon
his path, and God had given him a joy above all others in working for
the salvation of his comrades, though abuse and ridicule were often all
he received in return.

The next night a similar meeting was held in the Casino. The hall again
filled to its utmost capacity, while crowds stood in the street; other
souls again seeking salvation. The following is from a French pastor,
whose son, a student in Geneva, was one of those who were at this
meeting.

“I cannot resist the pleasure of telling you how thankful I am towards
God and the Salvation Army for all the good my dear son ... a student of
theology, has received in your meetings at Geneva. The letters which he
sends us make us weep for joy.

“On the 4th of January he wrote:—

“‘The Salvation Army will continue to do great good in Geneva. I passed
a long night of watch with them on the 31st of December, which I shall
never forget. Since then I am always happy, and can say, “Glory to God!”
every hour throughout the day. There is much disturbance caused for the
purpose of preventing the Army in its work, but every day it gains new
converts. One of the leaders of a band of roughs does not fear, for the
last week, to say that he is saved; also many others. I help these dear
friends as much as possible, although at times it is very difficult....

“‘We have several meetings during the day. I can not, unfortunately,
attend all of them, as my studies prevent me, but when I can, it is
always with a new pleasure. I love you with all my heart; also my dear
mother and brothers. I beseech these last to give themselves promptly to
the Saviour, and not to do it as I, their eldest brother, did, for I
remained a long while before I could dare to pronounce the name of
Jesus.”

“Help us to bless the name of the Lord for such a result! To show my
gratitude towards the Salvation Army, which does so much good among us,
by the grace of God, I ask you to enter my name, through Mr. Herbert
Booth, as a member of the Auxiliary of the Salvation Army. For this
purpose I send you an order for twenty-six francs fifty cents., which I
believe is equivalent to one guinea. This will be my subscription for
1883. I authorise you to make what use you like of this letter for the
glory of God. Give my best wishes to Miss Booth. _En Avant_ goes well
here.—Believe me to be, dear Sir and brother, your devoted friend in
Christ Jesus, the Great Captain of our Salvation,

                                                       “E. V., Pasteur.”

It was to this meeting that Charles Wyssa came, whose death occurred in
September of this year, and at whose grave the arrest of Miss Booth and
her companions took place. He wrote: “I was passing through the streets
of Geneva, and my eye fell on the placard of the Salvation Army. That
looks lively, I thought, and I went in.”

  “That night Charles left the hall with the arrows of conviction
  sticking fast in his heart. Three days afterwards (Christmas Day), in
  the Reformation Hall, he rose and came forward with forty others and
  gave himself to the Lord. He immediately became the target for the
  fire of the whole _atelier_ in which he worked. But so noble and so
  powerful was the testimony of his life, and so cheerfully and
  patiently did he bear all their mockery, that they were forced to
  acknowledge the reality of his salvation.

  “One day, when violently attacked by his fellow-workmen, the foreman,
  though himself an infidel, approached and called out to them to cease
  their mockery, adding, ‘It would be better for you all if you were
  like him. _You_ are _a shame_ to our firm!’ (alluding to their
  dissipated lives).

  “Many of his fellow-workmen came to hear him give his testimony in the
  great Reformation Hall. One night while a volley of mockery was poured
  forth, Charles stepped forward upon the platform, pale, but firm as a
  rock. ‘Ah!’ he said, ‘I now understand all this uproar and opposition.
  Why, if you pinch even a dog’s tail he will growl or bite. _Somebody_
  has got pinched since the Army came to Geneva, and of course he is
  angry. That “somebody” has been cast out of me, you know who he is—_le
  demon_!’ and then he went on to speak of the blessed Jesus who had
  come to live in his heart instead. Throughout those dark days, before
  and after the closing of the meetings and the expulsion of the
  Maréchale and officers, Charles was among those who stood the firmest
  and laboured the most actively as sergeant.

  “He suffered through the consequences of the persecution. The cold
  which brought on his consumption dates from a Sunday morning in March
  when the soldiers of the Geneva corps gathered in the chill shadow of
  Salève cliff on the French frontier, before the mist had lifted from
  the valley, in order that they might worship God, as they were
  forbidden to do upon their native soil. His great desire during his
  long illness seemed to be to lose no opportunity of speaking of
  salvation to those who came to see him. I have sat by many a
  death-bed, seen many a triumphant death, but never have seen a face
  more _radiant_ at the thought of the nearness of Heaven.”

It was Christmas Eve. The Salvationists had no hall. The officers went
to the Committee of the great Reformation Hall, and pressed upon them
the responsibility of allowing it to remain idle, while the attention of
thousands was now aroused to the question of salvation. It was granted
for two nights. A dense throng poured into the building each evening,
filling every space. The scene of the Casino was repeated on a larger
scale. The audience was for the greater part composed of people who
never entered a place of worship. This evening was a marvellous
revelation to some Christians present. For the first time, many of them
afterwards said, they realized the awful state of the great mass of the
population; they confronted the terrible fact that tens of thousands of
persons, with eyes closed to the light, and strangers to the joy of
salvation, were rushing down headlong to destruction. The cabarets, the
dancing saloons, the drinking hells, the rendezvous of prostitution, and
the theatres had poured their contents into the Reformation Hall.
Socialists—men of every nationality—who had found refuge in Geneva came
_en masse_. It must not be supposed that these audiences consisted
mainly of the miserably poor; these, the poorest of the people, are
probably less cynical and less formidable in their attitude towards
spiritual truth than are the classes to whom the Gospel appeal was now
mainly addressed in Geneva as also in Paris. There is a portion of the
population of Geneva which is perhaps even more disintegrated and
demoralized than that of Paris, owing to the admixture of international
elements, possessing no common traditions of law or religion or national
history, to which an appeal can be made. They are the refuse of the
earth, so to speak, loosely thrown together, without guiding principles,
absolutely without public spirit, unconscious of the existence of any
_common weal_ to be sought, lacking any kind of cohesion, and eager only
in the pursuit of some isolated, individual, material interest. The
editor of _le Témoignage_, who was present at one of the services at the
Rue Oberkampff, in Paris, wrote:—

  “There is one point of view from which one cannot but render an
  unanimous homage to the Salvation Army, that is, its ardent and
  devouring compassion for the wandering multitudes. That this Army may
  be very wrong, may itself paralyse its means of action in holding
  itself too much outside of the Church, is evident; but what is no less
  evident is that it reaches and takes hold of the masses upon whom I
  say that not only the Church, but the generous efforts of private
  persons, had little or no hold up to this time. In this respect its
  success seems to me striking. Every time I have attended other
  meetings of a religious character, it has never been without
  experiencing a piercing feeling of deception and regret. Always the
  same audience.... But the public which it was the object to gain, I
  said to myself—the public notoriously hostile—the public of our
  atheist press—the public of the great political meetings, in whose
  eyes Victor Hugo himself would pass for a clerical!—that public! Were
  is it? How is it to be acted upon? Very well; this public I have at
  last seen, I have seen it with my own eyes, at the meetings of the
  Salvation Army. And I have been rejoiced and moved, beyond all
  expression, to see it. In all my life I shall not forget the scenes at
  the opening of a new hall in Rue Oberkampff. My heart was divided
  between the very opposite sentiments of sorrow and joy in hearing
  these blasphemies and songs and cries, because at last! at last! the
  assault has been delivered, and the enemy _struck in the face_! And I
  had a very lively impression that my sentiments were partaken of by
  the members of the Army, to whose cold blood, energy, and, I will say,
  clever strategy, one would not know how to render sufficient homage in
  this emergency. They did not cease to repeat with a tone of
  conviction, ‘Your tumult will be appeased; one day, you, who blaspheme
  the most at this moment, will perhaps be the first to surrender. This
  is the position on which we wish to plant our standard, and _we will
  plant it here_.’”

These words apply equally to the case of Geneva.

To return to the meeting in the Hall of the Reformation, a large part of
the audience (of the nature described above) were so entirely strangers
to the idea of worship or of an Invisible Being to be addressed in
worship, that the sound of prayer called forth loud, derisive laughter,
with questions and cries of surprise and scorn. This would have been
enough to daunt any evangelists except those who, strong in faith in the
miracle-working power of the Holy Ghost, fear nothing, neither the loud
blasphemy of the multitude, nor the powers of hell let loose in their
midst. Every lull in the uproar was taken advantage of by the speakers
to fling some javelin from the armoury of God’s Word into the midst of
the opposing and surging host. The uproar itself even seemed at times to
render more pointed the words spoken in a clear unfaltering voice which
cut their way through it. Many an arrow went home, as subsequent
histories—not only told in words, but acted out in changed and holy
lives—have abundantly testified.

A more select meeting for prayer followed. In order to hold this in a
calmer atmosphere, the members of the Army retreated to the smaller hall
behind the platform. A crowd followed, and in a few moments the place
was filled to suffocation. The impression of what followed can never be
forgotten by those who witnessed it. While the roar of voices gradually
died away in the larger hall, a feeling of deep solemnity fell upon
those assembled for the prayer meeting. They had seen the collision
between the powers of light and darkness; and the stillness which
followed resembled the silence which succeeds a storm, in which clouds
full charged with electric fire had met in the midst of the hissing and
smoke and roar of the warring elements. To the faith of some it might
have seemed that they saw again in anticipation the vision granted to
Our Saviour, when he said, “I saw Satan fall like lightning from
heaven.”

Some of the ringleaders of the disturbance had pushed their way into the
smaller hall; but there they remained perfectly still, subdued and
overawed, with an expression on their faces of intense interest, which
showed that they felt that they were in presence of a reality in
religion which they had not before encountered. The officers did not
fail to take advantage of the scene which had just been witnessed to
urge their hearers to flee from the wrath to come, and be as decided for
Christ as the rioters were against Him. The presence of God was realised
in an extraordinary degree. The hymn was sung—

 “Tel que je suis, pécheur rebelle,
 Au nom du Sang versé pour moi,
 Au nom de ta voix qui m’appelle,
 Jésus, je viens à toi.”

The resolution expressed in these words found an echo in many hearts;
and then there followed a scene such as, it was said, had never been
witnessed in Geneva. More than forty persons (careless or mocking, a few
minutes before), arose, and one after another came forward, and kneeling
down, surrendered themselves to God, before the whole meeting. The
officers of the Army spoke to one and another of “the Lamb of God that
taketh away the sin of the world;” and again arose the chorus of the
hymn—

 “Ôte tous mes pêchés
 Agneau de Dieu, je viens a Toi;
 Ôte tous mes pêchés.”

The following is the experience, given by himself, of one who had gone
with the herd of cynics and sceptics to the meeting at the Casino, and
now came to the Reformation Hall:—“I was like the demoniac of Gadara. I
can say I was possessed; I was chained for fifteen years to a frightful
life. I was lost, my health ruined, my nerves shattered. I gave myself
up to despair. Everyone shunned me like a leper; but I refused to go to
the Great Physician of my soul, for in spite of all I clung to my sin,
and sacrificed everything to it. I contemplated suicide. It was then
that you arrived. I was at first astonished; then remorse seized me.
Then followed a frightful torment in my soul,—a real hell. I resolved to
put an end to it one way or another. Yet I thought I would go and hear
you once more.

“I had been in darkness and anguish since the day of the first
meeting,—on the point of committing suicide. No word had I been able to
recall of that day’s teaching,—nothing remained in my memory, except the
words of the sacred song, ‘Ôte tous mes pêchés’—(‘Take all my sins
away.’) These sounded in my heart and brain through the day and the
sleepless night—these, and these only. Bowed down with grief and
despair, again I came to the Reformation Hall, and to the after meeting.
The first sounds which fell on my ear, were again those very words,
‘Take all my sins away,’ and then you (to Miss Booth) spoke on the
words, ‘though your sins be as scarlet, they shall be as white as snow;’
you seemed to speak to me alone, to regard me alone,—and I felt it was
God who had sent me there to hear those words.”

When, Christian, in the “Pilgrim’s Progress,” came to a certain place
where there was a cross and a tomb, his heavy burden rolled away and he
was free. Such a place of deliverance did this burdened soul now find on
that Christmas Day, the anniversary of the Saviour’s birth, the birthday
of that Swiss Salvation Army. “Oh! if you knew,” he said, “the struggle
I had to go through. I spent a night in prayer to God which I shall
never forget. At last I came off with victory. Ah! yes, my God, Thou art
good, Thou didst uphold me. Now all is well. I shall have to pass
through much trial, but I am so happy now to be saved; I have thrown
behind me and forsaken all my sins.”

New Year’s Eve was marked by a fresh baptism of spiritual life. The day
is still spoken of as an era in the spiritual history of many, and
allusions to it occur in the letters of many of the converts, who then
consecrated themselves afresh to God’s service and prayed to be made
willing and able to suffer all His will. The clouds of persecution were
already gathering black and gloomy around them; and there, at the foot
of the Cross, they felt and confessed that nothing but the spirit and
power of early Christianity could fit them for the conflict. Hour after
hour their fervent prayers arose as they remained prostrated before the
Lord. This young band of new-born souls entered the New Year upon their
knees.

Evidence now began to come from all sides of blessing received in many
homes, of wild sons reclaimed, of drunkards and vicious men transformed
by the power of God, of light and joy brought into families over which a
cloud had hung. In several cases husbands and wives who had separated
were re-united, old enmities were forgotten, and enemies reconciled. A
workman who had come under this transforming influence came to confess
to his employer that he had stolen some materials from his _atelier_ for
his own use, and to make restitution.

Meetings for consecration were held in the early mornings, before the
dawn of day. Often as the gas was turned out at dawn, giving place to
the gray light of the morning which fell upon this crowd of kneeling
people, or upon those who had found the full freedom they had sought,
and were now rejoicing in the Saviour, it seemed an image of the
spiritual change,—the dull light of _self_ disappearing, and the dawn of
the true light arising upon their souls.

One of these morning meetings was thus described by an aged Genevese
gentleman of ripe experience:—

  “I was present at seven a.m. on Sunday morning. Oh, it was a
  Pentecost! hundreds kneeling—children, men, and women. I cannot
  express what I experienced. I never passed such moments. I was melted
  down before the Lord. I could only cry, ‘This is Thy Spirit, O Lord!
  this is Thy Spirit!’... The tongues of flame alone were wanting to
  show us by our bodily vision the presence of the Holy Spirit. But they
  were not absent; our hearts saw them. The language of my heart was,
  ‘Lord, now lettest Thou Thy servant depart in peace; for mine eyes
  have seen Thy Salvation.’”

Another Genevese thus wrote of the early morning meetings:—

  “Such a Sunday morning as we have witnessed to-day will by many never
  be forgotten. It has been said that there has never been seen anything
  like it since the time of Calvin. Oh, with what divine influence were
  we surrounded. While singing the hymn—

 ‘Accept my sacrifice,
 ’Tis on Thy altar laid,’

  we believe many truly laid their all on the altar. The attention of
  that vast audience was concentrated on Jesus. We drew nigh unto Him,
  and He drew nigh unto us. The intense earnestness of those prayers was
  such that many unconsciously spoke at the same time, acknowledging
  their unfaithfulness with tears of repentance. Jesus Himself was
  there, pardoning, and breathing peace into their troubled hearts. The
  meeting closed with a song of triumph in which heart and voice
  united....

  “It is with joy we learn from all sides that the Spirit of God is
  working among the Churches; special meetings are now being convened
  for the purpose of drawing in the unconverted; and we receive striking
  testimonies of God’s power. One cannot be astonished that the devil is
  doing all he can to injure us. But, forward, comrades! Walk in the
  light—have no fellowship with the works of darkness. Live to please
  God—to realise each instant the sweetness of His approval. Know
  nothing among men but Christ Jesus, and Him crucified; then victory is
  yours.”

Personal testimonies must have a force far beyond that of mere general
statement, for all who believe that God has made the true progress of
mankind to rest upon the quickening of the individual soul. I give a few
such, selected with some difficulty (owing to the _embarras de
richesse_) from many hundreds.

A Correspondent of foreign journals who had come to the meetings with
the sole intention of turning them into ridicule, and who till then had
professed to believe that all religion was a hypocrisy and a mockery,
wrote:—

“Thanks be unto God, even I am saved! For a long time I had wished to
find the light, but could not find it. I imagined I could correct evil
by evil, but never reached my object. I saw the evils of the world, and
looked out for truth and justice, but found no impersonation of them. I
thought that the Christian religion was a kind of madness, or a
collection of mysteries. I should like to have had all its
representatives burnt—churches, priests, books—all! When I first
attended the meetings of the Salvation Army, I was struck by the
sincerity of the character of its officers. I perceived that they
possessed something which I did not possess. But I said to myself at
first, ‘it is only the phlegmatic English temperament.’ But, by-and-bye,
my whole being was turned upside down. I could no longer think, read,
nor write. In fact, I had been shot through! I had no more peace. I
could do nothing, and thus I decided to give myself up entirely and
without reserve to the Saviour.”

The Federal Council, in its rejection of the appeal of Miss Booth at a
later date, cites the opinion of a medical man, who asserted that
persons in a weak or melancholy state of mind had been driven into
greater darkness or madness by the influence of the Salvation Army. From
a large collection of testimonies which tend in the very opposite
direction, I may give the following:—

“To the glory of our God I am able to say that I have passed from
darkness to light! My life—hitherto without hope, without aim, and
utterly useless—is transformed. I lived in rebellion against God. He
sent me many trials, but I constantly hardened myself against His will;
I tried to be sufficient for myself. Falling at last under a burden
which was too heavy for me, I wrapped myself in my solitary pride, and
lived alone. In spite of many hard lessons and the reiterated calls of
my Saviour, to which I refused to respond, I hardened my heart. What I
have suffered it is impossible for me to express. I locked myself up in
silence and reserve, sometimes endeavouring to put on a smiling face,
sometimes seeking a moment of oblivion in recreations of one kind or
another. At times I was tempted to fling myself into the intoxications
of the world in order to deaden my misery, but I hated the world as much
as I fled from God. It was too noisy for my broken heart. But now
darkness is turned into light.”[15]

A man of fifty years of age rose in one of the meetings, and said:—“I
have been an unbeliever, estranged from God, living honourably according
to the world, but caring nothing for spiritual things. I came to these
meetings, and to the knowledge and love of Jesus Christ. It was at a
meeting at seven in the morning that I first felt my sinfulness and saw
my life as a failure (_vie manquée_), and Jesus has pardoned me. O! what
joy have I missed throughout my whole life!”

Another thus speaks:—“The first time I came to hear you, I left the
meeting with very different feelings to those with which I entered; your
beaming countenances, in which one saw expressed a happiness beyond
anything earth can give, made so great an impression on me. Then I said
to myself, here is the religion which I need; it is not sad or gloomy.”
Again another:—“In the face of all the calumnies spread abroad against
the Salvation Army, I feel it my duty to declare publicly that, so far
as I have seen for myself, all that is said against them is absolutely
false. I have followed almost all their meetings at Geneva, and had
opportunities of observing the officers closely. I found the Salvation
Army a true family, whose members are bound together by the strongest
ties of Christian friendship. To those who call them hypocrites I would
say, ‘come and see for yourselves. Question them, live with them as I
have done, and if you can then discover that their lives or conduct are
not just and upright, you can blame or accuse them at your ease. But
take care not to judge them until you know them.’ Each time that I came
in contact with the Salvation Army, I experienced a happiness and joy of
which I had had no knowledge before; and while I lived among them I
learned how complete is their disinterestedness, and how great their
courage in supporting the grossest insults for their Master’s sake.”

Another independent witness says.—“One did not need to be a profound
psychologist in order to foresee that the Salvation Army would draw its
most hearty converts and its best supporters from the ranks of the very
persons who at the first met it with the most violent, gross, and
deep-seated opposition. These persons could not fail, sooner or later,
to be struck with that ‘demonstration of the Spirit and of power’ which
accompanies the appeals of these sincere and joyous evangelists.
Wherever the Army has passed, it is from the benches of the mockers that
they have the most largely recruited the benches of the penitents.” It
is impossible not to remark in connection with this indisputable
statement, on the suicidal folly of the men at present in positions of
power in Switzerland, in forbidding access for the people to this
powerful influence. Is not the power of the Holy Spirit the one and only
influence which is able to transform that mass of miserable humanity,
these “dangerous classes,” into good citizens and subjects; the only
influence which can avert the moral and political bankruptcy apparently
impending for their country? The sorrowful words have often risen to our
lips, in connection with that fair city, “Oh, Geneva, if thou hadst
known, even in this thy day the things that belong to thy peace! but now
they are hid from thine eyes.”

The following is a testimony of another kind, but not less powerful. It
is an extract from _La Scène_, a theatrical gazette of Geneva, of
February 6th, 1883, before the expulsions:—

  “The Department of Justice and Police has just issued a warrant
  forbidding the exercises of the Salvation Army. We congratulate the
  Department and the Federal Council without reserve upon this measure.
  But what would have been still more simple would have been expulsion
  in regular form. People do not spare the parasites who live and fatten
  on the public credulity; why spare those who open a shop to sell
  righteousness just as formerly indulgences were sold; persons from
  whom are not even demanded the necessary passports and papers, and who
  did not even pay for their permit of residence, nor for the police
  license to exercise their trade of public cheats? Popular good-sense
  has meted out the proper measure of justice to these cynical
  play-actors who parody religion, and we believe that it was high time
  that the Department of Justice and Police should appear on the scene,
  otherwise the people themselves would have scourged these sinners from
  the temple.”

The same paper, on Monday, 19th February (after the expulsions), has the
following, under the heading “Theatrical Chronicle”:—

  “Since the departure of the Salvation Army our theatre has lost a
  formidable rival, and the crowd are beginning to find their way back
  to us. The most terrible blow received by these speculators was the
  pamphlet of Madame the Countess de Gasparin. This courageous writer,
  in language elegant, modest, and full of conviction, has well defended
  religious Geneva, so outrageously insulted. She has fought step by
  step, entrenchment by entrenchment, these sorry soldiers, male and
  female, who make use of the name of God in order to live by their
  speculations. She has exposed, in a careful verbal translation whose
  exactitude a few cowards have dared to doubt, the cleverly designed
  plan of campaign of the bank of Booth & Co. In short, she has opened
  the eyes of those who only saw in the Salvation Army a religious sect
  which our laws should protect, and which should be allowed to carry on
  its work. All honour to Madame de Gasparin, and hearty thanks!”

To return to the experiences of the Army previous to the expulsions. At
one of the evening meetings a Salvation soldier came to ask Colonel
Clibborn to speak to a young man at the further end of the hall. On
going down he found a man of about thirty years of age leaning against
the wall, with tears in his eyes, and his whole appearance indicating
deep emotion. He said, “Oh, I am so wretched; I cannot sleep, I cannot
rest, I don’t know what to do. I see I shall have to give up my present
employment; and yet to do that is to sacrifice all I have.” He said he
was a Greek, and that his employment was that of a correspondent of
newspapers in Greece and Constantinople, for which he was expected to
write flippant and scoffing articles. When the Army first came to Geneva
he had written articles turning it to ridicule, in a style which would
suit the taste of those papers. But while attending the meetings in
order to get material for his articles, he became thoroughly convinced
of the sincerity and disinterestedness of the Army workers, which
surprised him, as he had always looked upon all religion as a hypocrisy
and a swindle. A Greek newspaper editor having asked him to send
extracts from “La Verité à l’egard de l’Armée du Salut,” he began the
translation of that work. He had not gone far before he became
completely convinced of the truth of all he read. “The book,” said he,
“when rendered into Greek was like an exquisite poem! Ah! I used to
believe in dynamite as the remedy for social wrongs, but now I see that
there is another power which can turn the world upside down and set all
wrong things right.” Colonel Clibborn pressed him to submit to that
power at once, to obey his conscience, and give his heart to God. A
terrible struggle was going on in his soul. He said that after
conviction had come upon him he had written an article defending the
Army, and had at once received an angry letter from the editor. His
companions, seeing the change, also wrote to Constantinople saying that
he had sold himself to the English for 5,000 francs. His conscience did
not allow him either to continue his employment, or to ask for the money
owing to him, as he felt he had earned it wrongly; and yet he could find
no other work. At times the temptation was strong to stifle his
convictions. The old scoffing spirit and unbelief would struggle for the
mastery; his great spiritual enemy suggesting to him that he was a weak
fool, and that all his convictions about God and salvation were a
delusion. Colonel Clibborn pressed him to put the matter to the test
there and then by a whole-hearted surrender; but he said the sacrifice
was too great; he could not take such a leap in the dark, or trust God
when he saw starvation before him, having already failed in every effort
to get other employment. But God did not let him go. As he continued to
read the Bible and attend the meetings his conviction strengthened and
deepened. One evening he had fallen into a state of almost complete
despair. He had been obliged to sell his watch and other things in order
to buy food, and he seemed weak, as if he had scarcely eaten anything
for several days. “Oh!” he said, “I am lost, lost! I have left the
service of the Devil, and yet God won’t have me. I have fallen between
the two.” Again he was pressed to trust. He said he could not trust God
in the way described to him. He could not trust blindly. If he only had
employment then he could believe. They showed him that it was just this
entire abandonment of himself to God that was necessary. Colonel
Clibborn knelt down with him, and after a time while they were praying,
the poor Greek broke forth, saying, “There! I can’t hold out any longer.
I give myself to God. He can do with me what He likes. It’s done. He may
have me!”

“It was dinner time when he was about to leave,” wrote Colonel Clibborn.
“Oh, how hard I felt it not to be able to ask him either to have some
dinner with me or to give him something with which to buy himself food.
I dared not even give him a few francs from my own money, so gross had
been the falsehoods in circulation about our giving money to people to
bribe them to speak in our meetings. I knew also that his own
independent spirit would prompt him rather to starve than to allow even
the shadow of a pretext for such stories by even taking a mouthful of
food. Alas! how cruel is the world. A gentleman had openly stated a few
days before that we paid four francs each to our converts, and this had
gone all over the town and into the papers with the usual additions, and
had excited the rage of the people against us. He subsequently wrote the
same to Neuchâtel, with a like result, and yet _never had a centime been
given to one of our converts from the very first_. We even decided once
not to employ one of our converts who was out of work for the moment,
when we had some work which he might have done, lest the enemy should
have a pretext for such stories. The next time I met the Greek, he was,
as I expected, enjoying the full assurance of salvation; all his burden
gone. Though an educated man, he had (like a true Salvation soldier)
accepted an offer of employment as a labourer in the fields in order to
earn his living.” This man had formerly been under police surveillance
as a dangerous socialist, but had now placed himself _en regle_ as a
peaceful and law-abiding citizen. All the conflicts and despair of soul
had gone, and the peace of God was in his heart.

He afterwards rose in one of the meetings, and stated that, “when they
spoke to me of Jesus Christ, I replied, ‘Don’t talk to me of Him; He was
a vagabond and a wild fanatic.’ When I was young my mother gave me a
Bible, and I threw it into the river in a rage.” Some time after the
expulsions, when the Geneva soldiers crossed the frontier to meet their
leaders in Savoy or Vaud, the Greek would also come, when it was evident
from his speech and countenance that he was “continuing steadfast in the
faith.” During the persecutions he had much to suffer. On one occasion
he was savagely attacked, knocked down, and cruelly beaten.

The following is extracted from a letter published by a Genevan lawyer,
in reply to a hostile criticism:—

  “When the Salvation Army arrived amongst us, preceded as it was by the
  reputation which the newspapers had already made for it, there were
  but few Christians who were not troubled or hurt. But these
  impressions were singularly modified among the best part of those who,
  having seen the work, followed it without taking any part, anxious to
  form their judgment upon what they heard and saw, and not upon what
  they had merely read.

  “Several have even gone so far as to consider that they were
  witnessing a work which brought them back, in a greater degree than
  any one would have imagined possible in this century, to what must
  have taken place in the primitive Church at the time and during the
  preaching of the apostles.

  “It is an incontestable fact that under the action of the Salvation
  Army a powerful movement the like of which has never been before
  witnessed among us, has been produced in Geneva. An evident action of
  the Holy Spirit has manifested itself. The question which is called
  ‘exciting _par excellence_,’ that of perdition or salvation, has been
  placed before a multitude of men and women of all classes; it is a
  question which up to the present they have succeeded in avoiding; and
  it has not been put forward, as your correspondent is pleased to say,
  ‘under a form more or less grotesque,’ but under a form most serious
  and incisive. Hence this display of unheard of anger on the part of a
  population which has felt itself wounded in its pride, disturbed in
  its false security, troubled in its conscience, and obliged to face a
  question which it found most irksome.

  “As to the opposition of Christians, it is kept up undoubtedly by many
  who do not render the full sense of the word conversion as meant in
  the Bible, and therefore do not attach sufficient importance to it;
  whilst others will only hear of one sort of conversion, namely, that
  of people well placed, of good education, grave, instructed, and with
  whom the question is well thought out. But there is not, thank God,
  only that one kind of conversion for people who receive an independent
  income and who are well educated. That which is adapted for some may
  not suit others.”

The Swiss adherents of the Army in Geneva continued to increase daily
after the expulsions and during the heat of the persecutions. Many
interesting extracts might be made from a mass of correspondence which
has been placed at my disposal, between these hunted but happy people,
the reading of which transports one in imagination to the times of the
persecutions of the Huguenots in France or the Covenanters in Scotland.
The “kitchen meetings” were very much frequented. A soldier writes of
one:—“We ended our meeting by thanking God for His presence, and asking
Him to be with us the remainder of the day. We then left our Hallelujah
kitchen to meet again at three in the afternoon; for, although we have
no halls, we manage to meet in kitchens and parlours, kindly lent us by
friends of the Army. Some of us met in a parlour; the others with
Lieutenant Thonger in the open-air. They all had a great blessing; and
although we were not all together, we felt we were all with the same
God. We closed our meeting at half-past four to meet again at eight in a
Hallelujah shop, where we thought there would be room enough for all who
knew we had a meeting; but when we arrived there at five minutes to
eight we found everything full, kitchen, stairs, shop, and all, and lots
unable to get in; so Lieutenant Thonger walked off with a detachment to
another Hallelujah kitchen. We had a splendid day. Six meetings
altogether. Our soldiers here are a brave set; some of them know what it
is to go through the fire; some are very much persecuted, but in spite
of it all they are faithful soldiers of the Cross.”

One result of the persecution became now sadly visible at Geneva, and
later at Neuchâtel, in the breaking down of the health of officers and
soldiers and friends in consequence of the choking atmosphere of the
meetings, which they were obliged to hold only in small, out-of-the-way,
and sometimes unwholesome rooms. At Geneva no private rooms could be
found large enough to hold the little army, and though the town was
divided into four districts, each to have its meeting, the same state of
things repeated itself everywhere, and the windows having to be kept
shut, in order to avoid attracting the attention of the police or
populace, the result soon became painfully visible.

Colonel Clibborn was obliged to remain a month absent in the north of
Switzerland through illness (his lungs having been most seriously
attacked from this cause). “It made my heart bleed,” he says, “on
returning to Geneva to find two of our devoted Swiss friends, who had
opened their houses to the meetings, completely broken down, and, though
strong, robust men of middle age, bent and feeble through the violence
of the malady in their lungs.”

Shortly after the closing of the meetings, the following letter, signed
by seventy-two mothers of families, was received by Miss Booth:—

“We, the undersigned mothers, desire to give our testimony and to prove
by many facts to all the persons with whom we have any influence—

“1. That the Salvation Army since its arrival in Geneva has been, under
the powerful hand of God, the blessed instrument of the salvation of our
children.

“2. That we have no fears in regard to the influence of the Salvation
Army upon our children; seeing that its officers draw their teaching,
exhortations, and encouragements from the Bible alone, whence also they
draw the force which animates them, and according to which they rule
their lives and conduct.

“3. That, as mothers and as women who love their families and their
fatherland, we can only bless the Lord for the good which he has brought
to us and our children by means of the Salvation Army. We ardently
desire that those of our families who have not yet embraced salvation
may do so; and we pray that they may become the faithful disciples of
Jesus Christ and valiant soldiers of the Salvation Army.”

 (Here follow seventy-two signatures.)

A similar letter was received by Miss Booth a few days later, signed by
upwards of thirty more mothers of families.

Throughout the period of the persecutions the chief weapons of attack
were furnished by the religious press of Switzerland. The first personal
attack upon Miss Booth—and the only one of this kind which appeared,
reproduced in the secular press—was signed “A Woman.” This anonymous
writer was the first to apply the epithet of _saltimbanques_
(mountebanks) to the Salvationists; a name at once caught up by the
comic and worldly papers, and afterwards constantly applied to them.[16]
It was a religious paper of Geneva which gravely suggested that an
explanation of the power exercised by the Salvation Army might be found
in mesmerism. This idea also was caught up by the secular press.
Sceptics, and Christians who have forgotten that we are living under the
dispensation of the Holy Spirit, may well be perplexed to account for
the spiritual power which is working marvels in our day, not among one
class, or one Salvation Army alone, but among all Christians whose eyes
are enlightened to read the signs of the times and the will of God. The
unbelievers of Judea said of Christ, “He casteth out devils by the
prince of devils,” and of the inspired band on the day of Pentecost they
said, “These men are full of new wine.” Translated into the language of
modern cynics or roughs, we have here a repetition of the same
accusations in the attribution to the instruments of God’s loving will
of a force called animal magnetism. “They mesmerise the people,” said
the polite critics. “They are drunk,” said the roughs. Alike strangers
to the spiritual world, it was impossible for them to understand this
mysterious power under which the strongest men, hardened in vice, or
confirmed in unbelief, were broken down under the hand of God—the God
who “woundeth and maketh whole,” who “killeth and maketh alive.” This
charge of magnetism or mesmerism prevailed to such an extent for a time,
that the Salutists found their every movement, every glance of their
eyes, suspiciously watched in the meetings, while even Christians shrunk
from their approach with a vague dread. Others asked, however:—“Can
magnetism save the drunkard from the dominion of his ruling passion,
break the chains of habit for the profligate and set him free to live a
life of purity, convince the materialist of the existence of God,
restore concord and love to families long blighted by selfishness and
sin, bring the lost prodigal back to his father’s house, reconcile
deadly enemies, and, in a word, work miracles of transformation in
hearts, lives, and homes?” “Mesmerism!” said a mother of a family. “If
mesmerism can work such a change as has been worked in my house, and
turn a dissolute, cruel-hearted son into a sober, affectionate and happy
man, then I wish all Geneva were mesmerised.” Not the smallest of the
trials of those dark days was the consciousness of distrust and
superstitious dread with which this charge of mesmerism for a time
surrounded the Salvationists.

Among the letters which Miss Booth received after her expulsion, was one
signed, “A mother whose family has been restored to her by the Salvation
Army.” A poor woman, the mother of an invalid son, wrote that since he
had become a convert, his peevish spirit had changed to one of patience
and joyfulness; “formerly he always grumbled about his food; nothing
pleased him; but now he is always satisfied.” A young man of weak mind,
whose mental infirmity had taken the form of dislike, or rather positive
hatred, to his brother and the rest of his family, became so changed
that he could now never do enough, he deemed, for his family, and for
that brother in particular. His limited powers and intelligence forbade
his doing anything great, but he would show his love by brushing his
brother’s clothes, blacking his boots, and watching every opportunity of
serving him. How lovely are the “peaceable fruits of righteousness” in
the weakest and most despised, as well as the highly gifted!

On an occasion when it was impossible to have the Reformation Hall for
mixed audiences, Miss Booth obtained it for a meeting of women. It was
filled with women only; _three thousand_ being present. A great number
of letters pouring in subsequently, proved the vital work which was
accomplished in many hearts on that day.

The following is from an old citizen of Geneva:—

  “On Sunday last the Salvation Army held a meeting at 5 o’clock in the
  morning at the Casino. The meeting was led by Miss Wallis, who made a
  simple but very impressive address upon several Biblical passages.
  There is in the early hours of Sunday morning a peculiar solemnity
  which inclines us more than at other times to heart-searching. I can
  never forget the moment when all were invited to consecrate themselves
  entirely to the Saviour. I saw advance and kneel down, one by one, no
  inconsiderable portion of the audience until there was no longer any
  more room for them. The little platform and pulpit were surrounded by
  a crowd of people in earnest prayer. One could not move, and those who
  were still seated had to rise to make way for the numbers who
  continued to press forward to kneel in their turn, and to testify
  publicly their determination to serve God faithfully. Among them were
  many men, and all ages were represented; but there was no trace of
  ephemeral excitement. I stood by the side of the platform, and handed
  one chair after another, as I contemplated with deep emotion this
  assembly on its knees. Prayers followed each other so spontaneously
  and rapidly that they sometimes became almost simultaneous. One of the
  last of those who spoke was a man well known, addicted to drunkenness,
  who after having signed the pledge, had been again on the point of
  falling. A hindrance of another kind had also held him enchained. He
  felt that this Sunday was the day of sacrifice, and testified publicly
  of his resolution to give up all in order to win all, and in that hour
  his heart was filled with joy.

  “Oh, joyful day! Every one seemed to have forgotten that the
  instrument which God had made use of was the Salvation Army. The human
  no longer pre-occupied their minds—each soul was _face to face with
  God alone_. Church of Geneva, awake! Forget the instrument of which
  God in these days avails Himself to call thee to a new and perhaps
  glorious destiny! Let us recognise that we have allowed ourselves to
  be swallowed up by considerations of forms, of ministries, of
  orthodoxy, and by jealousies of congregations, by doubt, by
  lukewarmness, by coldness. Do not let us lose our time in learned
  criticisms. Let us break through these old barriers—let us confess
  that we have SOMETHING TO LEARN!

 (Signed) “AN OLD GENEVAN GENTLEMAN.”

M. Favre, who has a number of mission halls in Geneva similar to those
of Mr. M‘Call in Paris, publicly stated at his annual meeting that his
work, far from having been injured by the army movement had been greatly
helped, and though he had had a little more disturbance in consequence
of a larger attendance of the more turbulent class, the blessing had
increased in proportion. This fact will interest those who have read the
charges against the Army of fettering the work of others.

The following was received by Miss Booth after her expulsion:—“We ask
ourselves still, even to-day, can it be true that all these events which
have just taken place are indeed a reality? Is it really possible that
they have driven from among us the friends who have done us so much
good? Something tells me they shall come back again. Oh, what a blessed
thing it is to know that nothing happens without the permission of God,
and that He brings good out of evil! For this, as for all else, we cast
ourselves into His arms, and all will be well.”

Also the following:—

“Dear Maréchale,—I must tell you, with the expression of my sincere
gratitude, how we thank you. Thanks to you and to all, for the good
which has come to our families since we attended the meetings of the
Salvation Army. Our house was indeed before a Christian house, but
everything has been changed since we understood the work of
sanctification. That teaching needs to be restored to the place where
God put it in His word. I am now astonished not to have earlier
understood these truths. I now understand why we did not go forward; or
rather how it was that we went backward, dragging ‘this body of
death.’...

“I take up my letter again, where I stopped eight days ago. How many sad
things have happened since. How much we have suffered; how much must
your heart be torn in seeing so much evil, so much iniquity, so much
sin. And what must make your heart bleed most must be the false
accusations of those so-called Christians who use their high social
position in favour of the work of God’s enemies.

“I think, dear Maréchale, that you suffer less than we do, because you
have come to give up all. Indeed, it must be so, else how could anyone
support such a terrible weight? But we know that God is strongest, and
that He can open the doors which Satan is trying to shut.”

I give the following, from a number of letters I have been allowed to
read, showing the loving and simple relation in which her girl officers
and workers stand to Miss Booth. Some of these epistles are full of
brightness and wit, as well as fond affection.

“My darling Maréchale, M. Audeoud has been fined 50 francs for letting
us have meetings in his own grounds. Shameful! But he will not pay it;
so what will be done I don’t know....

“I went to ——, and we had a blessed meeting amongst all the girls there.
I like ——; but she is not properly _saved_, but still I believe she soon
will be. She goes to the meetings with her mother. I like her mother,
too, but she is one of those who talk a good deal too much, a thing I
hate with all my heart. Her little girls are good—they come to our
soldiers’ meetings. Oh, we are working for the salvation of the young
and the old, that we may raise up a blessed army to serve God with all
their might.

“Precious Maréchale, we all pray for you, and believe hard for your
return to Geneva; it must be soon; the Lord will answer, and that very
soon. I often think your crown will be very bright when you go home to
the Lord, for you have gone through so much fire—fire that purifies and
makes bright; and those very people that caused you to pass through such
fire will envy you then. I pray that I may have a little place at your
feet there, and as near the dear Lord as I can get. I cannot help
praising Him for all the trouble, for I know that blessed are they who
suffer for His name’s sake. Oh, _that He should think us worthy to
suffer for Him_; to be despised as we are for the name of Christ! Jesus
is at our head. The dear Lord will help you. It will be victory! all
along the line!

“Accept a heart full of love, and believe me ever yours, to live
fighting by your side, fighting for the salvation of the world, and to
go from your side to be with Him in heaven, where we shall never part.”

A Swiss citizen wrote at this time:—

“In face of all the wicked things that are being said and printed with
reference to the work of the Salvation Army in our dear land of
Switzerland, I feel the obligation incumbent upon me as a Christian of
Switzerland to express to you for my own part my whole-hearted thanks
and most sincere affection for the great spiritual good that you are
doing here in Geneva, not only in bringing a great number of sinners to
repentance, but in reviving and refreshing a great number of Christians
who were altogether asleep with reference to their state. I am one of
the latter number, but I feel,—by the contact with your burning love for
souls, your heroic devotion to the cause of Christ, your holy enthusiasm
for the conquest of the world to the Kingdom of our Divine Redeemer—I
feel again burning in my own soul the flames of my first love for my
Master, and the desire to consecrate myself anew to the service of my
Saviour.”

A Pastor wrote:—

“The spirit of God has been working in a marvellous way all round my
parish, unknown to you. Many husbands who have been separated from their
wives have been re-united to them by the influence of your meetings and
our visits, all inspired by the holy fire lit in my heart by the Army.
On all sides I am now called to minister to broken hearts.”

The following from a mother concludes these extracts:—

“The Lord, who knows all things, saw at Geneva that there were souls
thirsting and famishing for truth and light. He sent His servants to do
more in two months than has been done in long years. I was very unhappy
in my soul when they came. But their entire consecration, the complete
abandonment of their will, made me understand in the depths of my heart
all that my life lacked. Glory to God, I am now assured that I am fully
saved through the blood of Jesus Christ, and by His Holy Spirit
sanctified each day, and kept each hour to do the will of my Heavenly
Father upon earth as it is done in Heaven. Five of my children have
passed from darkness to light, and are ready to give their testimony in
witnessing to other souls what the Saviour has done for them. Their life
is transformed; I can affirm it—I, their happy mother.

“I can affirm again, to the glory of God, that it is no delusion; it is
not imagination; still less is it by mesmerism or other such influences
that my children and I have been converted. But for us, as for many
others, consecrated lives, and hearts filled with the love of souls,
have spoken more clearly than ten thousand discourses.

“Oh! may the Lord give to those who have brought us so much good in
spite of persecution and the calumnies of their enemies, blessing upon
blessing, that they may reap in our dear Switzerland an abundant harvest
of saved souls. Amen!”


An idea of the solid respect and sympathy which the work had gained in
Geneva, may be gathered from the fact that on the 11th of January
upwards of 50 Swiss citizens, many of them gentlemen of position and
influence in the city, enrolled themselves as officers of order and
posted themselves at the doors in the passages and various parts of the
Reformation Hall each evening when the meetings were held there. They
continued their generous services in the most kind and courteous manner
(though often having rather rough work) until the meetings were closed.

 CHAPTER IV.


On the day of her expulsion, Miss Booth addressed an appeal to the Grand
Council of Geneva against the decree of M. Héridier. The following is
the text of the Appeal:—

 “TO THE PRESIDENT AND MEMBERS OF THE COUNCIL OF STATE.

  “GENTLEMEN,—I have to-day received notice of the decree of the
  Department of Justice and Police, withdrawing from me the permission
  to reside in the Canton of Geneva, and enjoining my departure from
  that Canton this day before 6 o’clock in the evening. The one reason
  alleged for this decision is that the Salvation Army has made several
  collections of money the use of which has not yet been accounted for
  in spite of the summons which I received from the authorities. My
  reply is, I have never myself made any collection, and have never
  touched the money collected. I am not the Treasurer of the Salvation
  Army, and the accounts are not in my hands. It was thus impossible for
  me to respond up to this moment to the demand which was made of me. I
  gave the fullest explanation to the persons who interrogated me. The
  only collections which were made were those made at the doors of our
  meetings. There has never been any collection made from house to house
  by members of the Salvation Army. I have not been guilty of any act
  whatsoever which would justify my deprivation of the permission to
  reside in Geneva, consequently, strong in the sense of right and my
  own conscience, I come to request you, gentlemen, to annul the decree
  of expulsion published against me to-day by the Department of Justice
  and Police.

 “Yours respectfully,
 (Signed) “CATHERINE BOOTH.”

In conformity with the Genevese law the Council of State appointed one
of its members to hear Miss Booth on the subject of her appeal. She
therefore came back from Lausanne to Geneva under a safe-conduct, in
order to appear before the Councillor of State delegated to hear her.
This took place on February 26th. The British Consul at Geneva supported
her by his presence at the interview. Miss Booth pleaded before the
Councillor of State, with clearness and earnestness, against the reasons
alleged for her expulsion. She reiterated the fact that the meetings of
the Salvation Army had always been held in private halls, that is to
say, halls belonging to private persons, or to societies, such as the
Salle de la Rive Droite, du Casino, de la Reformation, du Terraillet,
des Grottes, &c.; entrance to the former was entirely free, and for the
others cards of invitation were given, showing that they were private
meetings. They were never closed to the representatives of the public
authorities. The meetings, she avowed, had been several times
interrupted by noise and uproar; personal assaults even had been made in
the street. Miss Booth distinctly asserted that she had never been the
means, by a single word or act, of stirring up such manifestations. She
appealed to the fact of her permission of residence having been granted
for a year by the Council of State at the end of this long series of
meetings in connection with which these very disturbances had taken
place. Had they thought her responsible for the disturbances, they might
then and there have withheld the permission. It was only four days, she
reminded them, after this permission that the Council of State issued
its decree for closing the meetings. That decree had been scrupulously
respected by members of the Salvation Army, and since its publication
Miss Booth asserted she had lived in a most retired manner, having
accepted the hospitality of the family of M. Lenoir, in whose house she
was at the moment she received the warrant of expulsion. Concerning
these statements of Miss Booth in her own defence, the Federal
Government itself in its rejection of her appeal four months later says,
“The accuracy of the facts here recorded by Miss Booth to the Council of
State of Geneva cannot be disputed.” The Councillor of State appointed
to hear Miss Booth apparently assented to the justice of these
arguments. With a shrug of the shoulders he replied that these grounds
alleged by the Department of Justice and Police for her expulsion were
“only pretexts.” “It is not in truth for these reasons that you have
been expelled; it is on account of the noise you have made” (_bruit_).
Miss Booth, astonished, asked, “Do you then tell me, sir, that _false
pretexts_ were made use of for my expulsion?” The Councillor replied
hesitatingly, and somewhat apologetically, “These were only pretexts.”
He then spoke of Madame de Gasparin ... “a most distinguished lady,
belonging to one of the most ancient families of Geneva,” ... &c., &c.
Was the opinion expressed by Madame de Gasparin the true pretext then
for the expulsion? It would seem so in some degree. “You are young
women,” said the Councillor: “it is not in accordance with our ideas and
customs that young women should appear in public. We are _froissés_ by
it.” To this Miss Booth replied, with a sorrowful earnestness, and using
great plainness of speech, “Listen to me, I beg of you, sir. It is
contrary, you tell me, to your sense of what is right and becoming that
young women should preach the Gospel. Now, if Miss Charlesworth and I
had come to Geneva to act in one of your theatres, I have no doubt we
should have met with sympathy and approval from your public. We could
have sung and danced on your stage; we could have dressed in a manner
very different from, and much less modestly than you see us dressed; we
could have appeared before a miscellaneous audience, men and women,
young and old, and of every class; members of the Grand Council, M.
Héridier himself, and others, would have come to see us act; we should
have got money; Geneva would have paid ungrudgingly in that case; and
you would all have sat and approved; you would have clapped your hands
and applauded us; you would have brought your wives and daughters to see
us, and they also would have applauded. There would be nothing to
_froisser_ you, no immorality in all that, according to your ideas and
customs. The ‘noise’ we would have thus made would not have been a cause
of our expulsion. But when women come to try and save some of the 40,000
or 50,000 of your miserable, scoffing, irreligious population who never
enter any place of worship, when they come with hearts full of pity and
love for the ignorant and sinful, and stand up to tell the glad tidings
of salvation to these rebels, this mob among whom many accept the
tidings with eager joy,—then you cry out that this is unseemly and
immodest; you would not bring your wives and daughters to hear us speak
of Jesus, though you would bring them to hear us if we danced and sang
upon the stage of your theatre. Now you have expelled us; but still
there are these multitudes in Geneva who are dark, lost, unsaved; and
you know it. There they are; they exist. What will you do with them?
Say—what will you do? Are they not a danger? Does not their lost
condition cry out against you?” The Councillor of State was silent; he
sank into his chair in a state of temporary collapse. For the moment, at
least, the reality of the picture presented to him had touched his
heart; his manner was softened, though perturbed.

I may be permitted to commend the above arguments to others besides this
Genevan Councillor. Fathers and mothers have said, “We cannot approve
of, or think it right, that young women should come forward as
evangelists in that way;” and some have expressed a fear lest their
daughters should attend any meeting addressed by Miss Booth, or be
dangerously attracted by her example. These same parents, in some cases
professedly Christian parents, are in the habit of taking their
daughters to the theatres; in those theatres they may often see girls
younger than our young Evangelists, in every kind of attire—in male
dress, or scarcely any dress at all—performing their part in passionate
love scenes, it may be, for the amusement of the public. “Do you, then,”
I would ask these parents, “approve of young women coming forward
publicly in _this_ way?” If you reply “No”—(and probably you would not
desire your daughters to follow _that_ example),—then I ask, “How is it
that you can take your daughters to witness performances by young women
which you disapprove?” If you answer, “_Yes_,” then I would only ask
you,—What is your religion? Whence do you derive your principles of
right and wrong? As Christians who read the Bible, do you find anything
in the words or acts of Christ which enables you to conclude that He
approved of young women ministering to the pleasure of the public,—even,
we will suppose, in the highest and best exhibitions of histrionic
art,—while he condemned their giving any public testimony for Him and
for the Gospel? I do not find any hostile criticism dictated by an
inspired teacher, upon the bold and practical application of the
prophesy of Joel, by St. Peter, on the great day of Pentecost which
ushered in the New Dispensation of Liberty and of Power. He stood forth
and reiterated the startling and solemn words, in his address to the
assembled thousands:—“This is that which was spoken of by the Prophet
Joel; and it shall come to pass in the last days, saith God, I will pour
out my spirit upon all flesh; and your sons and your _daughters_ shall
prophesy; and on my servants and on my _handmaidens_ I will pour out in
those days of my spirit; and _they shall prophesy_.” And later, at the
close of that address, he said, very explicitly; “this promise is unto
you and to your _children_.” Never was the truth more clearly implied,
or with greater apostolic authority, that in “Christ Jesus there is
neither male nor female,” and that the Spirit of the Lord bloweth “where
it listeth,” producing outward manifestations, many and various and
wonderful.

In spite of the explanations which Miss Booth was able to make, strong,
as she said, in the sense of right and her own conscience, the Council
of State rejected her appeal.

The following is the text of the reply published by them to her appeal,
and which only appeared many weeks later:—

“The Council of State, on the appeal addressed to them by Miss Catherine
Booth against the decree of the Department of Justice and Police of
February 12, 1883, withdrawing from her the permission to reside in the
canton:

“Considering that Miss Booth declares openly that she occupies in the
Salvation Army in Geneva the highest hierarchical position;[17]

“Considering that in virtue of this position she is responsible for the
actions of the association which works under her orders;

“Considering that the Salvation Army has been the cause of grave
disturbances in Geneva, and that several of its members have disobeyed
the decree of the 2nd February suspending its exercises;

“Considering Article 28 of the Law of February, 1844, which runs thus:
‘The Council of State, in virtue of its supreme administrative power,
always possesses the right of sending out of the canton foreigners whose
presence may _be a danger to the interests of the country or to the
safety of the State_!’

“Having heard the report of the Commissioner appointed to obtain the
information required, in conformity with Article 5 of the Law referring
to the discipline of Foreigners, February 9, 1844;

“Be it decreed: To reject the appeal of Miss Catherine Booth.”

I have underlined the words “a danger to the interests of the country,
or to the safety (or security) of the State,” in order the more
emphatically to draw attention to the extraordinary change of attitude,
(shuffling it may be called), of the Genevan authorities.

It is a significant fact that in rejecting Miss Booth’s appeal, the
Grand Council entirely ignored, and by implication rejected as without
foundation in law, the ground of accusation brought against her by the
Department of Justice and Police. That ground was the so-called
irregular collection of money. These Councillors of State had probably
turned to the text of the law on collections of money, and finding that
it had been falsified on the responsibility of M. Héridier, they seemed
to have considered it more prudent to bury in oblivion, so far as they
were able, the past grounds of accusation? The irregularity of the whole
proceedings of the Geneva authorities in this matter, the vagueness and
astonishing absence of definitions in their accusations against the
Salvation Army come out strikingly in the course of the narrative, and I
venture to ask the patient consideration of the reader for the facts
which elucidate this. In bringing forward a fresh accusation, as the
Council of State does in its rejection of Miss Booth’s appeal, they
ought legally to have given her previous notice of the accusation, in
order to have given her time and opportunity for explaining herself.
This they did not do; and she thus found herself suddenly with a fresh
accusation brought against her of which she had no previous knowledge or
suspicion whatever, and at the same time an accusation so grave, yet so
vague, and so utterly unsupported by facts, that it was all but
impossible for her to refute it.

The law cited by the Genevan authorities confers on the administration a
power of the nature of such as, in our own country, has been
occasionally and rarely exercised, under extraordinary circumstances
which seemed to justify the suspension of Habeas Corpus, or some
extra-legal arrest, in the face of treasonable plots, threatened
insurrection, or discoveries of dynamite! Defenders, however, of
dynamitic measures for the reform of society, nihilists, and many other
dangerous persons drawn from the malcontent population of all lands into
free Geneva, have been allowed to write, to publish journals, and to
harangue meetings in peace in that city. The authorities only make use
of their exceptional powers under the influence of terror, in the
presence of two young girls who come saying, “Repent, for the Kingdom of
God is at hand,” and promptly secure for years to come (so they hope)
the “interests of the country,” and the “safety of the State,” by the
expulsion of the preachers!

In order the more clearly to place under the eye of the reader the whole
of the legal case referring to the expulsions from Geneva, I must take a
step forward in the narrative in the matter of dates, by introducing at
this point the appeal made by Miss Booth a few months later to a higher
tribunal, to the Federal Council itself, at Berne. It seems desirable to
give the whole of this rather lengthy document and of the reply to it of
the Federal Council, for those—and there are not a few—who have so far
had only a fragmentary, and sometimes a garbled account of the case
presented to them, and who desire to know on which side justice is to be
found:—

 TO THE HIGH FEDERAL COUNCIL OF THE SWISS CONFEDERATION.

  TO MM. THE PRESIDENT AND MEMBERS OF THE COUNCIL:

  GENTLEMEN,—Catherine Booth, eldest daughter of William and Catherine
  Booth, the latter living at 114, Clapton Common, London, subject of
  Her Britannic Majesty, has the honour to appeal to your high authority
  against:

 1. A Judgment of the Council of State of the Canton of Geneva,
 bearing date 2nd of last March, rejecting her appeal against a
 decree of the Department of Justice and Police of the same Canton,
 of the date 12th February, last, which withdrew from her the
 permission to reside in the Canton of Geneva, and directed her to
 quit the territory.

 2. Against the above-mentioned order of the Department of Justice and
 Police itself.

  The rejection of her appeal to the Genevese Council of State not
  having been communicated to the Appellant until the date of the 25th
  March, the lateness of the presentation of the present appeal is
  thereby explained.

  Miss Booth grounds her appeal upon the following facts and arguments:—

  (_Here follows an explicit statement of the facts and events, which
  have already been described in this narrative, after which the appeal
  is continued._)

  The Appellant as a subject of Her Britannic Majesty claims the
  privileges of the Treaty of Friendship, Commerce, and Establishment
  drawn up the 6th September, 1855, and ratified 8th February, 1856,
  between the Swiss Confederation and Her Majesty the Queen of Great
  Britain and Ireland. “This Treaty is based on the principle of a
  complete reciprocity between the ressortissants of each country.[18]
  It gives to the subjects of Her Britannic Majesty, the right of
  residing in any Swiss Canton on the same conditions and on the same
  footing enjoyed by the citizens of every other Swiss Canton.
  Consequently, British subjects are, together with their families, free
  to enter, to establish themselves, and to reside in any and all parts
  of the Swiss territory, on the same condition on which the Swiss
  themselves are permitted to enter, establish themselves, and reside in
  the United Kingdom of Great Britain, that is to say, on condition that
  they conform to the laws of the country.” The State Council of the
  Canton of Geneva appears to have misunderstood the terms of this
  International Treaty, since it withdrew from the Appellant on the 12th
  February, 1883, the permission to reside in the Canton of Geneva,
  which permission had been granted her on the preceding 29th January.
  Neither the decree of the Department of Justice and Police of the 12th
  February, nor that of the Council of State of the 2nd March following,
  pointed out any infraction whatsoever, on the part of the Appellant,
  of the laws of the Canton or those of the Swiss Confederation. The
  sole reason advanced by the Department of Justice and Police was the
  fact that the Appellant did not give an account to the authorities of
  the use of the money collections which were made in the meetings of
  the Salvation Army. Now, in the first place, the Appellant has never
  had the charge of the money, nor any charge whatever in connection
  with these collections. In the second place, the Genevese laws impose
  no obligation to render any account to the authorities of such
  collections. The Police Regulation of the 4th March, 1879, only places
  under this obligation those persons who have received from a competent
  authority a license to make collections of money from house to house
  or in the public streets. Such collections as these have never been
  made by the Appellant or by any member of the Salvation Army. The
  Council of State have in fact acknowledged the invalidity of this
  ground of accusation by refraining from reproducing it (in their
  rejection of the appeal of the Appellant), and by substituting in its
  place an accusation of an entirely different nature, in regard to
  which alone the Appellant has now to render her reply. The ground of
  expulsion now alleged by the Council of State of the Canton of Geneva
  may be examined from seven points of view (to which the appeal
  presently refers).

  The Appellant, it appears, then, was expelled from the Canton, not for
  any personal acts of her own, but for the actions of a group of
  persons, as it is professed, for which the Council of State hold the
  Appellant responsible. It is important to understand clearly that the
  Council of State does not lay to the charge of the Appellant any
  personal act whatsoever. This is most important from the point of view
  of the interpretation and application of the Treaty of Friendship and
  Establishment, for it results on the confession even of the Genevese
  authorities themselves, that the Appellant has never broken any of the
  laws of the country, and that consequently she has the right to place
  herself under the protection of the above named Treaty. The ground
  upon which the Council of State rests her expulsion is, the
  responsibility of the Appellant to the Genevese authorities concerning
  the action of a group which receives its orders from her in virtue of
  the “lofty hierarchical position” which the Appellant occupies in the
  Salvation Army at Geneva.


 I.

  Is the Council of State of Geneva qualified to appreciate officially
  the degree of responsibility of such and such members of the Salvation
  Army resulting from the internal organization of that Society?

  No.

  Because, First: The Council of State evidently possesses no documents
  sufficient to enable it to establish the degrees of subordination, to
  which members of the Salvation Army may be required to submit, and the
  amount of responsibility which the leaders of that Army are charged
  with in regard to those under them.

  Secondly: Does the Council of State possess such documents? If it does
  it is at least doubtful whether it would be justified in giving them
  an official sanction, and attributing to them legal consequences. It
  would be thus giving to them an importance which no Government has
  ever yet attributed to them.


 II.

  Even admitting a certain responsibility on the part of the Appellant
  in regard to the actions of members of the Salvation Army, it remains
  yet to be proved of what nature the acts are to which this
  responsibility applies. It is evident that they can only be acts which
  have been ordered to be performed by the Appellant. Even if the most
  complete and official resemblance were to be admitted between the
  Salvation Army and that which is commonly called an Army, and even
  supposing the Appellant were the General-in-Chief of that Army, it is
  clear that it would be impossible to lay to her charge the private
  acts of the soldiers under her. No general of an army has ever been
  held personally responsible for individual crimes or offences
  perpetrated by his soldiers. It is not therefore sufficient in order
  to prove the responsibility of the Appellant toward the Genevese
  Government that it should be proved that members of the Society in
  which she occupies a high position, should have rendered themselves
  guilty of a contravention of a Governmental regulation. It must be
  established that that contravention was perpetrated on her own order.
  Now the Council of State never asserts that this has been the case.


 III.

  Moreover, the Council of State does not define any one of the acts of
  which it makes the Appellant responsible. It neither indicates the
  persons whom it holds guilty nor the circumstances in which they have
  committed any offence. It is left to be understood only that there has
  been a contravention of the order by which the Council of State, on
  the 2nd February, _temporarily suspended the exercises of the
  Salvation Army_. No other act whatsoever is attributed to the persons
  for whose conduct the Appellant is held responsible. It is essential
  to note this point. Now, in coming to the question of these
  contraventions of the decree of the 2nd February, it is very difficult
  to give any exact definition of their nature. First of all, it is
  necessary to know what it is which the Council of State wished to
  suspend temporarily. Secondly, whether it had the right to order this
  suspension. When the Council of State was questioned by the members of
  the Salvation Army and other persons on the former point, it never
  gave any definite or categorical explanation which could serve as a
  rule of conduct; and with regard to the second point it is evident
  that the decree could not claim to extend to meetings held in a
  private house in which certain members of the Salvation Army may have
  taken part. Now it is a matter of public notoriety in Geneva that
  since the issue of the decree of the Council of State the public
  “exercises” of the Army have completely ceased throughout the Canton.
  The Appellant defies the Council of State to establish the contrary.
  It is very possible that certain members of the Salvation Army may
  since then have participated as individuals in religious meetings to
  which they have been invited by private persons at their own private
  houses; but there is no contravention of the decree of the Council of
  State in such an act; and, further, these are private actions for
  which the Appellant is in no way whatever responsible.


 IV.

  But if it were established that this order had been contravened, and
  that the Appellant herself was responsible for this, that would not
  suffice to justify her expulsion. The Treaty of Establishment
  guarantees to the subjects of Her Britannic Majesty free residence
  throughout the extent of the Swiss territory, the only condition being
  that they shall conform to the laws of the country. Now what is the
  sense and the exact meaning of this condition? Would the breaking of a
  law, or of a simple police regulation be sufficient to deprive a Swiss
  of the right of residing in England, or a subject of Her Britannic
  Majesty from the right of residing in Switzerland? Evidently not. To
  justify such a punishment, it is necessary that there should be a
  certain character of grave and persistent resistance of the law. The
  decree of the 2nd February does not cite any penal clause whatsoever
  authorising the execution of its order, so that in the case of any
  contravention of the decree on the part of a Swiss citizen no ground
  would exist for a prosecution against such. How, then, can it be
