  admitted that when the offence is committed by a foreigner, that
  foreigner can be punished by expulsion—a penalty much heavier than a
  fine or certain days of imprisonment? The Treaty of Establishment lays
  it down as a principle, in its first Article, that British subjects
  may be permitted to reside in every Swiss Canton, on the same
  condition and on the same footing as citizens of other Swiss Cantons.
  British subjects, then, cannot be expelled from any Canton except
  under the same circumstances in which a Swiss citizen could be so
  expelled. These circumstances are all specified by Article 45 of the
  Federal Constitution; but the Geneva Council of State does not cite
  any one of them to the charge of those who are expelled by it. The
  fact then even of a contravention of the _temporary_ police order of
  the 2nd February, would be for those who have been guilty of it, an
  insufficient reason for expulsion.


 V.

  The Council of State is itself so convinced of the insufficiency of
  this ground of accusation, that it has deemed it necessary to cite
  against the Appellant an article of the Genevese law of the 9th
  February, 1844, concerning police regulations in regard to foreigners,
  in the terms of which “the Council of State in virtue of its supreme
  administrative power has always the right to send out of the Canton
  foreigners whose presence there may _imperil the interests of the
  country or the security of the State_.” This declaration is analogous
  to that of Article 70 of the Federal Constitution, in which it is
  stated that “the Confederation has the right to expel from its
  territory foreigners whose presence may _compromise the internal or
  external security of Switzerland_.” These are measures of _public
  safety_ which every State has maintained the right to take in
  circumstances of a character eminently exceptional. Now what amount of
  weight has this article of the Genevese law in the presence of the
  Anglo-Swiss Treaty of Establishment? This declaration of the Genevese
  law bears the date of 1844. At that epoch the Swiss Confederation did
  not possess the legal competence which it possesses to day, nor did
  international relations at that period rest on so sound a basis. The
  maintenance of treaties with foreign States is a matter for which the
  Confederation is alone responsible. In any case of an appeal on the
  part of a foreigner against a breach on the part of the Cantonal
  Government of the International Treaty, which protects him in
  Switzerland, it is the Federal authority alone which is competent to
  take in regard to the foreigner and of his own Government the
  responsibility of the incriminated act, and of the grounds upon which
  that act rests. Under these circumstances, it is allowable to ask
  oneself if the declaration pronounced in the Genevese law of 1844,
  founded upon the supreme administrative power of the Council of State
  and anterior to the Federal Constitution of 1848, has not somewhat
  lost its significance. It must be noticed besides that this
  declaration is also anterior to the Anglo-Swiss Treaty which is based
  upon the Federal Constitution of 1848.

  But whatever may be said of this point of law it is not at all
  necessary to insist upon it in the presence of the inability of the
  Council of State of Geneva to establish or even to explain in what
  manner the presence of the Appellant and a few of her friends upon
  Genevese soil has imperilled “the interests of the country or the
  security of the State.” In so grave a question involving the
  application of an international treaty whose stipulations are
  reciprocal, it is not sufficient to quote a single article of a law,
  or simply to express an opinion. It is necessary that facts should be
  brought forward and proved. The Council of State of Geneva is bound
  therefore—First, to define the facts to which it alludes, and then to
  furnish the proof of the existence of such facts. Secondly, it is
  bound to demonstrate the manner in which these facts have “imperilled
  the interests of the country or the security of the State.” Until the
  moment when it shall have defined these facts and demonstrated their
  existence, the grounds upon which it bases its decree of expulsion
  cannot be admitted by any reasonable person.

  In fact, no one can believe, unless furnished with distinct proof of
  the same, that the few adherents of the Salvation Army, whose actions
  have been limited to the announcement at Geneva in private houses of
  the same evangelistic truths which are daily preached in all the
  Reformed Churches of Switzerland, and who cannot be accused of any
  want of regard toward the Constitution, the Government, or the people
  of Switzerland, can have placed in peril the interests of any part of
  Switzerland or the security of any part of the State; while the things
  which may indeed injure the interests of Switzerland are, the absence
  of any security for the foreigner who may be dwelling here; public
  disturbances which are allowed to continue unchecked; the prevalence
  abroad of the idea that any foreigner coming here is, without appeal
  and without mercy, at the disposal of the police.

  But the Appellant has too much confidence in the wisdom of the Federal
  High Council to suppose that it is needful to point out to it that
  neither she nor her friends can be held responsible for the troubles
  which accompanied their residence in Geneva, and of which they
  themselves have been the chief victims. It would be as true to say
  that a person robbed is responsible for the robbery committed to his
  injury, or a murdered person for the crime which has cost him his
  life.

  The Appellant can confidently affirm that she and her friends have
  conformed to every direction which has been laid down for them by the
  authorities; that the two or three advertisements placarded on the
  walls at the beginning of their sojourn in Geneva received, as the law
  prescribes, the sanction of the police; that these advertisements were
  put aside the moment it was stated that they had had a bad effect, and
  that the same was the case in regard to the musical instruments
  commonly in use among them in England, as soon as it was perceived
  that these instruments produced a painful effect upon Genevese ears.


 VI.

  The weight of the allegations concerning “grave disturbances,” and in
  general concerning the fatal consequences to the interests of the
  country brought about by the presence in Geneva of certain members of
  the Salvation Army, is considerably lessened by a close examination of
  certain dates. It has already been said that the Salvation Army held
  in Geneva during the months of December and January, seventy-three
  meetings, a certain number of which were accompanied by disturbances
  in the halls or in the streets. Let it be remembered, however, that it
  was on the 29th January that the Department of Justice and Police
  granted the Appellant her permission to reside in Geneva for a year.
  The exercises of the Salvation Army were suspended on the 2nd
  February, and the Appellant was expelled on the 12th February. Now,
  between these two dates (the 29th January and the 12th February) no
  disturbance had occurred of greater gravity than those which preceded
  those dates. A comparison of these dates shows that the disturbances
  mentioned by the Council of State in order to justify the expulsion of
  the Appellant had existed in the same degree before the 29th of
  January, the day on which her official permission of residence was
  granted to her. That is to say, that identical circumstances had not
  been previously judged by the Council of State to be of a nature to
  justify the withdrawal of her permission to reside in the Canton. The
  only new fact which had occurred was the pretended contravention of
  the decree of the 2nd February. (The Appellant refers to what has been
  already said on this subject.)

  The Council of State then is found to be speaking in contradiction to
  itself in supporting a measure so grave as expulsion from the Canton
  on the ground of facts which before seemed to it insufficient to
  justify the withdrawal of the permission of residence. Thus we have
  the true measure of the value of the ground of accusation cited by the
  Council of State.


 VII.

  The Geneva law of February, 1844, which grants to foreigners to whom
  the Department of Justice and Police refuses the permission of
  residing in the Canton, the right of appeal against this measure to
  the Council of State, secures to them at the same time the right of
  being heard by a member of that body specially delegated to that end,
  and of giving personally to the Council of State every explanation
  which they may believe useful in order to justify their appeal.
  (Article 5 of the above law.) This guarantee evidently is intended to
  allow the foreigner to explain himself with regard to the grounds
  alleged for his expulsion. Can this Genevese law be considered to have
  been fully executed when the Council of State, abandoning the grounds
  of accusation alleged by its own Department of Justice and Police,
  bases the expulsion upon entirely new grounds with regard to which it
  gave the foreigner no opportunity to explain herself? It is difficult
  to admit this. Such, however, is precisely the case of the Appellant,
  who finds herself confronted with new grounds of accusation in regard
  to which she has not been allowed to give any explanation before the
  Cantonal Authorities; whereas this would not have been the case had
  these grounds of accusation been mentioned (as they ought to have
  been) in the decree of the Department of Justice and Police. It is
  evident that in this matter the security granted to foreigners by
  Article 5 of the Law of 1844 has not been extended to the Appellant.
  It follows from the facts and reasons above mentioned:—

 1. That the Appellant as a subject of Her Britannic Majesty is
 shielded by the Treaty of Friendship and Establishment of
 September 6th, 1855, between the Swiss Confederation and the Queen
 of Great Britain and Ireland.

 2. That under this Treaty she has continually the right to reside in
 any Swiss Canton, on the same condition and on the same footing as
 a citizen of other Swiss Cantons.

 3. That in virtue of this right she received on January 29, 1883,
 from the Geneva Council of State, the permission to reside in the
 Canton for a year.

 4. That on the 12th February, 1883, the Department of Justice and
 Police of the same Canton gave her notice of the withdrawal of the
 above permission and of an order immediately to quit the Canton.

 5. That upon the Appellant having recourse to the Council of State,
 this authority, on the 2nd March, 1883, confirmed the decision of
 its Department of Justice and Police, while changing the grounds
 of accusation.

 6. That this judgment of the Geneva Government is in open
 contradiction to the declaration of the above-mentioned Treaty.

 7. That it is not founded upon any personal act of the Appellant of a
 nature to justify the withdrawal from her of the securities of
 that Treaty.

 8. That according to the terms of the said Treaty, the Appellant
 having the right to reside in the Canton of Geneva on the same
 conditions, &c., cannot be expelled except in such a case as would
 justify the expulsion of any Swiss citizen of another Canton.

 9. That the Appellant is not in a case to become amenable to such
 expulsion, and that the Council of State does not even profess
 that she is so.

  10. That a legal responsibility cannot be founded upon pure
 suppositions concerning her hierarchical position; and that,
 moreover, such a responsibility could not refer to any acts except
 those which the Appellant has herself commanded.

  11. That, moreover, the Council of State neither defines nor
 establishes any of the actions concerning which it undertakes to
 impose a responsibility on the Appellant.

  12. That neither has the Council of State established that the
 Appellant has contributed to “imperil the interests of the country
 or the security of the State.”

  13. That the “grave disturbances” indicated in the decree, besides
 being in no degree to be imputed to the Appellant, took place
 previous to the date on which the permission to reside in the
 Canton had been granted to her; and not having been judged then of
 a nature to justify the refusal of that permission, they cannot
 possibly serve to justify its withdrawal.

  14. That the grounds of accusation alleged by the Council of State not
 being the same as those advanced by its Department of Justice and
 Police, and not having been communicated to the Appellant, she has
 been deprived in this respect of the securities granted to an
 expelled person by Article 5 of the Genevese law, February 9th,
 1844. That (to sum up) the decree of the Council of State of
 Geneva, March, 1883, withdrawing from the Appellant the right to
 reside in the Canton, is contrary to the stipulations of the
 Treaty of 1855, between Switzerland and England, and ought, in
 consequence, to be annulled.

  For the above reasons stated the undersigned appeals to the High
  Federal Council, and petitions that it will please that Council to
  annul the above-mentioned decree.

 (Signed) CATHERINE BOOTH.

Here follows the Reply of the Federal Council to the above appeal, which
should be patiently read throughout.


 REPLY OF THE SWISS FEDERAL COUNCIL TO THE APPEAL OF MISS BOOTH.

  In _re_ the report of the Department of Justice and Police to the
  Swiss Confederation, and the other documents, whence are derived the
  following facts:—

  In _re_ the appeal of Miss Catherine Booth, born 1859, at Gateshead,
  England, daughter of William and Catherine Booth, now living in London
  (she being at this time in Neuchâtel, Switzerland), against her
  expulsion by the Council of State of the Canton of Geneva, 2nd March,
  1883.

  Miss Catherine Booth came to Geneva in the month of December of last
  year. In accordance with the Genevese law of February 9th, 1844,
  concerning foreigners, she obtained from the Department of Justice and
  Police, on January 29th, 1883, and in exchange for the deposit of her
  passport, a permit to reside in the canton of Geneva for a year. On
  the 12th February following, the Department of Justice and Police gave
  her notice of the withdrawal of this permission, and ordered her to
  quit the canton of Geneva on that same day before six o’clock in the
  evening. Legally this decision is founded upon number 5 of the Article
  27 of the Genevese law concerning foreigners, according to which the
  permission to reside may be withdrawn if the Department of Justice and
  Police learns that there exists anything which can be laid to the
  charge of the foreigner, which—had it been known in time—would have
  justified the refusal of the permission to reside. This decision is
  based in fact:—

 1. Upon what came to the knowledge of the Department subsequently to
 the date of the permission given, namely, that the so-called
 Salvation Army had organized upon Genevese territory collections
 of money, of the use of which they had not furnished any account,
 in spite of the summons which had been given by the authorities to
 do so, and in spite of the fact that such an account could have
 been given, since these collections were entered in a book the
 existence of which Miss Booth acknowledged, but which she refused
 to produce.

 2. Upon the fact that such a proceeding constitutes a grave
 infraction of public order and cannot be tolerated.

  Before quitting Geneva on the 12th February, Miss Booth addressed to
  the Council of State of the Canton an appeal against the Department of
  Justice and Police. She professes in this appeal never to have
  personally made any collection of money, nor to have held or had in
  her own hands the accounts of the Salvation Army, which indeed she had
  also stated before her interrogators. According to her the collections
  had only been made at the doors of the places of meeting, and never
  from house to house. Believing in consequence that she could not have
  come under any law in virtue of which the authorities could refuse her
  the permission to remain in the Canton if she had had previous notice
  of the ground of their accusation, she demanded that the decree of
  expulsion should be annulled. Conformably with Article 5 of the Geneva
  law concerning foreigners, the Council of State appointed, in view of
  an interrogation of the Appellant, a Commissary in the person of one
  of its own members, chosen, as the law requires, from outside the
  _personnel_ of the Department of Justice and Police. Miss Booth, who
  had after her expulsion retired to Lausanne, was invited to appear
  before this Commissary, and her interrogatory took place on the 26th
  February. The Council of State published on the 2nd March the
  following decree upon the appeal of Miss Booth. (Here follows the
  reply of the Council of State, which has been already cited.)

  Other foreigners had also arrived in Geneva about the same time as the
  Appellant, that is to say, on the 10th to 15th December.

  All these persons are members of a religious society, bearing the name
  of the Salvation Army, which originated in London in 1865, and was
  founded by William Booth, the father of Miss Booth, formerly a
  minister of the Wesleyan Church. The Society is under the Generalship
  of Mr. William Booth. Its organization is copied from that of a
  military corps. According to the indications furnished by the
  Appellant to the Sub-Inspector of Police at Geneva on the occasion of
  her interrogatory on the 8th February, the above-named persons occupy
  in the Salvation Army, or in the group of this Army now at Geneva, the
  following military ranks:—

 Miss BOOTH, Marshall.
 Miss CHARLESWORTH, her Aide-de-camp.
 Mr. CLIBBORN, Colonel.
 M. BOUILLAT, his Aide-de-camp.
 M. ZITZER, Captain.
 Mr. THONGER, }
 M. VINOT, } Lieutenants.
 Mlle ANNA FURRER, }

  Numberless meetings and public assemblies (the Appellant says 73) were
  held in Geneva by these persons, in large halls belonging to private
  persons or to societies. The advertisements used, the devotions, and
  the practices of the Salvation Army provoked some vigorous protests on
  the part of the population, and the public peace was daily troubled in
  a serious manner. For this reason the Council of State of Geneva, on
  the proposition of its Department of Justice and Police, decreed on
  the 2nd February the temporary suspension of the exercises of the
  Salvation Army. In reply to a letter from Miss Booth inquiring whether
  this prohibition applied also to religious meetings which might take
  place in private houses at the invitation of the inhabitants of those
  houses, the Department of Justice and Police made it clear[19] that
  the declaration of the Council of State was formal, and that the
  Department charged with the execution of it would not tolerate in any
  fashion whatsoever any participation of members of the Salvation Army
  in the proscribed exercises; adding that all those who should
  contravene the decree, or had contravened it, would be expelled from
  the Canton. Following up this announcement the Department ordered on
  Sunday, 11th February, the expulsion of four dignitaries of the
  Salvation Army, _i.e._, that of Bouillat, Zitzer, Vinot, and
  Charlesworth. As far as we can judge, the Appellant took no part in
  any religious assembly of the Salvation Army after the 2nd February,
  the date of the decree of the Council of State. This is not the case
  with regard to the four members above named. Meetings in which these
  assisted took place on the 5th, 7th, and 8th February, in private
  houses, and according to the statement of Miss Booth, in response to
  private invitations. Free access to the meetings, however, was
  granted, and the invitations for the meeting of the 7th February, held
  at Cologny, were even given from house to house by a servant of the
  proprietor of the house where the meeting was held. The assurance
  given on the 8th February by the Appellant to the Inspector of Police
  that the chiefs of the Army would abstain thenceforward from meetings
  even in private houses for religious purposes appears to have been
  observed. It has at least been officially stated by the police that
  Miss Booth had the evening of that very day engaged Captain Zitzer to
  retire from a meeting to which he had been invited in a private house
  at Eaux-vives, and where he had gone.

  With regard to the collections of money made at the end of the
  meetings, the Appellant stated in her interrogatory of the 9th
  February, to the Sub-Inspector of Police, M. Benoit, that the accounts
  would be published at the end of the three months of her residence at
  Geneva, and that the account books would be submitted to the scrutiny
  of the Department of Justice and Police as soon as her Secretary,
  temporarily absent, should have returned. She added that the sums
  collected had been used exclusively for general expenses, such as hire
  of halls, the necessary personal expenses of the officers, printing,
  &c., and that no one in the Army received pay. In a subsequent
  interrogatory, on the 12th February, before Police Inspector Müller,
  Miss Booth declared that it was impossible for her to furnish further
  information on this matter, as her secretary was still absent, that
  she did not know when she would be able to return, and that in fact
  she (Miss Booth) personally had nothing to do with the keeping of
  accounts, and in consequence could not give information on the
  subject. She added that the collections had always been made in the
  presence of the police, and that they had ceased to be made from the
  time when she was informed of the Geneva law upon the subject.[20]

  Several of the police reports demonstrate, as the Council of State has
  shown in its decree of the 2nd February, that the methods and meetings
  of the Salvation Army had stirred up during the month of January great
  popular agitation, and had daily troubled the public peace, while at
  the same time they seemed to threaten in the first instance the
  personal safety of the chiefs of the Army who had frequently been
  attacked. A letter from Dr. Olivet, Professor of Psychology at the
  University of Geneva, and Director of the Lunatic Asylum called
  l’asile des Vernaies, in a letter addressed to the Chief of the
  Department of Justice and Police of Geneva, dated February 11th, says,
  that in two recent cases of mental aberration which had attacked women
  at Geneva the cause of the malady was their participation in the
  exercises of the Salvation Army. This expert, as well as the Inspector
  of Public Health of the Canton of Geneva, Dr. Vincent (in a
  communication addressed to the Director of Justice and Police of the
  12th February), declare it as a certainty that the religious exercises
  practised by the Salvation Army must have unfavourable effects upon
  the mental state of “_nevropaths_” or of persons simply of a nervous
  constitution, from among whom the recruits of such religious
  exhibitions are generally made.

  Miss Booth has appealed to the Swiss Federal authority against the
  decision of the Geneva Council of State. In her memorial addressed
  from Neuchâtel to the Federal Council and to the President of the
  Swiss Confederation, through the medium of the British Legation at
  Berne, the Appellant attaches great importance to the stipulations of
  the reciprocal treaty of “Friendship, commerce, and establishment”
  concluded on the 6th September, 1855, between the Swiss Confederation
  and Her Majesty the Queen of Great Britain and Ireland. In virtue of
  the declaration of that international agreement British subjects
  possess the right of residing in any Swiss Canton on the same
  conditions as the citizens of other Swiss Cantons. Now the Appellant
  does not consider that she has rendered herself legally liable to
  expulsion. She does not consider that she would be found in such a
  position as that which would authorise the expulsion of a Swiss of
  another Canton, even if it were admitted that she were really and
  personally responsible for all which the Genevese authorities have
  laid to the charge of the members of the Salvation Army. She thinks
  that they cannot justly impose on her such a responsibility. She
  rejects the idea of the system which the Genevese Council of State
  have endeavoured to conjure up with regard to her upon purely
  hierarchical suppositions in order to make her responsible for acts of
  which she was not personally cognisant, and which she had not
  commanded. She adds that the authorities moreover have never defined
  nor established the acts of the members of the Salvation Army alluded
  to, and that they have no ground whatsoever for accusing her of having
  contributed to imperil the interests of the country or the security of
  the State. Moreover she asserts that the events characterised by the
  Council of State as “grave disturbances” had all without exception
  taken place previous to the date, 29th January, on which the
  permission to reside had been officially granted to her. They could
  not, therefore, seeing they had occurred before that event, adduce
  them after that event as a ground for the withdrawal of that
  permission. Finally, she alleges that the grounds of accusation
  against her alleged by the Council of State, being altogether
  different from those advanced by the Department of Justice and Police,
  and not having been communicated to her, she was thus deprived of the
  guarantees which Article V. of the Genevese law of February 9th, 1844,
  concerning foreigners, would have extended to her. In regard to the
  equality of treatment guaranteed to British subjects and to Swiss
  citizens of other Cantons by the Treaty of 1855, and in presence of
  the Articles 45 and 70 of the Swiss Federal Constitution, she
  considers she has the right to demand whether the expulsion of British
  subjects can be decreed by a simple decision of a Cantonal authority,
  and if an article of the Cantonal Police law is applicable in the
  case.

  The Genevan Council of State has confined itself in replying to this
  appeal of the 23rd May, to the presentation of a summary _exposé_ of
  facts, the substance of which is as follows:—“The Salvation Army lives
  by the _exploitation_[21] of credulous persons. In its Orders and
  Regulations, pages 61 and 64, it gives to this _exploitation_ a
  character which is regular and permanent. Undoubtedly, the Salvation
  Army must, in the name of the public interest, come under the
  _Cantonal Regulation of the 4th March, 1879, on the subject of the
  public[22] collections of money_. The Appellant, in her position of
  Marshal in the Salvation Army, a position which she has always
  defended, is responsible in the highest degree, as much for the
  infraction of this Cantonal Regulation as for the continual
  disturbances which have arisen.”

  After having further in a few words refuted the erroneous statements
  of the Appellant in regard to the question of her rights based on the
  Anglo-Swiss treaty, the Genevese Council of State concludes its reply
  by demanding the maintenance and confirmation of the decree issued 2nd
  March, 1883.

 1. In accordance with generally acknowledged principles of public
 law, principles to which the Swiss Federal Council adheres in
 theory and in practice, the declaration of a treaty between two
 countries ought to be looked upon as the international law
 according to which the condition of the rights of citizens and
 subjects of the one or other territory or State must be governed.
 The Appellant, as a British subject, has thus the indisputable
 right to claim all the advantages which the reciprocal treaty of
 Friendship and Commerce of the 6th September, 1855, between
 Switzerland and Great Britain assures to British subjects
 sojourning in Switzerland.

 2. Although the treaty in question has formulated as an international
 law, the principle of equality in the matter of residence and
 domicile between the subjects of either country, we must not lose
 sight of the fact that this equality is not meant to be of
 unlimited application. The citizens or subjects of one or other of
 the two countries cannot be admitted to enter, establish
 themselves, or reside upon the territory of the other except on
 the condition that _they conform to the law of the other country_.
 Moreover, it is expressly provided that the citizens or subjects
 of the one or other of the contracting parties residing or
 established in the territory of the other State may be expelled,
 or returned to their country by a judicial sentence or by a police
 regulation legally adopted and executed.

 By these clear and explicit reservations, contained in all
 treaties of establishment down to the most recent times which
 Switzerland has concluded with foreign States, the principle of
 equality in matters of establishment and residence between
 foreigners and Swiss citizens has been restricted in a most
 decided manner.

 3. The argument on which the Appellant endeavours to demonstrate that
 since the Confederated States in 1848 placed exclusively in the
 hands of the Confederation the charge of their international
 relations, and that having regard to the positive declarations of
 Article 70 of the Federal Constitution, 1874 (Article 57 of the
 Federal Constitution, 1848), the power of expelling citizens of
 the contracting States from Swiss territory can no longer be
 exercised by the Cantons;—this argument, we say, rests on an
 erroneous conception of the legal position in which foreigners
 resident in Switzerland are placed. In fact there cannot be any
 doubt whatsoever that the Cantons have the right, side by side
 with the Confederation, to exercise freely police control over
 foreigners on their territory. Moreover, the exercise of Cantonal
 authority in police matters in regard to foreigners has come to be
 even the rule, while the exercise of such police authority on the
 part of the Confederation is only the exception. It is a principle
 strongly rooted in both the doctrine and the practice of the Swiss
 Federal public law, that the Cantons in virtue of their
 sovereignty have the right of expelling strangers by an order of
 police, and particularly in cases of disturbance of public
 tranquility and order.[23]

 4. On the other hand, it is true that the supremacy of the Cantons in
 matters relating to foreigners is limited by the Treaties, and
 more especially by the Treaties of Establishment which the Swiss
 Confederation has concluded with foreign States. In this respect
 the Constitution and our Legislation oblige the Federal Council to
 exercise vigilance in order that the Cantons may not make an
 arbitrary and improper use of their powers, or act in defiance of
 the treaties, in police matters relating to foreigners who may
 settle on our territory.

 5. It cannot be doubted, after having established these points of
 law, that the police control over foreigners in the Canton of
 Geneva, established by the law of the 9th February, 1844, has been
 in no degree curtailed, either by the Federal Constitutions of
 1874 and 1878, nor by the Anglo-Swiss Treaty of 1875. There may,
 however, be room to enquire whether the application which the
 Cantonal authorities of Geneva have made of that law in the
 present case in the expulsion of the Appellant, is justified or
 not by the facts, and whether, under these circumstances, these
 authorities have sufficiently respected the legal forms and
 guarantees.

 6. With regard to this matter it is necessary in the first place to
 settle a question of form. In her appeal Miss Booth complains,
 among other things, that the reasons alleged by the Council of
 State for maintaining her expulsion were not communicated to her
 before the decision, in order that she might have been heard on
 the matter, and that thus the Council have not acted in
 conformity with Articles of the law in question. This objection
 does not seem to be well founded, because the expulsion was
 decreed by a Departmental decision for reasons known to the
 Appellant, and the Council of State was perfectly free in virtue
 of its administrative power, and in order to support the decree
 of compulsion in conformity with Article 5 of the law already
 cited, to base its own action on motives of public interest and
 the repose and security of the State. It had no obligation to
 observe in this matter, the formality prescribed by Articles 4
 and 5 of the law concerning the procedure which is to be
 observed in any matter of appeal against a Departmental
 decision.

 7. In the main it must be acknowledged that the authorities of the
 Canton of Geneva had the right to decree the expulsion of the
 Appellant. Miss Booth has declared herself that she holds, as
 Marshal of the Salvation Army, the highest position among the
 members of it which have been acting at Geneva. Now, the
 organization of that army is designed after that of a military
 corps. In virtue of that position, Miss Booth is therefore
 responsible for the acts and movements of a group of that army
 which is under her orders. Consequently, it undoubtedly was her
 duty to give an account in a complete manner, and without reserve,
 to the police authorities, of the collections made in Geneva in
 public meetings organized by the Salvation Army. Notwithstanding,
 this she neglected to do in spite of reiterated[24] notices given
 to her, and she has never explained her conduct in a satisfactory
 manner. There is no need to inquire here whether the notices in
 question were given to the Appellant by the authorities in virtue
 of a police regulation applicable to this particular case, or in
 virtue of the right of surveillance and police control over public
 collections, which belongs incontestibly to every political
 authority. The reasons alleged by the Department of Justice
 and Police of Geneva in support of its decree of expulsion
 (reasons confirmed by the Council of State) being a sufficient
 justification of the measures against which the appeal is lodged,
 the Federal Council abstains from entering into the ulterior
 reasons set forth in the decree of the Council of State of Geneva.


 DECREED:

 1. That the appeal of Miss Catherine Booth be dismissed as
 groundless.

 2. That the present decree be communicated to the British Legation at
 Berne, for transmission to the Appellant, and that it be also
 communicated to the Council of State of the Canton of Geneva.

  Decreed at Berne, 24th July, 1883, in the name of the Swiss Federal
 Council.

 (Signed) L. RUCHONNET, President of the Confederation.

 (Signed) — RIGNIER, Chancellor of the Confederation.

Intelligent readers scarcely require to have it pointed out to them how
hopelessly self-contradictory the whole action of the Swiss Cantonal and
Federal authorities has been in this matter up to this point. We have
seen, first, that the pretext invoked in the warrant of expulsion of the
Department of Justice and Police could not stand examination, but was
proved to be entirely unjustifiable; secondly, that the State Council of
Geneva found themselves forced to abandon that pretext (resting, as it
did, on a falsified regulation), and that they did abandon it in their
reply of March 2nd to Miss Booth’s appeal, and adopted other grounds for
the maintenance of those warrants, viz., that _the presence of these
foreigners (two ladies) endangered the interests and safety of the
State_! We now see, thirdly, that the Federal Council at Berne, in its
rejection of Miss Booth’s appeal, in its turn abandoned as indefensible
the new ground adopted by the Genevan Grand Council, and actually
re-adopted the false pretexts concerning the collections of money which
had been abandoned by the Genevan Council as untenable.

It is impossible to follow the whole of this extraordinary transaction
without a feeling of astonishment and regret. It is difficult to believe
that we are reading a record of Governmental proceedings in a free and
enlightened country in the nineteenth century. If such documents had
been turned out by some inexperienced junior clerk in a public office,
it would have been characterized as showing culpable carelessness, a
want of education, or an extraordinary absence of clearness of
reasoning, or all three. One cannot help asking—are there not on the
bookshelves of that capacious Federal Palace at Berne collections of the
different Cantonal laws and police regulations? If so, what fatality, we
may ask, prevented those Ministers of State, MM. Ruchonnet and Rignier,
from referring to copies of the laws and regulations of Geneva, in order
that they might verify the statements of M. Héridier, and clearly
understand the legal grounds upon which they themselves, as well as the
Cantonal authorities, stood, before committing themselves to the
publication of a document which was likely to be widely read, and has
been read with surprise and bewilderment by lawyers and others of
foreign States as well as in their own country. Had the Federal Council
called for a copy of the law _sur les collectes_, they would have seen
at once that nothing less than a deliberate falsification of that law
had served to furnish the Chief of the Genevan Department of Police with
an excuse for expelling Miss Booth. It is worthy of note that in
dropping the vague charge made by the Geneva State Council against her
of being a danger to the State, the Federal Council—whether consciously
or inadvertently—plead the cause of Miss Booth, establishing in their
reply the validity of a great portion of her statement in her own
defence. In doing so, they are obliged to fall back only the more
completely on the broken reed of the falsified law concerning
collections of money. It would seem, however, that some suspicion of the
inadequacy of that ground of expulsion (the collections) may have
crossed the minds of the experts who, we must suppose, drew up this
reply for the President and Chancellor of the Federation to sign. This
may be gathered from the following sentence in the reply, which I
reproduce:—

  “There is no need to enquire whether the notices in question (to
  produce the accounts) were given to the Appellant by the authorities
  in virtue of a police regulation applicable to this particular case,
  _or in virtue of the general right of surveillance and police control
  over public collections which belongs incontestibly to every political
  authority_.”

It is a most startling statement which is propounded in the sentence
given in italics. Every novice in politics is aware that no political
authority in any modern free State has, or would dare to assume, a right
of “surveillance and police control over public collections.”[25] The
Federal Council show here an extraordinary want of appreciation of the
guarantees of individual freedom provided by their own excellent
Constitution. They have also forgotten or they ignore the clause of the
Anglo-Swiss Treaty of Friendship, Establishment and Commerce already
quoted, which makes it illegal to search for, or examine or demand the
production of the books or accounts of those persons of either country
residing in the other. A well-known Swiss lawyer, of whom I asked
information on these points, informs me that no right whatever of police
control or surveillance of public collections exists in Switzerland.
Further comment on this subject is needless; and, indeed, it would be a
relief if the whole of the miserable business could be buried in
oblivion.

 “Oh! what a tangled web we weave,
 When first we practice to deceive.”

The little town of Rolle in the Canton de Vaud, on the shores of the
Lake of Geneva, now became a centre of the work for a portion of that
Canton. The first meeting, conducted by a few of the “Invincibles,” the
persecuted Salutists from Geneva, was held in a sort of greenhouse,
which was crowded in every corner. The florist to whom the house
belonged, said he trusted God with his glass house, and if it should be
broken he thought he had faith enough only to say “Amen!” A powerful
influence was felt here. The sound of prayer and of weeping filled the
crowded place from end to end. One of those present wrote, “the people
had only read books of falsehoods, and ridiculous newspaper articles
against us. But the God of truth will open their eyes. One poor old
Christian gentleman, who had been in this way deceived about us, was
moved to tears, and begged us to come in the afternoon to his house.”

The Vaudois government, following the example of that of Geneva, now
lost no time in putting out a decree forbidding the meetings. Owing to
the difficulty of finding private rooms, many meetings were held in the
open air. The English tourist indulging in memories of a Swiss summer,
may imagine that such meetings for worship in the open air must have
been very delightful, in the midst of fragrant pine forests, on mountain
sides, or in meadows richly carpeted with summer flowers, and in sight
of the dazzling snow of the distant mountains, suggestive of all that is
purest and fairest in the natural or spiritual world. And so indeed it
was at times, as, for example, on Ascension Day, when Miss Booth was met
by a number of her friends from Geneva, and a joyous gathering was held
by the shores of the lake, near Rolle. The whole day was spent in
prayer, thanksgiving, and the relation of God’s dealings with one and
another, until the sun declined towards the west, and the company
separated, returning homewards “with great joy,” even as the disciples
returned from Olivet after that prolonged gazing up into heaven after
their ascended Lord, who had “gone up on high, leading captivity captive
and receiving gifts for men”—the supreme gifts of the Holy Ghost—even
for the rebels who should one day lay down their arms and become His
disciples. But not always was it so pleasant or so easy to achieve a
Sunday gathering for worship and personal communion. Some self-denial is
required in the case of the hardworked man or woman who leaves the house
before sunrise to walk a long distance in inclement weather.
“Yesterday,” a soldier wrote from Rolle, in April, “we had a good
open-air meeting in spite of the police and the rain. We left the town
in small groups, like people going out for a stroll, having decided to
meet in a little path where we should have to walk in single file. All
went well and the column moved onward, everyone looking very happy at
the thought of meeting together with the Lord, although it was in secret
and at great risk. After some time of climbing and searching for a
suitable place in which to pitch our camp, we found a corner in the
brushwood among the long wet grass. We began to sing, when we were
interrupted by a whistle from one of our sentinels. We sent off a scout
to hear what it was. He reported that our friends the police were on our
track. We were quite on the mountains, and the showers of rain which
fell now and again, did not improve the rugged paths, when there were
any paths at all. But it mattered not; we all began climbing again to
find another spot; young and old, fathers, mothers and children, all
climbing up the slippery slopes together, helping each other, or pulling
themselves up by the branches of the trees, while the leaders in front
directed them by an occasional shrill whistle. At last we halted, our
feet wet and our garments draggled. We placed two sentinels on the
heights above, but we had no more alarms, and had a splendid meeting.”

Another wrote:—“Our Rolle comrades are obliged now to meet at three
o’clock in the morning; but even at that hour they have not been allowed
to worship God in peace, the night watchman having called up the police
to disperse them. Pray for our Rolle comrades; for it is almost
impossible for them to meet to pray without being hunted and stopped by
the police. As the lake is neutral ground, we are in treaty for the hire
of a large barge which we can anchor off the shore, reaching it by a
boat from the land, manned by strong salvationists....” “The power of
God was strongly felt at these Rolle meetings. A tall powerful man who
came to the meetings in the first instance in order to break them up,
was cut to the heart by some words spoken, and fell on his knees, and
another who had been a smuggler joined the ranks of the Salvationists,
and both became firm and devoted Christians.”

Towards the summer the spirit of persecution began to rage in the Canton
de Vaud, as it had done in Geneva. The meetings had ceased to be held
openly; small gatherings in private houses were all that could be
attempted. On the first of July the house of a Rolle citizen, in which
it was suspected that a few persons had assembled to pray, was attacked
by a large and riotous mob. As those within left the house, bucket after
bucket of water was poured upon them—men and women alike—drenching them
to the skin. At ten o’clock in the evening, a systematic bombardment of
the house was begun, and lasted till past midnight. Large stones were
thrown, and the doors forced open; windows and shutters were broken and
the front of the house seriously damaged. The Rolle Salvationists and
their leaders were obliged to conceal themselves as they could in holes
and corners during the whole night. The police were absolutely inactive.
Two days later several of the Salvation officers were threatened with
instant death if they attempted to meet for prayer. Fire engines were
brought out to play upon the poor worshippers as they came and went.
Another meeting was thus described.

“We began our meeting at six o’clock in the morning, on Sunday. There
were only a few old people gathered together. However, the Lord was in
our midst and blessed our souls. We met again at one o’clock to start
for an open-air meeting. We had two hours walk before us in the broiling
sun, but we felt the Lord was with us. We at last arrived at the
village, where we found the people gathered together waiting for us by
the side of the lovely lake. About 220 country people came from the
different villages around the place. They listened to every word, as one
after another spoke of salvation. We had not long commenced when a
terrible thunderstorm came on. We all went into a barn which a dear
friend lent us. The power of the Holy Spirit was here among us. When the
invitation was given to all who wished to give their hearts to God to
come forward, one big man came forward; then came another, and another.
There were six of them, who all fell on their knees at the feet of
Jesus, while we sang,

 “Reviens, reviens, pauvre pécheur!
 Ton Père encore t’attend.”

A Salvationist day’s work in the Canton Vaud is thus described:—

  About seventy of us went by steamer to the little town of Nyon, some
  miles higher up, on the shores of the lake.

  As a specimen of the kind of justice we receive at the hands of some
  here who have authority, we have only to cite the following incident:
  As soon as we started from the pier we all placed ourselves in the
  fore part of the steamer, and there began to sing some of our
  favourite hymns. We had not been long so engaged before the captain of
  the steamer sent word that he would not allow us to sing; so the rest
  of the voyage had to be spent in silence. An old Genevese who was with
  us told us that he had been in the habit of travelling on the boats
  for the last twenty years, and yet had never before seen any singing
  of any kind stopped. It is needless to add that had we been a band of
  ordinary tourists, however worldly or foolish our songs might have
  been, we should have been able to sing to our hearts’ content; but
  because we happened to be a band of Salvationists, and our songs were
  the songs of Zion, we were forbidden to sing them; and yet this is a
  country where people are so fond of boasting of freedom and liberty of
  conscience!

  On arriving at Nyon we had the pleasure of seeing Capt. Becquet and
  Lieut. Patrick, from Neuchâtel. We had not been long in the place
  before the whole town turned out to see these strange “Salutistes,”
  and of course the devil did what he could to annoy us, in the shape of
  four drunken men, who, poor fellows, did all they could, in their
  blindness, to do the devil’s work well, but, as might be imagined,
  with but little success.

Means and opportunities for meetings with the dear friends of the Geneva
portion of the Salvation Army were rare, and often accompanied with
difficulties. The open-air gatherings, necessarily arranged with care
and prudence, were often very delightful, and the memory of them will
never cease to dwell in the hearts of those who shared in them. Under
the fire of persecution, and in the pursuit of a common, arduous and
holy work, the firmest friendships are formed, and the richest memories
are stored up. Some of the poor converts of Geneva said they would be
glad to walk eight leagues to greet their leaders once more. One wrote:
“I walked that distance one day and I was not in the least fatigued,
although I had only a slice of bread from six o’clock in the morning
until five o’clock in the evening. God gave me strength to walk and to
go on in my hunger.” Having received information in the first week in
March that certain roughs in Geneva meant to organize themselves on the
following Sunday to disturb a private meeting of the Salvationists, it
was decided to worship upon a mountain in Savoy on the French side of
the lake of Geneva, where there were a number of quarries. The
arrangements were kept strictly secret, and the place of meeting only
made known to the soldiers the evening before at all the outposts of the
Army in the various districts. The weather was now beautifully fine,
perfect for open-air meetings—a cloudless sky during the day and a full
moon at night. Miss Booth, who was still residing at Lausanne, crossed
the Lake with a few of her friends on Saturday afternoon to the Savoy
shore, and went thence by rail to a village near the Swiss frontier. The
necessary notice of the intention to hold a meeting had been given to
the French authorities, the Prefect of the locality being thoroughly
friendly, and affording every facility for a meeting. The spot chosen
was a hollow in the cliff of the Grande Salève, which towered up a
thousand feet above the spot where the meeting assembled. It was early
morning, and the grey morning mist had not yet lifted, when a number of
adherents and officers of the army appeared along the road leading from
Geneva, with faces full of joyful expectation. Troop after troop was now
seen defiling along the paths on the slopes of the mountain, their faces
ruddy with the sharp morning air, waving signals of recognition and
loving greeting. Sentinels were posted at various parts of the roads in
the valley below to direct the worshippers to the spot. Great enthusiasm
prevailed at the thought of meeting once more with their exiled leader,
Catherine Booth, and all seemed justly proud to be able to give her the
assurance by their unbroken ranks that the power of God is sufficient,
not only to save but to keep, in the hour of the fiercest trial. A
circle is formed, and all the voices are raised in a beautiful hymn of
praise. Now all are kneeling upon the stones, and prayer rises to
God—such prayer as only such surroundings and such influences can
inspire. The blue lake lies below, flashing in the early sunshine; the
assembled group is still, however, in the chill shadow of the mountain;
but the mark of light advances rapidly. It was here, in the pure but
chill air beneath the shadowy cliff, that poor Charles Wyssa caught the
cold which attacked his lungs, and from which he never recovered. He had
come, exhausted by his labours, to the place of meeting. One who was
present wrote “Never can I forget the scene as, while one of our brave
men was telling how God had guarded him and kept him safe through a
storm of opposition, the first ray of sunlight broke suddenly across the
precipice above, and fell on the assembly, bathing us all in light. We
felt that the light of fully-restored liberty would be all the brighter
for the temporary shadow and gloom.” A very solemn and happy influence
pervaded the meeting as the Maréchale spoke of what God expected from
that band, and of all He had already done for those who had dared to
follow him at all costs. During her address a number of persons friendly
to the Salvation Army, and who had been let into the secret of the
meeting, continued to arrive. Some boys, sons of Roman Catholic parents,
were seen setting fire to the underwood near where the assembled
worshippers were gathered, and carefully feeding the flame. Then the
meeting learned that the smoke which arose straight and blue,
heavenward, was the appointed signal that a meeting of the Salvation
Army was being held. This had been planned by the friendly country
people and excursionists to the Grand Salève; so that when the afternoon
meetings began a large crowd had gathered. The sound of the singing
re-echoed along the cliffs, bringing other stragglers from all sides.
“Among those assembled were the poor Greek of whom we have already
spoken, and many, if not all, of those whose testimonies have already
been quoted.”

In order to secure quietness, and to escape being hunted by the police,
the meetings in the Canton Vaud were held at 5 o’clock in the morning.
When even this early hour was found to be too late to avoid disturbance,
it was changed to 3 o’clock in the morning. These harmless worshippers
were literally hunted “like a partridge on the mountains,” as King David
in his dark days phrased it. The difficulties and toil of climbing over
rocks and through forests did not daunt the hunters, who appeared to
follow up their prey with ever increasing zest. No bitter feeling was
ever expressed, or even felt, however, by the Salvationists against the
persons who thus persistently troubled their peace. All they ever said
of them was—“They know not what they do. They have been blinded and
deceived in regard to our true character and motives.” It was arranged
on one occasion to meet at a certain spot at the edge of a remote wood
in the Canton Vaud. On reaching the place it was found to be occupied by
a tall man in the dress of a tourist. He was surveying the landscape
through an opera-glass, but as the whole of the lake and the view to
which he had directed his glass was obscured in a thick mist, some of
the party suspected a trap, and one of them went forward to speak to
him. The man appeared confused, and after a time slipped away. The next
day the same salvationist met his friend the tourist on the platform of
the railway station in the uniform of a policeman!

The work of winning souls went forward. I take the following individual
cases from the records of one of the workers, giving them in his own
words:—

  “There was X., the bootmaker of Rolle, a notorious infidel, who used
  often to stand on the step of his shop and insult the Pastor of the
  place as he passed by, converted one night at a little meeting where
  he had come out of curiosity. He immediately testified for Christ, for
  which cause most of his customers left him, many coming to take away
  work which they had given him. But he never wavered, trusting in his
  newly-found Saviour. There was that wild girl who had brought such
  sorrow and disgrace upon her family, and whose remarkable conversion
  was the means of bringing father and brothers and sisters all to the
  Saviour’s feet. When the mother, who had been absent for a time,
  returned, great was her astonishment to find all the family thus
  changed, and praising God. In such a circle, and before such a
  transformation, conviction was immediate, and the mother also gave her
  heart to God. What a change in that formerly wretched, godless home.
  The sad-hearted mother, the drinking husband, the lost girl, the
  careless sons, all turned into praying and godly people!

  “A Vaudois being asked how he came to be an adherent, replied, ‘Ah!
  well, like everyone else around here, I was strongly opposed to the
  Armée du Salut, having believed the newspaper statements that it was a
  band of “purse cutters” and comedians who drove people crazy, and I
  had forbidden all my family to attend any of the meetings. My wife
  greatly wished to go, but I prevented her by violent threats. Hearing
  that there was to be a meeting of the Army in a wood on the shore of
  the lake, I went myself, however, out of curiosity and to pass time. I
  should tell you that I would not admit that women had a right to
  preach; nevertheless, it was by a woman’s preaching that I was
  converted. When I arrived at the meeting, instead of finding a crowd
  of long-faced melancholy people, I found a number of such
  happy-looking beings, that I forgot all my prejudices, and listened
  without even realising that I was listening.

  “‘The Maréchale explained with great clearness how a sinner could come
  to Jesus just as he was. Her words made such an impression upon me
  that from that moment I had no more rest of mind. Though a Protestant,
  I had never before realized how a man could be converted to God. When
  I returned home, I said to my wife (who had not dared to go to the
  meeting for fear of me), “Well, I for one will never speak against
  these ‘salutistes’ again.”

  “‘Notwithstanding I tried to resist my conviction; but it became all
  the deeper. I was miserable. The next day, while planting “haricots,”
  I was praying in an agony to Jesus to receive me just as I was, and
  take away all my sins, when suddenly a bright light seemed to flash
  into my heart. I saw my sins thrown behind me, and as if hidden by a
  thick veil. Such an immense joy filled my soul that I felt as if I
  should _eclater_, and I immediately praised God for having saved me.
  My old mother who had prayed for me ever since my childhood, and who
  was lying upon her death-bed, had the unutterable joy of seeing me
  converted and saved. How often she had said to me, “Thou wilt be
  converted yet! God has promised it to me! Thou art kicking against the
  pricks now! Thou art afraid of the Armée du Salut. Yes! I know why.
  Thou art afraid to have to give up thy worldly pleasures!” O! I do
  praise God that he has saved me and my whole family as well.’”

Meanwhile the _Maréchale_ and her friends continued to be cheered from
time to time, by good news of their Geneva converts. The following is
from the mother of a family at Geneva.

  “MY DEAR MARÉCHALE,—It is most interesting to note day by day the
  progress that our young soldiers make, and the power with which they
  proclaim salvation to sinners. Each week new recruits are added to our
  ranks. Ah! if all the powers of hell rise up, and if earthly
  authorities refuse us protection, we will lean all the more upon the
  Rock of Ages, who will never fail us in the day of distress.

  “The mothers whose children have been transformed know that the Spirit
  of God alone can have worked this miracle; and they can reply with
  joy, ‘Far from estranging our children from us, the Salvation Army
  restores them to us in leading them to Christ.’ It is the world that
  takes them away and destroys them body and soul, restoring them to us
  neither in this world nor in the next. Dear Maréchale, all the
  soldiers pray for you that the Great Captain may supply all your
  needs. We are sadly beset on every side; the enemy gives us no rest;
  but, glory to God, we are fighting and not discouraged. Yesterday,
  Sunday, we had six meetings. At eight o’clock in the evening the
  congregation was too large for the place, and we had to have an
  overflow meeting. It was a blessed day for many. We shall continue to
  pray and believe.

 “A MOTHER TO WHOM THE ARMY HAS RESTORED HER CHILDREN.

  “Geneva, 9th April, 1883.”

It may be interesting to compare for a moment some past outbursts of
intolerance in Switzerland with the present events. The following is
extracted from the records of the Council of State of the Canton Vaud,
in the year 1824. It is a decree against the Momiers,—revivalists of
that period:—

  “Considering that reports have reached us for several years concerning
  the conduct of a new religious sect vulgarly called Momiers,
  introduced into our Canton, and also concerning meetings of that sect
  which took place occasionally, even during the hours of public worship
  in the churches:

  “Considering that the erroneous and exaggerated opinions professed by
  the above-named assemblies are absolutely subversive of social order,
  as much from the point of view of the unity of the family as from that
  of their influence on civil and religious institutions:

  “Considering, also, that the above-named sectaries have placed
  themselves by their discourses, their actions, and their propagandism
  in a position of open aggression against the National Church:

  “Be it decreed,

  “That the meetings above-mentioned shall be expressly forbidden as
  being opposed to public order and religious peace.

  “All persons whose conduct tends to the gaining of proselytes to this
  new sect in a manner opposed to public order and religious peace shall
  be prosecuted and punished in conformity with Article 58 of the
  Correctional Code; and every person furnishing any building or room
  whatsoever for the holding of these prohibited assemblies shall be
  regarded as accomplices, and prosecuted as such.

  “Every person having permitted or directed such an assembly, or seen
  to have acted as its president, shall also be prosecuted....

  “Given under the seal of the Council of State, 15th January, 1824.

 (Signed)
 “CLAVEL.
 “BOISOT.”

  This decree was powerless to arrest the religious revival of that
  period. On the contrary, it gained for the Momiers the sympathy of
  many persons who otherwise would have remained neutral. The decree was
  only revoked in 1834, and during the whole of that time the Salutists
  of the period never ceased to cry “Forward!” And God blessed them
  abundantly. On the 22nd November, 1846, the Oratoire of Lausanne, the
  locality of the meeting of the Momiers, having been attacked by
  rioters, a new decree was issued, dated 2nd December, in which the
  Council of State _forbade every religious assembly whatsoever outside
  the National Church_. On the 5th February, 1847, the Council of State
  called out the military force, in order to put a stop to every
  religious meeting whatsoever outside the worship acknowledged by the
  Constitution, and ordained that those who transgressed this order
  should be visited with heavy fines, imprisonment, and other
  punishments, threatening to drag them quickly before the Tribunals;
  for now the war of the Sonderbund had kindled a still greater popular
  hatred against the Momiers. In June, 1849, the Grand Council again put
  forth a decree forbidding all the meetings. All magistrates or
  functionaries of the Government who took any part in them were
  dismissed from their posts. The sixth decree of interdiction continued
  in operation for ten years, and was repealed in 1859 by the Grand
  Council. In 1862 the Council of State established the principle of
  religious liberty which is now guaranteed by the existing Constitution
  of the Canton De Vaud, as well as by the Federal Constitution.

It is instructive to compare all this with the facts occurring in the
present year, 1883. The following is an extract from a letter addressed
to the Vaudois Council of State, November 4th, 1823, by a native of
Vaud, Mr. Jacques Magnin, of Mont la Ville.

  On Sunday, 2nd of November, certain Christians of Mont la Ville and
  the neighbourhood having assembled themselves together, as their
  manner is in a room which they had hired, in order to read together
  the Word of God, to sing His praises, and to exhort each other to a
  holy, just and religious life,—during our service, and while we were
  singing the 5th Psalm, a stone was thrown which broke two panes of the
  window, and which struck one of the worshippers. On going out of the
  meeting we were stoned by a great crowd of persons of all ages and of
  both sexes. One of my brothers and myself were pursued along the road
  with blows; stones and sticks being flung at us. We were knocked down
  and dragged along the road, and thrust into a ditch. In this
  condition, when I began to address my prayers aloud to God my Saviour,
  they filled my mouth full of mud, beat me in the face, and seizing me
  by the hair of the head struck my head on the ground several times,
  crying, “Pray now to your Saviour, and see now if He will come and
  deliver you.” The crowd shouted “kill him,” and other voices said,
  “No, don’t kill him, but mutilate him.” We were also robbed. They
  found in my pocket a Testament, and considering it quite worthless,
  they returned it to me. Then we were dragged to the Mont la Ville, an
  hour’s journey, the mob frequently repeating its outrages against us
  with fury, inventing also other modes of torture, such as tying us
  together, and also tying us to a block of wood which they forced us to
  drag after us, continually beating and kicking us, scourging us with
  rods, mocking us in every way, and pronouncing every kind of blasphemy
  against the Eternal God.

  As for me and all true Christians, this is not the kind of treatment
  which would induce us to renounce the Saviour who redeemed us. We are
  ready to seal with our blood the righteousness and truth of the Word
  of God, and as for you, honourable sirs, we warn you that you take
  good care that our blood be not upon your heads.

 A WITNESS OF THE VAUDOIS MOMIERS, 1823.

Many years have passed since the deposition of this witness; but the
true witnesses ever and again renew their youth like the eagle.

 CHAPTER V.


Allusion has already been made to the proclamation of the Grand Council
of Neuchâtel, on the 30th January. The news of this proclamation was
telegraphed to England, and cheered the hearts of all lovers of liberty
here, who rejoiced to think that while Geneva was acting in so arbitrary
a manner, in regard to religious and civil liberty, the Canton of
Neuchâtel, faithful to its great traditions, had opened its doors to
strangers guiltless of breaking any law, and had spoken publicly in the
tone with which we are familiar as emanating from free Switzerland. The
following is the text of the proclamation:—


 “TO THE CITIZENS RESIDENT IN THE CANTON.

  “DEAR CITIZENS,—

  “For several days past disturbances of a grave character have taken
  place on the occasion of certain religious meetings. At la Chaux de
  Fonds as well as Neuchâtel, public meetings have been disturbed,
  inoffensive persons assaulted in the streets, and windows broken by
  projectiles. At Neuchâtel the worship of a church, long established in
  our Canton, has been violently broken in upon.

  “The Council of State is determined to enforce respect for public
  order, liberty of meeting and liberty of worship. It has nothing
  whatever to do with the particular tendencies of this or that public
  assembly, or of this or that form of worship. It does not regard the
  doctrines professed in these places. Its duty is to protect the
  liberties and security of all. It will not fail in this duty. But
  before taking exceptional measures for the re-establishment of public
  order and liberty of meeting, which up to this date had been respected
  in our Canton, we desire to appeal to the Republican sentiments of our
  fellow citizens. One of the most glorious conquests of our Revolution
  of 1848, was the securing of liberty of public meeting, and absolute
  liberty of worship. These liberties are enshrined as follows in our
  Cantonal Constitution:—

  “‘_Article 11._—Public Assemblies, as well as Associations, which, in
  their object and in their methods have nothing illegal, cannot be
  either restrained or forbidden;

  “‘_Article 13._—Every profession of faith, and every form of worship
  alike shall enjoy full liberty, and shall have an equal right to
  protection, on the condition that those who profess and follow such
  forms of faith and worship conform to the law for the regulation of
  the external exercises of public worship.’

  “Up to the present time no special action has been judged necessary to
  put in force the law on the external and public exercise of worship.
  Absolute liberty is given to all churches and to all religious
  societies. Why should we now abandon this liberal practice so
  honourable to our Canton?

  “We appeal, dear citizens, to your feelings of justice and toleration.
  Let us give full liberty to the manifestation of all opinions, and of
  all creeds. Let us discuss them, if we will, and even judge them
  severely; but let us beware of seeking to suppress them. Let us avoid
  even the appearance of persecution. If that which you may hear in some
  public assemblies is repugnant to your feelings, avoid these meetings,
  and cease to support their promoters. It may be that these people are
  seeking to advertise themselves by an unwholesome notoriety. You can
  refuse then to give them the pleasure of creating such notoriety for
  them. If you wish to protest, convene yourselves public meetings.
  Discuss these matters, and manifest your opposition by reasoning and
  argument, and not by noise, mockery, and acts of violence. We have a
  confident hope, dear citizens, that you will respond to our appeal,
  and will again assume the calm and dignified attitude so appropriate
  to free Republicans. Do not let it be said that the right of meeting
  and liberty of worship guaranteed by our constitution are a dead
  letter.

  “Citizens of Neuchâtel, we boast of being a free and civilized people;
  let us show that we know how to respect the public manifestation of
  convictions, religious or otherwise, however absurd they may appear to
  us. Let us carefully guard all our liberties, and let us not destroy
  them for ourselves by ceasing to respect them in others. Let us know
  how to be tolerant, if we desire that others shall be tolerant to us.

 “In the name of the Council of State,

 (Signed),
 COMTESSE, President.
 GILLAUME, Secretary.


  “Neuchâtel, 30th January, 1833.”

The reign of justice and of liberty, however, in this Canton was but too
brief. The evil influences which had been at work in Geneva appear to
have travelled with telegraphic swiftness, and swept down on Neuchâtel
like a storm from the Jura. The letter of Mme. de Gasparin was in the
hands of all, as soon as an intimation reached the Geneva authorities of
the probability of the persecuted Salvationists taking refuge in the
neighbouring Canton. The evil seed found only too many minds well
prepared to receive it and to develop rancorous fruits “after its kind.”
It is said also that M. Héridier was at this time in communication with
his friend M. Cornaz, who occupies a similar position with himself as
head of the Department of Justice and Police in Neuchâtel. As kindred
spirits, probably they found it not difficult to agree as to the
continuance of the extraordinary measures already familiar to the public
of Geneva. The Salvationists—who had been invited to come to Neuchâtel,
by a letter signed by a certain number of the citizens—in the hope and
faith that their work would be now carried out under conditions of
freedom, had engaged the great rooms at the Hotel de Mont Blanc, the
proprietor of which, M. Elskes, had shown himself liberally disposed, in
this as well as in other matters, towards English visitors. In January,
the first pioneers of the Salvation Army arrived in Neuchâtel, and,
though welcomed by many, were assailed from the first with opposition
from the most noisy and degraded of the population.

In giving the sequence of the events which ensued, M. Du Pasquier spoke
as follows,—at the trial at Bondry:—“The Council of State, repudiating
the principles set forth in its firm and noble proclamation of the 30th
January, and allowing itself to become the docile instrument of certain
rioters, who could have been promptly brought to reason by a little
vigorous action, entered on a false path by partially putting a stop to
the Salutist meetings. The disturbances were the more encouraged by the
voluntary inactivity of the police, and their development led to the
Decree of the 15th of June, forbidding all meetings whatsoever of the
Salvation Army, until such time as it should have received the
authorization of the State; this prohibition being based on the
assumption that the Army was a ‘religious corporation,’ in the sense
laid down in Article 72 of our Neuchâtel constitution.”

A few friends subsequently opened their houses for private meetings, as
there was no prohibition to such. This, however, as we shall see, did
not prevent the roughs from persecuting and ill-treating all who were
seen attending such meetings.

One of the Salvation Soldiers wrote thus at the time of the earlier
meetings at the Mont Blanc:—

  “I cannot describe to you all that has passed during this day. Two
  hundred and fifty people in the Hall at half-past seven in the morning
  to sing and pray. At three o’clock the Hall was more than full. There
  are 500 seats, but there were more than 1,000 people crushed into the
  Hall, and several hundreds in the corridors. But in the evening, what
  a crowd when we came to open! Cries from all sides from those who were
  being almost suffocated in the crush. The movement in the crowd was at
  times like the waves of the sea. They say that never has Neuchâtel
  seen such a crowd at a meeting, either religious or political. The
  people in the houses opposite lit up the street and the crowd by
  coloured Bengal fire, which they lit in their windows. I could see
  that there were souls there who were thirsting for Salvation.”

  A little noise commenced, when at once one of our Neuchâtel friends
  rose up, and speaking to the meeting, said—“In the name of Neuchâtel I
  protest against this noise!” Then about 500 persons rose up and
  seconded the speaker, and out went the disturbers.

When the invitation was given for those who desired Salvation to come
forward and give themselves to God, two young men came out. One of these
came forward hurriedly from his place, and threw himself down, saying,
“I will! I will!”

Another soldier wrote at this time:—

  “Hallelujah, for all the Saviour has done in Neuchâtel during my
  absence. What a change! When I left there were only a few soldiers
  upon a very small platform, and now the platform is three times as
  large as that of Paris, and yet it is too small. On my arrival I
  attended a Holiness meeting for soldiers. What a joy to find myself
  amongst these redeemed of Jesus, won through the instrumentality of
  the Army which has been so despised, and where the devil has so
  furiously endeavoured to hinder the work of our brothers. Certainly
  Satan has not been dethroned from all hearts, but glory to God he has
  from many. Many souls have been rescued, and others who slept in false
  security had been awakened.

  “Yes, He manifests His power here, and if His servants have had to
  suffer persecution, and will have to suffer it, yet they begin now to
  rejoice over the fruit of their labours. The Saviour is faithful to
  His promises.

  “Since Saturday we have had two meetings—one on Sunday morning at 7,
  and another at 3. The hall was crowded, and many had to be sent
  away—but more than all was the blessing received. God alone knows how
  many have been blessed besides those who testified.... Tuesday at 3
  o’clock another meeting. On Wednesday morning at 6 o’clock again
  another, at which at least 400 were present. What slaughter in the
  ranks of the devil!

  “The Lord bless the Salvation Army at Paris. Amen!

  “Neuchâtel.

 “C. H.”

The storm, which had been daily gathering strength, now burst forth
first more openly at a meeting in the Hotel Mont Blanc at which Miss
Booth was present. The Neuchâtel authorities pretended that their police
force was insufficient to keep order. It is impossible here to refrain
from a brief criticism in advance. When Miss Booth was arrested in
September following, in the forest at a considerable distance from
Neuchâtel, the journey to which place involved rough work for those who
arrested her, there appeared on the spot the Prefect of Police with two
of his police staff and fifteen Gendarmes. Now if it was possible to
provide at a short notice so strong a force, in order to arrest an
unresisting girl in a remote forest, it surely would have been equally
possible to have supplied a sufficient force to prevent the disgraceful
outrages committed by the mob night after night in the streets around
the Hotel Mont Blanc, in the city itself. On the occasion of the meeting
referred to, the noise and disturbances within the hall were so great
that the words spoken could only be heard, as in the first meeting in
Geneva, in the intervals of uproar. The chief disturbances came,
however, from the outside. In an open space surrounding the Mont Blanc
there was a surging mass of rude noisy people. In the hope of quelling
the uproar, and of winning some of those souls, Miss Booth left the hall
and presented herself among them in the open air. She gained their
attention in a few moments, and spoke gently but firmly of the aims of
the Salvation Army, and their desire to win the people to true happiness
and peace. A ringleader of the mob, a man of some intelligence and of
powerful appearance, rudely interrupted her, and, holding up his pipe,
cried, “This is my god.” Then there came a wild response from the
people—“And the money-box is their god”—meaning that the object of the
Salvationists was to gain money. Confusion and uproar followed. But once
more Miss Booth gained a hearing, and then with much power she argued as
a messenger of Christ. “I appeal to you,” she said, “as intelligent men,
as men able to reason. Do you really believe that we would have left our
homes, our friends, our country, to come to Switzerland to be treated as
we have been treated, to be hooted, pelted, ill-used and blamed in every
way, in order to get money? No; I believe you have more good sense than
to believe such folly as this. You know what our motive is—that we wish
to see you delivered from the slavery of sin, and brought into the
glorious liberty of the children of God.” The man with the pipe stared
at her open-mouthed as she spoke. Looking at him she said, “You will
follow me into the hall, and you will listen to me?” She retired, and
this leader of the roughs quietly followed her accompanied by many
others. He put his pipe in his pocket, sat down, and listened with
wondering attention. With the ordinary protection which justly they
ought to have had from the authorities, and with liberty to make such
appeals as these in the midst of the storms of noise, blasphemy, and
ridicule they were accustomed to meet, there can be no doubt that the
Salvation Army would have won its own way, peace would have been
restored, and much blessing would have been the result for the
population of that Canton, concerning whom we hear the constant lament
of demoralization and lawlessness through habits of drink, and other
vices.
