On such occasions as the above, Miss Booth said to me,—“I was powerless;
there was no one there to enforce order; and I just threw myself on God,
asking Him to silence the people for a moment. He heard my cry; He did
it for me; He held the people in silence and attentive suspense until I
had given them _His_ message.”

The following extracts from letters (the originals of many of which I
have seen) will give some idea of the work accomplished in spite of the
interdictions of the authorities and the violence of the mob:—

  “Although the hall of Mont Blanc is still closed at night, and we are
  pulling against wind and tide, we are conquering, for the ‘Lord of
  Hosts is with us.’ It is easy enough to write pamphlets against us;
  easy to close halls; quite easy for the ‘enemies of the cross of
  Christ’ to hinder work in many ways, but when the Holy Spirit works in
  hearts and consciences, _who shall_ hinder? And, bless His name! He is
  working here.”

Another wrote:—

  “For many weeks I have wanted to tell you of the happiness I have in
  my heart since Jesus is mine. Blessed be the day when you came to our
  country; it had need of some ardent servants of God who would proclaim
  without fear His salvation. I know that I am not magnetised, as some
  here say; but I have found the way to true happiness. And you, dear
  friends, take courage; yours is the King’s work, and you, will be
  ‘more than conquerors’ through Him who has loved us.”

Another says:—

  “Before the Armée du Salut came to Neuchâtel I wanted to be saved, but
  I always fell again into sin and had lost courage. I praise Him that
  He sent you here to touch my heart. I had heard the Gospel often, but
  it now seemed new to me. I could not keep away from the meetings, and
  one day I resisted no longer, and gave myself to God. Since then the
  chains that held me to sin are broken, and my heart is at peace.”

  “How often I am asked,” wrote another Neuchâtel citizen, “What do you
  think of the Salvation Army? I should like to give you the answer from
  my own experience. I am sure it is just the sort of people we need in
  Neuchâtel. We are tired of studied sermons, most of which do not come
  from the heart, and therefore do not reach it. We need the Word of God
  with power, and the Holy Spirit. We are not at all surprised to see
  these dear friends amongst us; it is an answer to the prayers of many
  a child of God; but we are grieved indeed to see what hard work they
  find, and much of it because of the so-called Christians. The devil,
  too, sees he has already lost some of his slaves, and is raging.”

A girl soldier wrote:—

  “Last afternoon meeting (Saturday), they locked us into the hall with
  an immense chain and padlock, and after some time only we succeeded in
  getting free through another door, having made it known to the
  proprietor. The mob followed us home, throwing stones, mud, &c., and
  saying the most awful things. Few of our friends come now to the
  afternoon meetings, as there is always a crowd, and they don’t like
  stones and mud thrown at them, and some declare they are afraid of
  _being blown up_, as dynamite stories are still being circulated!”

After a meeting in which the danger of harbouring bitterness in the
heart had been dwelt upon, a soldier wrote:—

  “A man came to see me next morning, and said he had kept _rancune_ in
  his heart against a man; he must go and put things square or else no
  peace for him. A testimony from a thief, who told of deliverance from
  sin, stirred the whole meeting, and as Captain Becquet talked of the
  ‘love that thinketh no evil,’ hearts darkened by jealousy and hatred
  were broken down, and many were those who for the first time could
  honestly pray, ‘Forgive us, as we forgive others.’ On Sunday morning
  the hall was full at seven, and many standing. A powerful meeting. In
  the afternoon, 1,000 people in the open air. A young German, who has
  been one of the worst of our persecutors, gave his testimony for the
  first time, to the wonderment of his old comrades, some of whom have
  since followed his example in giving themselves to God. Glory to
  Jesus. Although surrounded by difficulty, we are marching forward;
  gentlemen and ladies have offered their houses for evening meetings.

  “Few of the Neuchâtelois Christians probably guessed that sin existed
  to such an extent so near their peaceful dwellings until its
  originator was openly attacked in the Salvation Hall, but they are
  fully alive to it now, and most anxious that war should be waged.
  Although all the public evening meetings have been forbidden by the
  police authorities, every afternoon sees a goodly congregation of men
  and women assembled together to hear of full salvation. The dear Lord
  meets with them, and sin-weary souls find rest in His presence. Most
  of these are women, of all ages. The tales they tell us of long
  unfaithful years spent in silence, and Divine calls to testimony to
  Jesu’s saving power which have been quenched, stifled, and unheeded,
  because of the universal tradition handed down from their fathers
  which shuts their mouths, calling them disobedient children if they
  dare to praise their Lord openly, have made us shudder for the
  responsibility the authors of these traditions have taken upon
  themselves.

  “Every day fresh cases come to our knowledge of this description. At
  last they are hearing and heeding their Saviour’s voice, saying, ‘Ye
  are my witnesses.’”

Two of the officers who had been walking through the streets on a Sunday
evening, wrote:—

  “The streets were mostly deserted at that hour, but the first band of
  people we met was a dozen or so of roughs, who not being able to
  disturb a Salvation meeting, were singing Salvation songs as an
  amusement—

 ‘I do believe, I will believe,
 That Jesus died for me;
 That on the Cross He shed His blood,
 From sin to set me free.’

  “Oh, how we longed that the words they idly and mockingly sung might
  sink into their hearts and awaken their slumbering consciences?
  Perhaps they did; who knows? We walked on through one street and
  another, longing to find an opportunity of inviting sinners to Jesus.
  Loud talking in a room with partly closed shutters attracted us. We
  peeped in; and there we saw several small tables surrounded by groups
  of sturdy fellows, just the kind the Lord wants, drinking freely, and
  spending their Sabbath evening in coarse talking and jesting.

  “We longed to go in, but, alas! we must not.”

From another Neuchâtel Salutist:—

  “The infidel doctor, with two other men of the same character, was at
  the afternoon meeting. I feel sure he will be saved. He listened to
  all that was said, and never smiled; had not even the courage to light
  his cigarette, but kept rolling it all the time between his fingers.
  Pray for the doctor—he is miserable. I wish you had seen him. O Lord,
  save our enemies, we constantly pray!”

Miss Booth thus wrote herself of one of the meetings, which were still
allowed to be held on Sunday, being forbidden during the week:—

  “On Sunday morning the meeting lasted from seven until a quarter to
  eleven. It was raining hard, yet the hall was quite full, and it was
  one of the most blessed meetings I ever attended. After singing and
  some prayer from two or three soldiers, the meeting was opened for
  testimony; and having so rarely a chance to testify in these times,
  they rose three and four at a time; although the meeting lasted four
  hours, they had not nearly all an opportunity....

  “A sister said that it was her wish that the Army should go all over
  Switzerland to bring the same happiness it had brought to her heart.

  “And then followed our French version of—

 ‘I mean to fight for Jesus,’

  and certainly, judging from the way our Neuchâtel comrades joined in
  that song, there is no doubt about their determination.

  “Here a man rose, who I was told could neither read nor write. There
  was perfect stillness when he told us how God had saved him, what he
  and his wife were, and what they are now. I was much struck, as was
  everybody, with his earnestness, and the power in his prayer
  afterwards, showing that he had received the Holy Ghost, which had
  done more for him than all the education in the world could do. Then
  rose Jacques. Everybody knows him; a very big powerful man. He told us
  how he was scoffed at and persecuted, but how happy he was in spite of
  all. A sister followed who has been suffering much from friends who
  ought to have been the first to pray for her. She said—‘You can take
  all away—reputation, everything—but you cannot take Jesus, who lives
  in my heart. He is everything to me. I can suffer all while I have
  Him.’

  “I cannot but glorify God for the wonderful things He has done in
  Neuchâtel when all has been, and is, in the dark. Our hall shut
  up—forbidden even to meet to pray; opposition of the bitterest kind
  and from all sides to contend with; yet on Sunday morning I felt like
  Miriam, after hearing those wonderful testimonies, taking a
  tambourine, and saying, ‘Sing unto the Lord, for He hath triumphed
  gloriously.’”

Extract from the Journal _L’Aurore_, March, 1883. Special correspondence
from Neuchâtel:—

  “The great news of the day is the presence of the Salvation Army. They
  have hired an immense hall in the Hotel Mont Blanc. Their meetings
  have been often disturbed from the outside by a noisy and
  evil-disposed crowd. There has been an outburst of hatred and
  exasperation, such as one would not have thought possible in our
  little town—windows broken, blows, ribald songs, and savage cries.
  Everything has been done to upset the meetings, which have, however,
  continued with calmness and courage, God being evidently with these
  people.

  “I was, for my part, much prejudiced against the Army and their manner
  of working. _But since I have seen them at their work_, and witnessed
  their ardent love for souls, and the strength and power which this
  consecration of body and soul to the Saviour gives them (the sacrifice
  even of their lives), I am fully persuaded that their work will be
  blessed to many. Immense crowds rush to their meetings—men in rags,
  poor women, porters, workmen, together with altogether different
  classes. These evangelists present an immediate, _present_ salvation,
  with much power and with so much simplicity that every child can
  understand it.

  “They have already many converts and many testimonies. We are not
  accustomed here to these public testimonies, and this habit shocks
  many people; but it certainly gives to those who testify a decided
  position as converts, and a great blessing accompanies it.”

I make no apology for the introduction here of a letter written to me by
Miss Patrick, the gallant little Salvation soldier at Neuchâtel, who
patiently endured insult and even personal violence, in the course of
the campaign at Neuchâtel, and who asked to be allowed at the end to
share Miss Booth’s imprisonment in order to minister to and cheer her,—a
favour which was granted to her. She wrote as follows:—

  “DEAR MRS. BUTLER.—I will give you as well as I can, the principal
  facts of our Neuchâtel warfare. Our first meeting was held on Sunday,
  January 21st, at 7 a.m., attended by a good number of Christians, most
  of whom gave us a welcome as servants of God, although many of them
  are now our opponents. It was a blessed time of consecration, and of
  supplication that the blessing of God might rest on our future work.
  The afternoon and evening services were very short, difficulty
  presenting itself in an overcrowded hall and a few disturbers. On
  Monday evening, amidst much crowding and confusion, God helped us to
  hold a Salvation meeting, several gentlemen of the town offering to
  keep order in the hall. After a powerful address by Captain Becquet,
  specially bearing upon the necessity of instant decision, and
  concluding by the question ‘who will give himself to God now?’ two
  young men sprang out from among the audience with the words—‘Je veux,
  je veux,’ and knelt down to implore mercy and pardon. These two are
  good soldiers under the banner of Christ to-day. The next evening the
  first serious disturbance occurred, a rush to the hall beginning an
  hour or two before the time for opening, and we soon saw that mischief
  was contemplated. When once the hall was full, the doors were closed,
  the police having arrived to quiet the impatient mob outside; but from
  the first it was easy to see their power was little or nothing. This
  night a band of organized roughs had entered the hall, the meeting was
  rendered impossible by laughing, shouting, whistling and song-singing,
  and although by far the greater part of our audience were anxious to
  listen, it was useless; we therefore cleared the hall as quickly as
  possible and passed out, followed by a howling mob, who laughed at the
  police, calling them ‘old women.’ No announcements of our meetings
  having been published, we were surprised at the rush of people.

  “Anxiously wishing to avoid any ‘scandale,’ we decided to close our
  hall for a few evenings, and continue only day meetings. A few nights
  later we opened with admission tickets. Storming, window-breaking
  etc., followed, which might with a little firm dealing on the part of
  the police, have been stopped at once, the rioters being chiefly boys
  from 16 to 20 years of age. The police however only proved useful in
  walking with drawn swords home with Captain Becquet, cries of ‘Au lac,
  au lac,’ resounding from all sides. Surprised and startled at this
  reception from the so-called Christian town of Neuchâtel, and
  surrounded by many Neuchâtelois who confessed their shame of these
  proceedings, we expected some means would be taken to render our
  meetings practicable. Instead of this, we received an order to close
  our hall at nights. This we did, although sadly enough, as the class
  we wished to reach and lead to the Saviour could not come until
  working hours were over. Still, God blessed our day meetings,
  especially one just then held for young women, when thirty-eight
  professed to give themselves to God. Our morning meetings also, often
  commencing at 6 a.m. were abundantly owned by God.

  “Slander and calumny was our next difficulty. A dear old French lady
  working with us was the first object of attack. Her character was
  called in question, and falsehoods set adrift, and after a few weeks,
  when leaving Neuchâtel for another field of labour, she remarked, ‘My
  people (in France) laugh at God; but this is worse; these people are
  too cruel!’ Cruel, indeed, was the pamphlet which a few days after was
  scattered through Neuchâtel, _i.e._, that of Madame de Gasparin, a
  book which will have to bear the light and judgment of eternity. ‘I
  will repay, saith the Lord.’ The charge of Jesuitism there brought
  against us, spread like poison; we were met by it in the streets, even
  in little children who shrank from us as if we would do them harm.

  “In visiting houses, door after door was shut in our faces; former
  friends were now foes, and would cross the street to avoid us; a tide
  of opposition swept against us; our friends were the omnipotent, the
  unchanging God, and a handful of those who dared to go against the
  tide, to stand with us in the dark, feeling that the King of Glory was
  our guide.

  “About this time our dear Maréchale, Miss Booth, arrived. In the
  evening we held many meetings in a crowded hall, with wondrous power
  and blessing. On Sunday, special meetings. But presently another
  arrêté came forbidding these meetings on Sunday, as the report was
  circulated that some roughs from Geneva intended to ‘blow up our
  hall!’ Once more these rioters were encouraged, and we were forbidden
  to hold meetings. I cannot remember the dates, but from this time one
  ‘decree’ followed another; forbidding first Sunday meetings, and then
  afternoon meetings, and then every meeting whatsoever at our _locale_.
  During this time many friends had thrown open their houses to us, and
  we accepted their invitations gratefully; every evening in crowded
  rooms of private houses we worked on. Many a happy, holy gathering we
  have had in tiny rooms and passages, many a sinner has ‘crossed over
  the line’ and is on God’s side to-day as a result of these meetings;
  so we praised God and took courage. Still papers, secular and
  religious, rained accusations upon us, the latter more bitterly than
  the former.

  “One Sunday evening—our hall being closed, and we longing for a united
  prayer-meeting with our soldiers (many of whom had been discharged
  from good situations because they were _Salutistes_)—we received an
  invitation from one of the leading members of a Christian Church to
  join with all our soldiers in their evening service. It was accepted—a
  prayer meeting was announced—a wonderful time followed—tears and
  prayers were mingled. Amongst others, led by the Spirit of God, our
  beloved Maréchale prayed. _God_ was not angry, for he answered in
  filling us all with joy and strength. But, as a result, a confused,
  divided church followed. We stood and wondered! What had we done? A
  _woman_ had prayed in a chapel! Just now a large meeting was held at a
  café against us; the avowed enemies of Jesus joined hands with His
  professed followers in order to have us sent away. A petition was
  started against us, to be signed at all the cafés; dear Miss Booth was
  struck in the street; ink, water, &c., were showered upon us from the
  windows of private houses;—in the streets, sticks, blocks of wood, mud
  and stones. Anonymous letters, threats and curses met us at every
  turn.

  “One evening our soldiers were attacked when leaving one of the
  private houses after a small meeting, and only because God kept us,
  did we escape and got home without anything serious. On June 12th, the
  house of Monsieur Aimé Humbert was assailed while a few friends were
  met for prayer;—from the Salvation band only myself and two soldiers
  being present. On Wednesday, June 13th, the house of Monsieur Convert
  was besieged by hundreds of infuriated people who stoned and yelled,
  smashing all the windows within reach, until after midnight, our
  soldiers escaping by means of a ladder.

  “This brought not a single reprimand for one of our assailants,
  although the ringleaders must have been recognised by the police who
  were present looking on; but it brought a prohibitory decree against
  all meetings in any place whatsoever, ‘even forbidding a Swiss citizen
  to receive us in his home for singing and prayer.’ The people, excited
  and led on by those who should have protected us, proceeded to all
  lengths. Some, we know, were paid to give us blows; and so many pints
  of beer were given for panes of glass broken, &c. After all my
  comrades had left Neuchâtel I stayed to cheer and encourage the Swiss
  soldiers; and the fire was hotter than ever. As I walked down the
  street, windows were thrown open, and I was hissed and spit upon,
  pushed off the pavement and followed by the police. One day I was
  chased partly through the town by a man who held a huge hatchet over
  his head, threatening to bring it down on mine. He said, ‘If you are
  saved you are ready to die, you say! Then death to the Jesuits!’
  Praise the dear Lord, it did not come down, although I was ready. At
  other times we heard from bedroom windows schemes to do us harm, and
  especially the English girl who would not go away. Never, perhaps,
  have we had such deadly opposition; never, perhaps, lighter and
  brighter hearts; for God was with us at Neuchâtel, and we are
  convinced that His work there shall never fall.

  “I write to you about these little sufferings for Christ’s sake,
  because I was told you wished to hear; but they are so little, and
  have been so outweighed by the blessing and joy by their side that
  they are as nothing, and we can only look back on these months with
  thankfulness for many lessons learned in the darkness.—Dear Mrs.
  Butler,

 “Yours’ fighting for Jesus,
 KATE H. PATRICK.”

Miss Charlesworth also wrote to me as follows:—

“Miss Booth and Miss Patrick were both struck in the streets of
Neuchâtel. We were kicked almost every day in the streets, in broad
daylight, pelted with stones, sticks, mud, glass, &c., &c., and even had
ink thrown over us. Miss Patrick was struck in the face, and so were
some of the soldiers.”

Miss Booth wrote to her friends about this time from Neuchâtel:—

  “I snatch a moment in the midst of numerous occupations to send you a
  line. This town is in a state of great agitation. Meetings are held
  against us to consider how to put an end to the Salvation Army. A very
  crowded meeting was held the other night. The leader spoke of
  expelling us, saying that we got money and brought disunion into
  families, and that we must be hunted out. Here a gentleman of the
  town, who stands up for liberty, defied them to expel us under their
  present constitutional laws, and said that liberty of conscience and
  meeting existed among them. The speaker was interrupted with cries of
  ‘To the lake, to the lake!’ Another meeting is to be held to-night to
  vote against us. A petition is also being signed against us; one is
  also being signed for us.

  “I feel impressed that God is about to undertake for us in a wonderful
  manner. The spirit of prayer has specially come upon me lately. If we
  only had a barn to meet in—any place for prayer. On Sunday evening I
  went to hear a pastor in the Eglise Libre; a great number of our
  soldiers present. When the preaching was over, a gentleman rose and
  said that he wished to continue the prayer meetings, such as he had
  held last year. I had before expressed to him a wish that, our hall
  being shut up, our soldiers could meet somewhere to pray, and he had
  asked me to attend this meeting, and ‘I was glad when they said unto
  me, let us go up into the house of the Lord.’ My soul longed for
  united prayer at this moment. The pastor left, and the after prayer
  meeting was opened by this gentleman, who asked that the prayers might
  be short and to the point. One after another prayed, our soldiers also
  and Captain Becquet, and, although I had not intended to do so, I
  followed. It was a blessed meeting, and I felt it very good to be
  there. One has since remarked, ‘Oh, I felt impressed that we should
  have stayed all night before the Lord. I can understand your all-night
  meetings now, and my spirit longed for one.’ And so did mine, but the
  devil, who knows too well the force of prayer, is doing his utmost to
  stop us.

  “The next morning (Monday) I hear a Council of the Church was called.
  It appears I should not have prayed; but the Holy Spirit led me and I
  dared not disobey; besides, great blessing was given.

  “Well, I am grieved to tell you that the church is closed against the
  night meetings. It is one of the saddest things I have heard of. But
  we must go on praying and believing. Meetings are being held in
  drawing-rooms of different friends every night; but the rooms are
  crowded and very hot, and it tries our strength very, very much. Just
  now is a critical moment with us. I do hope you pray.

  “Next Sunday morning we are going to have a special prayer meeting at
  six a.m. Expectation runs high. Oh, my soul, believe for great things!

 “CATHERINE BOOTH.”

I received from a citizen of Neuchâtel the following account of some of
the scenes already mentioned:—

“On the 8th May, some sixty persons were assembled in a private room for
prayer, when a great noise was heard in the street, and stones were
thrown against the doors and windows. The persons leaving the meeting
later were insulted, pursued in the streets, and pelted with stones and
mud. Four windows were broken, and it was with the greatest difficulty
that members of the Salvation Army at this private meeting succeeded in
escaping by means of a back door. The mob seemed bent on their
extermination. A complaint was lodged with the Prefect by the master of
the house (M. Aimé Humbert), but with no result whatever. The same house
(in the Rue du Château) was a second time attacked.

“On the 12th June thirty persons were assembled for prayer in response
to special personal invitations. They were nevertheless attacked by a
part of the population.” (A crowd of men stationed themselves around the
house, and when one of the young daughters of M. Humbert went to the
door to entreat them to desist, they assailed her with weapons which she
herself said were far more intolerable than stones or mud—that is, with
the most gross and indecent language, mixed with reproaches against her
father on account of his noble work for the abolition of the institution
of patented vice. Before these she felt compelled to retire, though
difficulties of another nature would not have daunted her). “Again was
heard the cry of the crowd, ‘We have our pastors—our good pastors—we
have no need here of salvation.’ It was generally, alas! in the name of
the pastors that they perpetrated the most illegal and outrageous deeds.
One of the Municipal Guards happened to pass at this moment, without
interfering or even noticing the violence of the mob. They cried after
him, ‘There is a meeting of the Salvation Army here, and these are
forbidden by the Council of State.’ ‘You can arrange the matter with
Mademoiselle,’ the officer calmly replied, and passed on. Shortly
afterwards a great crash was heard as an immense stone was flung into
the court, breaking several panes of glass, and covering the outside
roof of the court with the fragments. Several other windows were also
broken. This state of siege lasted till 11 o’clock at night, when the
mob, tired out, dispersed.

“Two days previously to this a similar private meeting had been arranged
by a well-known citizen of Neuchâtel, M. Convert, at his own house,
where the same kind of scene had been enacted. A crowd of three hundred
men surrounded the house, and bombarded it with stones and other heavy
missiles, yelling furiously. Above all the noise was heard the familiar
cry, ‘We have our pastors; we want no Salvation.’ The front door was
broken to pieces, and several windows were destroyed. M. Convert, who
had gone to make an application to the Prefect of Police, arrived, and
was pelted, and received with yells and groans from the populace. M. de
Montmollin, the chief of the Municipal Police of Neuchâtel, always the
friend of justice and order, endeavoured, but without success, to
disperse the mob and restore quiet. Two of his gendarmes who were
present endeavoured to the best of their power to quell the disturbance,
and were furiously threatened with being thrown into the Lake. The
persons attending the meeting only escaped by means of a ladder hastily
planted against a window opening on a small back court. M. de Montmollin
received them in a room of the Municipal Hotel, which stands at the
other side of the said court. The rioters having discovered their
successful flight, moved round and renewed their attack on the Municipal
Hotel itself. It was after midnight when, wearied out with this coarse
and illegal amusement, they retired, and the persons who had attended
the meetings were able to return to their homes. Private meetings in
houses were from this time almost abandoned, as it seemed useless to
endeavour to hold them without the smallest assurance of police
protection and at hazards so great as these.”

In regard to the pastors whose names have been so unhappily used as a
rallying cry by the enemies of religion and justice, it is certain that
they also will have to suffer persecution in their turn if they are
absolutely faithful to the trust now committed to them. Their sorrow
must have been great in witnessing the manner in which their own work
for the Master had been placed in direct opposition to a work equally
undertaken in His name. The mob, however, who thus professed loyalty to
their ‘beloved and honoured pastors,’ had probably never seen their
faces, nor ever heard the gospel message from their lips, and are as
ready at this moment to stone and insult these very men whose names they
have dishonoured, the moment they perceive that _their_ teaching also
strikes directly against all the vicious habits and institutions to
which they are in their secret hearts so deeply pledged.

During the earlier days of this agitation in Neuchâtel, M. Aimé
Humbert[26] wrote in a _brochure_ published at that time:—

  “For several years past my educational work has led me to inquire into
  certain economic questions, amongst which that of the influence of
  spirituous liquors and narcotics has presented itself in relation to
  its effect on families and on society. I was struck with the severity
  of the judgment which came under my eyes of one of the greatest
  historians of France—the Freethinker and Democrat, Jules Michelet. He
  says that, ‘in 1610 men began to have recourse more and more to the
  brutalizing habit of drowning their senses in drink, and soothing
  themselves into oblivion with tobacco. Two new devils then appeared in
  the world—alcohol and tobacco. Nations and entire races have been
  since enfeebled by them, losing themselves in this silent abyss, the
  depths of which means the annihilation of love?’ Nothing has given me
  so much pain as a recent report from the Director of the Normal School
  of Hindelbank upon the degeneracy of the youth of the Canton of Berne.
  The Educational Synod of Berthoud has expressed the same opinion.
  Amongst the principal causes of the degeneracy of the race, formerly
  so strong, all who have anything to do with schools and all the
  friends of the people write down on the first line—bad nourishment,
  whiskey, and the immoderate use of tobacco.”

M. Humbert goes on to record the fact that the first temperance society
in Switzerland, was formed in 1877 (during the session in Geneva of the
first Congress of the British and Continental Federation) and reminds
his fellow-citizens that when a law was framed by the Neuchâtel
Government for the repression of drunkenness, it passed to the
_Referendum_, and was rejected by the people. He observes that the
Salvation Army requires of its adherents abstention from drink and
tobacco. Will the people again protest against a moralizing influence,
so much needed, by driving out these new reformers? M. Aimé Humbert, was
one of the combattants in the struggle which preceded 1848, and one of
the chief actors in the deliverance of the Canton of Neuchâtel from the
Prussian yoke. He continues to be ever watchful for the true interests
of his country. He concludes his careful examination of the legal aspect
of the question of the Salvation Army as follows:—“The fall of Neuchâtel
will be all the greater from the fact that no State in Europe has
occupied so advanced a position since 1848, from the point of view of
the great principles of the common law. It would not be difficult to
demonstrate this by citing a whole series of legal measures of our
Canton. I need only, however, mention that which refers to religious
liberty. No commentary can be stronger on the spirit in which that
article was conceived, than the fact that although it has been put in
practice since 1848, up to the present day, the final clause, “freedom
of worship is guaranteed on condition that the worshippers conform to
the legal regulations on its external observance,” has been practically
a dead-letter; for our authorities have never found it necessary, nor
have they had the desire, to regulate the external exercise of forms of
worship. They have justly felt that the common law offers at all times,
and in all places, the best and surest guarantees.... I have set forth
the constitutional principles concerning liberty of thought, of belief,
of speech, of meeting &c., under which we have lived, and which have
been observed, whether they please every one or not. In this respect
Neuchâtel and Switzerland itself are only acting conformably with the
general law of progress, which constantly tends towards the elevation of
the intellectual standard of humanity. This law is inflexible; it does
not fail to come to the aid of peoples and governments who are faithful
to it; while it comes in open contact with all retrograde tendencies,
whatsoever may be their origin. The Federal Constitution of 1848 was a
work of progress, resulting from the Pact of 1815. Ancient prejudices
and selfish interests however, had succeeded in limiting the guarantee
of freedom of worship to the Swiss who belonged to one or other of the
Christian professions of faith. According to Articles 41 and 42 of the
above Constitution, Jews, even if they were Swiss, were on these points
excluded from the common law of the Federation. The great law of
progress was, so to speak, endangered by these two articles. The people,
it is said have sanctioned them. This is true; but liberty must be made
to enter Switzerland by another door; and the Spirit of progress set to
work simply to conclude the treaty of commerce between France and
Switzerland, which placed reciprocally on a footing of perfect equality
the rights of the citizens of these two nations, abstraction made of all
considerations of religious faith; and shortly afterwards (January,
1866), the Swiss ratified a new edition of the defective articles, which
the Federal Assembly were asked to revise in accordance with the spirit
of progress. It is absolutely useless to endeavour to promote a
retrograde movement in Switzerland, in this domain, or in any other
whatsoever. Just as well might we attempt to return to the days of
_diligences_. For those who complain of the inconveniences which steam
presents as a motive force, progress has no consolation to give. Rather
she warns—‘Wait a moment, and you shall have electricity!’ Let us get
out of this retrograde agitation as out of a bad dream; let us follow on
the path which our liberal institutions have indicated to us, outside of
which there is nothing for us but confusion and anarchy. Thus alone can
we work for the maintenance of our good reputation, and the
uninterrupted reign of peace and purity in our own dear country....
Instead of repressing and silencing the agitation around us, we must let
the steam escape, open the safety-valves, and bring all to the light—the
full light—of rational discussion. In the presence of every new problem
which arises in our troubled epoch we must never forget that in liberty
alone we have the key to every solution. To sum up:—The impressions
which I carried away from the meetings at the Hotel Mont Blanc, and
which were subsequently confirmed, are that the Salvation Army must rank
amongst the list of the strangest phenomena of our great social,
political, and religious crisis, while it is one of those enterprises
which neither ridicule nor violence will succeed in annihilating. Here,
if ever, the saying of the wise Gamaliel ought to be remembered:—“If
this counsel or this work be of men, it will come to naught; but if it
be of God ye cannot overthrow it; lest haply ye be found to fight
against God.”

The Council of State having received certain petitions for and against
the continuance of the Salvation Army in the Canton, and a further
petition signed by pastors who, while not sympathising with the methods
of the Army, claimed liberty for it as for all, proceeded to draw up a
report on the whole question to be presented to the Grand Council. It
seems desirable to give large extracts from that Report as follows—

  “Report upon the three petitions presented, namely:

  “That containing 9,944 signatures, demanding severe measures against
  the Salvationists.

  “That containing 163 signatures, claiming for the Salvationists
  freedom of worship and of meeting, and

  “That signed by 56 pastors and ministers of the Canton which, while
  condemning the practices of the Salvation Army, pray, that its
  adherents may be allowed freedom of action so long as their practices
  have nothing in them contrary to law and good behaviour, and demanding
  the continuance of religious liberty.”

The report gives a complete history of the action of the Salvation Army
in Neuchâtel, and from it I take the following extracts:—

  “It was on the 21st January that this sect held its first meeting at
  Neuchâtel in a hall which some one had let to them. Preceded by its
  renown for eccentricity, and accustomed to stir up noise and scandal
  wherever it gives its representations, the Salvation Army could not
  fail to attract the curiosity of a number of Neuchâtel idlers. Very
  soon the peculiarity of their proceedings, the erection of a platform,
  and the monotonous repetition of mystical hymns sung to vulgar airs,
  allocutions, appeals to come to public confessions, sale of songs and
  pamphlets, and repeated collections of money served to stir up
  opposition of different kinds. Many turned these proceedings into
  ridicule, while others were indignant, looking on the whole thing as a
  profanation of religion. Others, however, already accustomed by the
  practices of the Methodist sect to mystical exhortations, stood up
  energetically in defence of the Salvationists. Hence arose conflicts,
  and on the 25th January a window at the Hotel Mont Blanc was broken,
  on the 27th a tumult occurred inside the hall, and on the 28th the
  disturbances were repeated.

  “... The Council of State therefore has thought it desirable to refer
  to the constitutional principles by which it is guided in the matter
  of freedom of worship and public assembly. An appeal was made to the
  population to abstain from attending the meetings, but this was not
  heeded. The crowd continued to frequent the Hotel Mont Blanc, the
  tumult becoming so great that the gendarmerie, although reinforced,
  found itself powerless to repress the disturbances without resorting
  to force.... The Prefect of Neuchâtel received on the 2nd February an
  order to notify prohibition of the meetings to the Chiefs of the
  Salvation Army residing in Neuchâtel. Up to that time the Salvation
  Army had been looked upon as one of the numerous Methodist sects which
  under different names have invaded French Switzerland for fifty years
  past. We esteemed it right to follow in regard to them Article 13 of
  the Constitution. We acknowledged that by that, all Christian sects
  ought to enjoy equal liberty and protection, provided that they do not
  offend against public order and good behaviour.”

  (After considering the question as to whether these conditions had
  been observed, the report goes on to say):—

  “But in the meantime an unexpected event occurred; the publication of
  the Orders and Regulations of the Salvation Army by the Countess de
  Gasparin, from which we learned that this Army is not a simple sect or
  religious association, but that it possesses very much more the
  character of a religious corporation. The Council of State therefore
  put out on the 9th February the following decree:—

  “‘Considering the establishment at Neuchâtel of a sect known as the
  Salvation Army;

  “‘Considering the extracts from the Orders and Regulations of the Sect
  published at Geneva by Madame de Gasparin;

  “‘Considering Article 72 of the Cantonal Constitution, which
  prescribes that no religious corporation shall establish itself in the
  Canton without an express and always revocable authorisation of the
  Grand Council;

  “‘Considering that it is necessary to decide by a formal inquiry
  whether the Salvation Army does possess the character of a religious
  corporation before calling upon it to claim the authorisation of the
  Grand Council;

  “‘The Council decrees:

  “‘That it charges the Department of Public Worship to proceed to the
  above-mentioned inquiry.’

  “The different documents treating of the Salvation Army were
  consequently collected and studied by the Department of Public
  Worship, which, on the 14th March, addressed a succinct report to us,
  accompanied by the original documents. This report concludes that the
  Salvation Army constitutes really a religious Corporation, and that in
  order to establish itself in the Canton it must have the authorisation
  of the Grand Council. Just at this moment, however, the Army seems to
  have been forgotten; its meetings were not attracting any except a few
  devotees and inquisitive persons, and, after having consulted our
  Department of Public Worship, we did not think it expedient to attract
  public attention again to the question. We therefore reserved the
  right to act on the opinion of the Department of Public Worship until
  fresh disturbances should call for action. The Salvation Army was not,
  however, satisfied that its scandals should cease. New leaders
  presented themselves in order to stimulate the zeal of their
  adherents, and re-excited the curiosity which had partly died out.
  Miss Booth, the _Maréchale_, appeared in person, accompanied by her
  faithful adjutant, Miss Charlesworth. The meetings were now more
  frequented than ever, and were organised in the houses of private
  persons, to which the audience was invited by cards. After this
  certain persons of Neuchâtel were elevated to the rank of officers,
  and received the distinctive signs of this new religious order. The
  disturbances in the streets recommenced. The chiefs of the Army having
  attempted to recommence the meetings at the Hotel Mont Blanc, the
  Council of State thought it right to confirm its previous order, by a
  letter addressed to the Prefect of Neuchâtel on the 29th March.

  “... Although the chiefs of the Army had promised to conform to the
  order transmitted to the Prefect, we soon learned that meetings were
  continued. There was a meeting held in the open air at Pierre a Bot on
  the Sunday afternoon. Private meetings became more frequent, and
  invitations were so numerous that the enemies of the Army came to know
  of them. Complaints were made _apropos_ of broken windows and other
  damages, but nothing could be done, seeing that no one was able to
  indicate the authors of these excesses. The report of the Council of
  State goes on to describe another order given to the Prefect on the
  same subject, much in the same sense as the previous one. The
  Salvation Army now gave up all meetings except those in the open air,
  or those which were strictly private in the houses of friends.

  “‘Private meetings,’ says the report, ‘continued as usual, and we
  received a petition signed by Edward Becquet, Anna Furrer, and Kate H.
  Patrick, in the following terms:—


 “‘To the Republican Canton of Neuchâtel.

  “‘Gentlemen,—

  “‘The undersigned officers of the Salvation Army present in Neuchâtel,
  have received from the Prefect a communication concerning the letter
  you addressed to him on the 14th current, and take the liberty
  respectfully to present to you the following observations:

  “‘The Council of State forbade the public meetings in the evening and
  those on Sunday held by the Salvation Army, and embraced in this
  prohibition even meetings which might be held in private houses. The
  Council of State grounds its prohibition on Article 72 of the Cantonal
  Constitution, which runs thus:— “No religious corporation may
  establish itself in the Canton without an express and always revocable
  authorisation of the Grand Council.” Now, if we mistake not, this
  article was introduced to forbid the establishment in the Canton of
  religious Orders, but it never had in view religious associations
  which might hold meetings in the Canton. This may be proved by the
  fact that no religious association independent of the State has ever
  been called upon to demand the authorisation of the Grand Council. The
  State ignores them, or only accords to them the protection which it
  accords to all the other inhabitants of the country, as your
  proclamation of the 30th January so well sets forth. The Salvation
  Army is not a religious corporation, nor does it seek to be recognised
  as such. Hence we do not understand in what manner the Council of
  State is able to attribute to it that character. The Salvation Army is
  simply an association of persons who enter it and leave it freely at
  will, and who never take any vows of obligation. As such it has a
  right to the protection of Article 11 of the Cantonal Constitution,
  since it is impossible to find any proof that there is anything
  contrary to law either in its aims or the means it employs. Now such
  proof has not been adduced, and in the letter addressed to the Prefect
  of Neuchâtel the Council of State alleges absolutely nothing which
  could justify the exceptional measures adopted against us. Our
  meetings take place in private houses, and they have not in any way
  troubled public order or tranquillity. Consequently, basing our appeal
  upon Articles 49, 50, and 56 of the Federal Constitution, and Articles
  11 and 13 of the Neuchâtel Cantonal Constitution, we respectfully beg
  you, gentlemen, to be so good as to annul the decree which you
  published on the 14th of this month, and to give us freedom of
  meeting.

 (Signed)
 “‘EDWARD BECQUET.
 “‘ANNA FURRER.
 “‘KATE PATRICK.

  “‘April 20th.’

  “We cannot conceal our astonishment, Gentlemen, at the sight of two
  foreigners and a young lady of Lausanne taking upon themselves to give
  us a lesson in Constitutional law! It is true, however, that the same
  persons take upon themselves to preach lessons of Christianity to our
  pastors. The population of Neuchâtel continued to be agitated by the
  persistent presence of the Salvation Army. The Sunday morning meetings
  began again to be disturbed, and on the 4th May the popular meeting
  held on the Place du Port appointed an Active Committee which drew up
  the petition (against the Salvationists), which is now before us
  signed by 9,944 persons. The Council of State, after having examined
  again the publications relating to the Salvation Army, became more and
  more convinced of the special character of that association, which
  undoubtedly has the character of a religious corporation.
  Consequently, on the 22nd of May, the Council of State issued the
  following decree:—

  “‘Having received a request from the officers of the Salvation Army
  residing at Neuchâtel, demanding of the Council of State to repeal its
  decree of the 14th April forbidding the meetings of the Army in the
  evening, and on Sunday, and to authorise again freedom of meetings as
  often and whenever the Army may please to hold them.

  “‘Seeing that in accordance with the result of the inquiry made by the
  Department of Public Worship, the Council of State deems that the
  Salvation Army has the character of a religious order or corporation,
  being constituted upon a military basis, and placed under the
  authority, unlimited and uncontrolled, of a General, to whom all the
  officers of the Army owe absolute obedience; that such an institution
  cannot be compared to any simple association freely pursuing a
  religious end, and not interfering with the rights of the citizens or
  of society, but must be looked upon as a corporation such as is
  contemplated by Article 72 of the Cantonal Constitution, and the
  establishment of which cannot take place in the Canton without the
  authorisation of the Grand Council—

  “‘Seeing, moreover, that a petition is being signed in the Canton
  demanding of the Grand Council to take such measures as are necessary
  against the Salvation Army,

  “‘The Council decrees:

  “‘1. That it cannot grant to M. Becquet and his companions calling
  themselves officers in the Salvation Army the permission which they
  demand in their letter of the 20th April;

  “‘2. That the Council forbids provisionally for as long time as the
  officers of the Salvation Army shall not have received the
  authorisation prescribed by Article 72 of the Constitution, all
  meetings whatsoever and wheresoever of the Army.’

  “Since the publication of this decree the Salvation Army seems to have
  beaten a retreat. Occasionally some timid attempts have been made to
  hold meetings, but we have undertaken that all such meetings shall be
  repressed until the Corporation in question has received from the
  Grand Council the authorisation which is requisite.

  “After this historical exposition of the facts, it may seem useful to
  examine into the causes why the population of Neuchâtel, generally
  indifferent spectators of all such things, have been so much agitated
  with regard to the Salvation Army’s meetings. If this agitation had
  not existed, and if the authors of the disturbances in the streets had
  not felt themselves supported by the inward convictions of the great
  majority of the population, it is very clear that the public peace
  would not have been troubled as it has been. We shall endeavour to
  explain the matter. For several years past we have seen passing
  through our Canton missionaries of every kind and of both sexes, who,
  with the object of rekindling religious zeal, have organised meetings
  under different names. Our population, frequently annoyed by the
  intrusions of these modern apostles, and irritated at seeing
  themselves treated by them as if they were pagans, have not until now,
  however, disturbed their assemblies. Nevertheless complaints have been
  frequently heard on the part of those who found it disagreeable to see
  their wives and daughters leaving the domestic hearth in order to go
  to these meetings. Others were indignant, and justly so, on finding
  that secrets which ought to have remained restricted to the family
  circle were divulged in confessions publicly organised by the
  missionaries in question. Seeing that these so-called religious
  meetings were only temporarily maintained, those who suffered on
  account of them exercised patience; but now that the Salvation Army
  has published its intention to establish itself at Neuchâtel in a
  permanent manner, and to hold three meetings a day, and to introduce,
  so to speak, an element of permanent trouble among our families, our
  population have revolted. Three facts principally tended to exasperate
  the public: The frequency of the meetings; the manner of conducting
  them; and the collections of money. When a father of a family sees his
  wife or daughter leaving his house at half-past six in the morning,
  only to return at eight or nine o’clock, and then going out to a
  prayer-meeting at three or four o’clock in the afternoon, and perhaps
  also to a religious meeting in the evening, he may well ask himself,
  ‘What will become of the housekeeping, and what will the children do
  in the absence of their mother?’ Also there are women to whom it is
  necessary to earn money for the support of their families, and who
  must consequently sacrifice their work to join the mystic worship of
  the Army. It is not to be wondered at that the indignation felt at
  these things should overflow, and that anger should be kindled against
  those who thus draw away mothers of families from their domestic
  duties. Religious fanaticism evidently cannot become the ideal of
  modern society, and if we see violent manifestations against the
  attempt to introduce it into our midst we must not be astonished. We
  are accustomed to simple and dignified religious ceremonies, and our
  inward sentiment is wounded when we see sacred things lightly treated.
  We are not accustomed to see the state of souls and consciences
  paraded outwardly. A certain instinctive modesty tells us that the
  religious man must always remain modest and reserved. We would stamp
  under our feet these habits and sentiments of the Salvation Army.
  Their worship seems to us a vulgar parade. They think it necessary to
  cultivate a noisy style of music, to sing mystical hymns adapted to
  vulgar tunes, and incessant repetitions of the same cries and the same
  phrases. Their object is to produce excitement, madness, religious
  hallucination. They see that, rendered by their influence more or less
  unconscious of what they are doing, those who assist at their meetings
  will more readily give themselves up to pantomime and acting, will
  jump on the platform and confess their sins and the sins of their
  neighbours. Is it strange that those who desire to increase respect
  for religion, and not make it an object of derision should protest?
  and those who do not wish that such practices should lead to hysteria
  and madness, can they be said to be in the wrong?... And when we hear
  it asserted that that is the true Christian worship, that outside of
  these practices there is no serious religion, and when one sees people
  who pass for coryphites of the purest churches attending and
  supporting these practices of the Salutists, may there not be a
  temptation in the minds of many to take exception to Christianity
  itself, and to turn every kind of worship into ridicule? Truly it is
  an astonishing thing to have seen the pastors of our different
  churches taking under their protection a sect which despises them.

  “But the essential object of the Salvation Army is to collect money.
  Its publications turn continually on this point. The sale of pamphlets
  and journals in order to realise money, repeated collections,
  reiterated appeals for funds, &c., make it clear enough that the good
  things of this world have a great attraction for them. A book of hymns
  edited by Miss Booth, the Maréchale, concludes thus:—‘Funds are always
  needed.’ The report of the Salvationists war in 1882, published by
  their General-in-Chief, terminates with a subscription form in favour
  of the above-mentioned leader. Readers are invited to fill it up....
  On account of these incessant appeals for money, the Salvation Army
  realises large receipts. The effect produced on its friends is so
  powerful that they will go through all kinds of hardships to increase
  the collections. As the Salvation Army has a good end in view, they
  say the money originally destined for the support of the family may as
  well go to enrich the Army. The end, which is the sanctification of
  souls, will sanctify the means—that is to say, domestic robbery. Hence
  furious discussions, quarrels, and troubles brought into the midst of
  the family.

  “It is worthy of remark, that the Salvation Army began its operations
  in Neuchâtel on a ground which was well prepared. We have already said
  that for many years past, meetings of different kinds had led the way.
  Moreover, for a long time certain pastors of our churches have
  emulated each other in their zeal in organising outside of the regular
  forms of worship, meetings of every kind. They seem to have imagined
  that religion consisted essentially in going constantly to these
  extraordinary services, and those who took part in them did not
  hesitate to speak of themselves as the only true Christians. ‘Oh, he
  is a good man, he goes to the prayer meetings,’ we have frequently
  heard it said. Following the natural bent of the human mind, these
  Christians have been superceded by others professing to be still more
  Christian than themselves, who brought these meetings to perfection,
  adding to them more fervent prayer with the addition of groans and
  tears. Others followed with new hymns of a somewhat mystical nature,
  then a form of confession made its appearance, at first timid, but
  afterwards growing in publicity. Professions of faith were demanded;
  and last of all the Salvation Army arrived, outstripping all these in
  its practices, so much so that even some of the ‘mystics’ thought they
  went too far. No doubt the Salvation Army will in its time find
  another sect which will profess to be still more Christian than
  itself. We can only hope that the example the Salvation Army has
  given, will cause those persons who carry religion to extremes to
  pause and reflect. It is to be hoped that they will see that nothing
  can be more hurtful to the cause which they defend than to choose in
  place of a simple and dignified public worship which is open to all,
  and at which everyone may find himself at his ease, conventicles
  frequented by persons believing themselves more religious than their
  neighbours, and who unfortunately are very far from proving by their
  conduct that their religion has moralised them. The Salvation Army is
  the last drop in the already over full cup of popular indignation
  swelling day by day against those who by their religious exaggerations
  bring trouble into families. Though we may have to regret the
  unfortunate manifestations of this indignation, we must remember that
  those whom we blame are persons not accustomed to moderate their
  feelings. True indignation ought never to excuse violence, and it is a
  pity that certain of our citizens have not more self-control. A
  dignified and calm protest would have brought about more quietly the
  result we all desire.

  “It now remains for us to justify the last measure we have taken in
  regard to this question.... We are at liberty to examine how far the
  presence of this Salvation Army as a religious corporation is
  compatible with public order and good behaviour. In what sense do we
  call the Salvation Army a religious corporation? The special character
  of a corporation is that those who are adherents of it are placed
  under the discipline of, and are dependent entirely on the authority
  of a chief, while devoting their activity to the service of this
  corporation. We observe also that all religious corporations occupy
  themselves in amassing capital.”

  (The report goes on to argue further that the Salvation Army must be
  regarded as a corporation.)

  “All that we have stated appears sufficient to prove that the
  Salvation Army certainly constitutes a corporation, and we are of
  opinion, gentlemen, that you will approve of our having made reference
  to the necessity of your authorisation before permitting the
  continuation of meetings which we should not have forbidden if it had
  been the case of an ordinary sect. If this corporation demands your
  authorisation, we beg leave to pronounce our opinion on that head; it
  will be our duty to decide whether the existence of this corporation
  in our Canton is compatible with public order, and whether anything in
  its proceedings and manner of worship is an offence against decency
  and good behaviour....

  “Consequently, we propose to you, gentlemen, the adoption of the
  following decree:”—

  (The decree has been cited before, and ends thus):—

  “The Grand Council approve of the action of the Council of State in
  having forbidden the meetings of the Salvation Army until the
  authorisation for establishing itself in the Canton has been granted,
  and charges the said Council to take care that this decree is observed
  by all whom it may concern.

 (Signed),
 “COMTESSE, President,
 “G. GULLAUME, Secretary.

  “13th June.”

This decree was adopted and published throughout the Canton.

One of my objects in this narrative being to present a true picture of
the state of feeling on each side concerning the events which occurred
in Switzerland in relation to the question before us, I think it well to
give to the reader, as much as possible, the documents and written
testimonies referring to them, rather than to tell the story throughout
in my own words. These testimonies and documents speak for themselves
with no uncertain sound.

Here follows one of the petitions upon which the Grand Council based the
judgment and decree of June 20th, on the report presented to it by the
Council of State:—

  “MM. President and Gentlemen,—

  “At the beginning of this year a corporation called the Salvation Army
  entered our Canton professedly in order to preach the Christian
  religion. This corporation, organised in England, has produced amongst
  us a great perturbation, public order has been troubled at Neuchâtel,
  families are divided, and the peace of the country is compromised. The
  Salvationists as they are called make public collections of money, the
  employment of which it is impossible for us to control; and certain
  facts lead us to believe that our fellow-citizens have been more or
  less the victims of their speculations. Our population is excited, our
  youth above all are subjected to an evil influence, religion is
  profaned, and the great majority of the people are agreed in
  testifying that the manœuvres of the Salvation Army are a cause of
  demoralisation and of the negation of all the principles which lie at
  the root of our Neuchâtel constitution. Paternal authority is
  undermined, social ties are relaxed, the indigent classes instead of
  being succoured, suffer from this state of things; and in spite of all
  this, regardless of the maintenance of public peace, this corporation
  continues its agitation. Several citizens of Neuchâtel have taken the
  initiative of this petition, hoping that the whole Canton will join in
  it. The undersigned address themselves to the Grand Council with full
  confidence, entreating it to take into consideration the ways and
  means necessary for restoring order, by causing these speculators to
  cease their operations. In one word they pray that the practices of
  the Salvation Army may be put a stop to as quickly as possible. While
  bearing in mind Article 13 of our Constitution, we take the liberty,
  gentlemen, to draw your attention especially towards Article 72 of the
  same Constitution, which treats of the formalities to be observed by
  religious corporations.”

Here follow 2,278 signatures.

It should be observed that a large number of these signatures were
collected in the country villages of the Canton, and are those of
persons who had probably never seen or heard the Salvation Army, and who
readily adopted the views concerning it expressed by the zealous
collectors of the names. How many of these persons really regarded as a
grievous thing the “profanation of religion” referred to in the
petition, it would be difficult to estimate; certainly it could not be
said of the drinking, vicious, and irreligious portion of the people
that any feeling of respect for religion prompted their protest against
the Salvation Army.

The following counter-petition was presented by citizens of Neuchâtel,
which only received sixty-three signatures, some of those being,
however, names of weight:

  “M. President and Gentlemen,—

  “The undersigned citizens of Neuchâtel, or Swiss living at Neuchâtel,
  having learned that a petition is in course of being signed in our
  city praying the Grand Council to take measures in respect of the
  Salvation Army in order to forbid their meetings, feel it to be their
  duty to claim, on the other hand, from the Grand Council, that it
  shall respect the right of meeting and the law of the constitution by
  which it is conferred.

  “The undersigned are strangers to the Salvation Army, and reserve
  their judgment of it from a religious point of view, but they claim
  for its adherents, as they would claim for others and for themselves,
  the right of meeting, and of continuing their religious worship
  according to their own views; for this is a Constitutional right which
  cannot justly be either restricted or violated, even as the Council of
  State itself acknowledged in its proclamation of the 30th January.

  “Therefore the undersigned are persuaded that the Grand Council,
  acting now upon the same principles, will exercise in this particular
  case, as in every other, its high authority in order to maintain them,
  and in this persuasion we beg you to receive the expression of our
  profound respect.”

The third petition addressed to the Grand Council was from 59 pastors of
the Canton of Neuchâtel, who, while expressing disapproval of the
methods and action of the Salvation Army, besought for them, as for all,
freedom of meeting and of worship, pleading on behalf of complete
liberty of conscience.

A curious view of the duties of the Police in those troubled days was
expressed in M. Cornaz’s organ, _Le Reveil_, of May 14th, as follows:—

  “Some of our journals are raising a hue and cry about the inactivity
  of the police. It seems to us that they have a false notion of the
  duties of the police in a free country. The public force has no
  business to interfere for the protection of persons who exercise their
  functions in the _etat major_ of the Salvationists. It has no business
  to be accompanying the Maréchale wherever she pleases to walk,
  following her at a distance as though she were a Princess of the blood
  royal. It is not the business of the police to stand in the evening
  before the houses where the Salvation Army are pleased to hold their
  private meetings, which, however, strongly resemble public meetings,
  and at which are found persons who had no previous knowledge of each
  other. If these meetings are private, those who convene them have no
  more right to the protection of the police than a person who asks a
  few friends to take tea.[27] If, on the contrary, these meetings may
  truly be called public meetings, owing to the number of those
  attending them, they are illegal, and a violation of the formal decree
  of the Council of State. The police have no right to take any steps
  for the protection of an illegal thing. The Salvation Army is in our
  Canton a permanent occasion of scandal and disorder. Excepting a few
  honest persons like Colonel de Perrot, the greater number of its
  adepts are very worldly people who simply wish to give themselves the
  airs of religious folk. They think they have found a means, for want
  of any other, of distinguishing themselves from the vulgar mob. They
  have a jargon, a fashion of their own; they are a mixture of vanity
  and false humility, of mimicry and false education. For a long time
  past we have been disgusted with British importations of this
  character, which so strongly savour of base speculation that the most
  improbable thing that could happen (even in the opinion of the
  Salvationists themselves) is that they should voluntarily and
  spontaneously give up collecting money, amassing large sums, and
  living at the expense of the public like any wretched mountebanks who
  might visit our town. Our people who toil from morning till evening do
  not quite understand that there is any obligation to show any
  particular respect to people without education and without talent, who
  pay themselves out of their pockets for gesticulating for two or three
  hours a-day, and shouting ‘hallelujahs’ and ‘amens.’ They think that
  the _Maréchale_ and her female companions would be better employed—and
  more usefully—knitting stockings or studying a cookery book. Some of
  our contemporaries have come to the conclusion that it would be well
  to ask for the establishment of _special constables_, who would be a
  kind of body guard for Miss Booth, and sentinels at the Mont Blanc. We
  doubt if they would find many persons willing to carry out this
  brilliant idea, and we think that, if carried out, it might only prove
  the signal for street brawls in which the Salvationist acrobats might
  come badly off. And after all, what right would these gentlemen have
  to act as police against their fellow-citizens? and what measures
  could they take to prevent the formation in turn of a special
  batallion opposed to them, who would make short work of their
  usurpation of public police functions? There is no doubt that the
  Salvation Army in coming here has greatly shaken and shattered the
  nervous system of our population, who are naturally very
  impressionable. If it has succeeded in exalting some, however, it has
  completely exasperated others; and herein it has obtained what it
  sought. ‘Those who sow the wind, must reap the tempest.’”

The _Evangeliste_ referred to the same events in the following remarks:—

  “The town of Neuchâtel continues to be greatly agitated on the
  question of the Salvation Army. The meetings which the English
  Evangelists held on Sunday, 6th May, at the Hotel Mont Blanc, were
  violently assailed, and people going out from these were grossly
  insulted and maltreated. The Federal Colonel, M. de Perrot, instructor
  of artillery, who was present in uniform, kept order to the utmost of
  his power in the hall. He was himself assaulted and abused by the
  rioters,—‘indignant,’ as certain of the journals say, ‘at seeing an
  officer in active service, fulfilling the functions of a policeman
  amidst a troop of mountebanks, who have made such a ridiculous noise
  in the world.’ The same day Colonel de Perrot wrote to the _Gazette_
  of Lausanne a letter, in which he complains of the attitude of the
  religious journals, and declares that the Salvationists are Christians
  such as have never yet been seen, who are not content with fine words
  and phrases, but who practice the Gospel in the manner of the
  Christians of the Primitive Church.”

Colonel de Perrot, a member of one of the ancient families of
Switzerland, is instructor in musketry practice of the Swiss troops. The
discipline and influence he exercises in that capacity cannot be too
highly commended. In conversation with him one is reminded of our
historical portraits of some of Cromwell’s Ironsides. A man of fervent
and practical piety, great simplicity of character, and with a short
brusque mode of expressing himself, even in religious exercises, he
appears a thorough type of the old-fashioned Christian soldier.

The following is the letter of Colonel de Perrot alluded to; addressed
to the _Gazette de Lausanne_.

“I ask for a small space to protest publicly against the infamous
treatment and calumnies to which the members of the Salvation Army are
exposed at Neuchâtel. I know these people personally; I have both seen
and heard them in public. I have had the honour and privilege to receive
them at my table, and even to offer to one of them hospitality for a
short time. I have read all the polemical arguments of Madame de
Gasparin, &c., all the attacks of the so-called religious journals
which, with an exception here and there, have scattered these calumnies
broadcast, while never yet have they shown the common fairness of openly
retracting the infamies they have published. “Every day one hears these
clever writers and pious orators attack the Salvation Army in public.
But has anyone ever heard or seen them speak or take up the pen to claim
the protection of the laws in favour of these persecuted brothers and
sisters? No! these good people in clerical garb, and these well-known
writers busy themselves only, like many others, in the ignoble work of
throwing stones at two men and two women! They say it is a foreign
importation, and must be expelled! It is too late! The truth has burst
upon us! We have seen these simple, modest, fearless, loving Christians
at work. If they are expelled, there will be hundreds of persons who
must be expelled with them.

“The Salvation Army is exposed at Neuchâtel to the insults, the hatred,
the contempt, of a certain number of blackguards who, feeling themselves
supported by an approving silence on the part of their betters, take the
liberty, and are permitted in broad daylight, to attack and burst in the
doors of the Hotel Mont Blanc—to throw mud and offal at the officers,
and even to _strike women_. How is it that these people enjoy full
liberty for this, whilst their victims are deprived of the protection of
the laws? Is it right that a troop of boys, or a few savages, should
make the laws and form the police of the town? If the authorities are
powerless now to enforce respect for the law in favour of the Salvation
Army, they will in like manner plead that they are powerless to protect
us in our houses from being assaulted and broken into one of these days.
If the police were only to claim the assistance of fifty citizens, we
should soon see whether, in twenty-four hours, all these disturbances
could not be brought to an end. I declare openly, and after all that I
have read and heard against the Salvation Army as it appears at
Neuchâtel, that there exists in my mind only _one thought_ ... and it is
this: Here are Christians such as I have never yet known, who are not
content with words and phrases, but who _practice_ the gospel as in the
times of the primitive Church, and this is the reason why I love them.

 (Signed) “DE PERROT, Colonel.”

Among the various testimonies cited, the following, emanating from a
public-house organ, may be interesting:—

  “These new visitors to our town have from the first aroused the most
  complete and unmistakeable antipathy of our citizens. How could it be
  otherwise? for their conceit, self-sufficiency, &c., are not at all in
  harmony with our simple and laborious life? These enlightened
  preachers do not seem to make a sufficiently careful choice of their
  expressions; for example, they say, ‘the devil is among the
  public-house keepers, the devil is in the whiskey, the devil in the
  wine, &c.’ You can very well understand that preaching like this is
  not at all to our taste. Do they wish us to be plagued with a new
  Phylloxera? They come to tell us that our vineyards are our enemies,
  that the vines must be pulled up! Surely it is already quite enough to
  have two Protestant churches in our midst which hold exactly the same
  dogmas. It has already been rather more than we could bear to have for
  several years past acrimonious conflicts going on upon grounds of
  faith and religious practice. These modern warriors might have done
  better to leave us at peace. They would, in doing so, have better
  followed the advice of Christ, who said: ‘Peace on earth and goodwill
  toward men.’ But now we see what is the end of the work of these
  marplots, these meddlers with our enjoyments, in regard to our city
  which was so quiet before these new preachers came to make a
  revolution among us. Now what is the remedy? When a troop of comic
  actors, mountebanks, or vagabonds of any kind come to a town, and
  trouble the public peace, the authorities—that is to say, the Prefect
  of Police—takes care that they are immediately sent away. This is what
  we demand in the interest of our good and honest population. Let these
  comedians—(with all due regard to their high profession)—be thanked
  for the services they have rendered us, and requested to return to us
  that which they have taken from us—_peace_.”

Previously to the publication of the decree of the 20th June, forbidding
all meetings of the Salvation Army, and, subsequently to a decree of the
22nd May, closing the meetings held on the week days in the evening, M.
Convert, being convinced of the illegality of that decree, continued to
invite the Salvationists to his house, using his freedom and rights of
citizenship as he and his fathers before him had ever done. In
consequence of this, the following correspondence took place between the
head of the police and M. Convert and others:—


 “To Mr. Robert Convert.

  “SIR,—I have the honour to inform you that the Council of State has
  this day ordered me to keep a very strict watch over the carrying out
  of its decree of the 22nd May last, a copy of which has been supplied
  to Mr. Becquet, captain of the Salvation Army, and to bring up to the
  Tribunals all those who shall be found disobeying it. I therefore
  invite you to abstain henceforward from calling together at your house
  any members of the Salvation Army, either Swiss or foreign, _or to
  take part in any meeting of this kind, whether in a private house or
  in public_. Trusting that you will take this warning into your serious
  consideration, I am, Sir, &c.,

 (Signed) “For the Prefect, C. GERSTER.

  “June 16.”

To what an extraordinary state of siege freedom had been reduced, at
this date, in Neuchâtel, when a citizen, a man of high character and
respectability, could be forbidden to take part in a private meeting in
a private house!!

The following is M. Convert’s reply:—


 “To the Prefect of the District of Neuchâtel.

  “SIR,—The undersigned have received the letter which you have done
  them the honour to address to them of the 16th June, in which letter
  occurs the following passage:— ‘I invite you to abstain henceforward
  from calling together at your house any member of the Salvation Army,
  either Swiss or foreigner, or to take part in any meeting of this
  kind, whether in a private house or in public.’ They consider that the
  above invitation is contrary to the declaration of Articles 49, 50,
  and 56 of the Federal Constitution, as well as of the 11th and 13th of
  the Cantonal Constitution, and they protest against this restriction
  imposed upon their Constitutional rights. They take, moreover, the
  liberty, Sir, of begging you to observe that, according to the terms
  of your letter every meeting whatsoever which may have a religious,
  moral, or philanthropic character is rendered impossible, if it is
  made compulsory to prove that no member of the Salvation Army has
  taken part in it.

 “We have the honour to remain, Sir, &c.,
 “CONVERT and NIESTLE.”

Rejoinder to the above:—


 “To Mr. Convert,

  “SIR,—I have received the protest which you have addressed to me this
  day in your name, and in that of Mr. Niestle, against the invitation
  contained in my letter of the 16th. This protest leads me to think
  that I have not clearly explained myself, or else that you wilfully
  fail to understand me. That being so, I will endeavour to be more
  clear. The decree of the Council of State of the 2nd May, to which I
  called your attention, does not, as you appear to think, contemplate
  interference with all meetings whatsoever which have a religious,
  moral, or philanthropic character, but the meetings of the Salvation
  Army, or (in order to be still more explicit) such meetings as those
  which have taken place at the houses of M. Aimé Humbert, M. Coulin,
  and more recently at your own house, Sir, and which have been
  occasions of tumult and disorder, of which you are cognizant. This
  decree is perfectly consistent with Article 50 of the Federal
  Constitution, to which you draw my attention, and which only
  guarantees the free exercise of public worship within limits
  compatible with public order. Now it is incontestible that public
  order has been greatly disturbed on these occasions, and that the
  superior authority has the right, as the Grand Council lately stated,
  to take measures to prevent the renewal of such scandals, and to
  secure that the tranquillity of the town be not imperilled without any
  reason for the same, by a limited number of citizens, however
  honourable they may be, who only look at one side of the question,
  braving public sentiment, and persisting in claiming rights which are
  disputed by the majority of the population. There are circumstances,
  and this is one, in which men should know how to give way, and to wait
  patiently rather than to run counter to public opinion, or to wait
  till time has done its work and modified that public opinion if at
  present it is erroneous. Be so good as to communicate this letter to
  Mr. Niestle.

 “I am, &c.,
 “CORNAZ.”

  “22nd June.”

The three petitions before alluded to, and the report of the Council of
State, of which extracts have been already given, having been considered
