  the armchairs of the Councillors were not in the least shaken.
  (Laughter.) Do not let us have any nonsense about public safety. M.
  Dubs (one of the highest legal authorities) has written: ‘Behind the
  pretence of public safety there is always found a private interest.’
  Let us beware of panics! They tell us the people wished these measures
  to be taken, and that they claimed them in their petition. It is a
  most curious thing indeed that in the decree one finds almost the
  identical terms used as in the petition, and it would seem as if the
  pens which indicted the two documents must be in near relation. Is it
  right that the Government should follow the people in this manner in
  all its caprices? No, the people are themselves bound to obey the
  Constitution. It is not possible that they can annul it by a petition.
  They understood this on another occasion, for in 1873, 10,000
  petitioners demanded that the Ecclesiastical law should be submitted
  to the people. On that occasion people were reminded of the respect
  due to the Constitution, and were told that an imperative mandate
  could not be proclaimed by them, and the petition was rejected. There
  was another way in which the case before us might have been dealt
  with, and that was by revising the Constitution itself, which at
  present seems to be too liberal in tolerating us, and in doing so they
  would have only pandered to the imperial will of the mob. You are men
  of judgment, we have had plenty of proofs of that lately. (Laughter.)
  Why do not you do this? And then you might have taken into your
  counsels the authors of the petition themselves, amongst whom there
  are several advocates. (I regret it for the honour of the Neuchâtel
  bar.) But no, you do not venture to revise the Constitution, and why?
  Because you like sometimes to quote these beautiful inalienable rights
  the existence of which attracts foreigners to our country, and which
  have been so prominently exhibited, framed and glazed, at the Zurich
  Exhibition. Yes, it was necessary to hold to these fine rights of the
  Constitution, and yet to stick to our friends! (Sensation.) Thus they
  try to find a middle course, and in doing so they fall into a quagmire
  and everybody, jury, president, lawyers, accused, everybody in fact
  now plays at hide-and-seek, out and in, amongst the Articles of the
  Constitution. (Laughter.) We are reminded continually that a law even
  if it is unjust, ought to be obeyed. This is a convenient doctrine for
  the Government, who if it could be only sure of our obedience, could,
  by a sudden decree, suspend the Constitution itself. But in doing so
  they are guilty of an aberration which debases the Constitution to a
  lower level than an ordinary law. When two laws are in distinct
  contradiction to one another, one cannot be held guilty for choosing
  one in preference to the other. But on the other hand one is certain
  to be in the right if one has on one’s own side the Constitution,
  which is above the Cantonal law. Every persecution which has taken
  place in the past has pleaded legality in its favour. Every past
  persecution has been based upon texts of law and excellent decrees;
  such texts and decrees as you have allowed to prevail against the
  Constitution, which is itself the source of all law and the source of
  your own authority, you magistrates! In such a case as this, a high
  authority tells us we have the right of legal resistance. Authors are
  agreed in saying that under such circumstances a rebellion, such as M.
  Audeoud of Geneva has raised, is legitimate. Our resistance (that is
  to say, the resistance of the Salvation Army) is passive, limiting
  itself to the continuation of the exercise of the right of which it is
  desired to deprive it. Finally, by the oath which Judges take, they
  are themselves deprived of the power of making an application of an
  unconstitutional law. If you find, therefore, that this law is
  unconstitutional you have no right to apply it.” (The speaker
  concluded by demanding the acquittal of all the accused.)

To this address, of which no adequate idea is given by the above, the
Procureur-Général declined to reply, and the prisoners were each asked
in turn whether they had anything to say in their defence.

Captain Becquet, called upon first, pointed out how the disturbances at
the commencement had been a “got-up affair” by people who had never seen
the Army. As to its _methods_, which the Procureur had talked so much
about, though they had never used a drum or cornet at Neuchâtel, yet he
thought they had as much a right to use _every_ instrument as David had.
He would give them a specimen of old-time religion, and as the
Procureur-Général had quoted largely from a pamphlet, he asked to be
allowed also to make a quotation. He then opened his bible and read the
150th Psalm! Cries of derision were heard from some in the audience
while the Captain read and commented. “The Army had attacked sin and the
devil—_that_ was why it was attacked and ill-used, but nothing in earth
or hell would stop its progress.”

Miss Booth then made her defence. A defence it could hardly be called.
It now seemed as if it was not the Salvationists, but the authorities
who were on the defensive.

(The Procureur-Général had himself remarked in his speech: “What do we
find before us? People who show the slightest sign of repentance?... No!
no! but a handful of people who come here with a coolness, and _aplomb
simply superb_, to tell us that they have done nothing wrong, who
presume to talk to us about ‘law,’ and to declare they are in their
rights and mean to stick to them!” And he was right, for certainly
prisoners at the bar never seemed more at ease nor more calmly
trustful.)

As she rose, calm and self-possessed, and faced the President, none but
her intimate friends understood the wonderful God-given power of spirit
and soul required in that moment to enable Miss Booth to triumph over
the exhaustion and acute suffering of those two long days following
twelve of imprisonment. She said:—

“I have no need to enter upon the legal aspect of this question; that
has been ably dealt with by M. Monnier. Yet as M. le Procureur-Général
himself went off the ground of law to discuss at great length the
doctrines and methods of the Army, I feel I cannot for truth’s sake be
silent; although I am fully aware that we are not here to enter upon a
disquisition about Christianity.

“Permit me first to say that if the Conseil d’Etat had any doubts or
misgivings concerning the methods and end of the Salvation Army, the
honourable members should have sought information from competent judges,
that is to say, from those who know us, who had both seen and heard us,
before accepting the opinions of persons who had done neither one nor
the other.

“What is the need for the Salvation Army? Allow me to read an extract
from one of your own journals:—‘Cantonal Governments, the Federation,
neighbourhoods and families see with alarm the flood of demoralisation
rising menacingly higher and higher; and instead of seeking to destroy
the causes of this deluge, they only take away the remaining dams.’ Can
you deny the existence of the state of things described by this paper?

“It is needless to enlarge upon the necessity for a Salvation Army in
the face of these facts.

“The Procureur-Général has said, in speaking of the work, that it moved
the entire population, and that there must be a cause for this. He has
reason to say so. I agree with him; there is a cause, far deeper than
any that has been mentioned here to-day. It is at this cause we
strike—which exists _in the heart of man_.

“As to our aim, we are trying to bring these people who outrage your
laws, who fight against God, to the feet of Him who alone can change
them, to the only hope that exists for them, the Saviour of the world!
We work, we live, we suffer to do this. This is our one and only
object—to bring the world to the Great Deliverer, Jesus Christ!

“Our only message has been, ‘Repent and turn ye to the Lord that your
sins may be blotted out.’ We want to see the drunkards, the thieves, the
outcasts washed in the precious blood of Jesus, and changed into
peaceful, loyal citizens.

“This being our end, it is to the interest of all Governments to protect
us; and in protecting us they protect themselves.

“In our efforts to attain this end we have met with the bitterest
opposition in Switzerland, because our motives have been wilfully
misrepresented. Lies, as absurd as untrue, have been circulated about
us; and we have been bitterly persecuted—first by those who resist the
light, and secondly by those who have been misled, and will one day
regret their action.

“The great accusation against us is that we disturbed the public peace;
in a word, we were the cause of scandal. If to preach the Gospel, to
denounce sin, and to pray over the masses is a scandal, then we are
guilty of a scandal, even as Christ and Paul were. The Procureur-Général
has attempted to describe in detail our proceedings, yet this gentleman
has avowed that he has never attended a single meeting, never put his
foot inside the Mont Blanc; he cannot, therefore, be considered capable
of judging since he has never seen them! (Laughter.)

“M. le Procureur-Général has said we attacked the town with drums, and
trumpets, and other extravagant things. I ask M. le Procureur-Général
how many drums he has seen in Neuchâtel, or trumpets? Not one. What is
the truth? No musical instrument, with the exception of a piano, has
been employed. No flag, no processions. The only bill that has been
issued I hold in my hand, which simply announces that the Salvation Army
will hold meetings at seven, three, and seven.

“M. le Procureur-Général has referred to the language of the
Salvationists. He says we teach that all that is necessary for Salvation
is for one to jump on the platform and say, ‘_Je suis sauvé_’ (‘I am
saved’) after five minutes’ self-examination. No one in Switzerland has
ever heard us make such an outrageous statement. It is not true.

“Notwithstanding, there may come a day when M. le Procureur-Général and
others would give all the world to be able to say, ‘Je suis sauvé!’ The
time will come when he will have to face the great unknown future,
appear before another tribunal, to face another Judge! Then he with
others will prove the value of those words, ‘Je suis sauvé!’

“But what are our proceedings? (1.) We have sung hymns in the hall that
we had hired. Everyone can procure a little book of the hymns we use,
and judge for themselves. Here is a specimen:—

 ‘Seul refuge de mon âme,
 Je veux m’appuyer sur Toi;
 La paix que mon cœur réclame
 Est à Tes pieds, ô mon Roi!’

(2.) We read the Bible. Switzerland has not waited till the Salvation
Army came to read the Bible. You know this Book already. (3.) We pray,
and (4) we persuade men to leave the road of sin and death—to renounce
evil, and give their hearts and lives to the Saviour. And God is our
witness that we have _done nothing more_.

“Is that a scandal? To sing, and speak, and pray in the name of Jesus?
It is not we that throw stones, that break windows, that howl and hoot
after respectable people in the streets. It is not we who violate
domiciles. It is against all logic to say that _we_ are the scandal of
your country.

“Ah! the question of all questions, the question which every intelligent
man ought to face, is—What are we to do with the masses? If they are not
reached by the power of the Gospel—if they are not won from sin and the
power of Satan—a day will come when they will turn round against you,
occasioning terrible trouble and disorder, and awful will be the
consequences. Then, Messieurs, you may have reason to regret your action
in this matter. If these disturbers are capable of manifesting such
hatred, such rage against citizens who pray to God, they will also be
capable of manifesting the same rebellious spirit against _any other
opinion, or any other law, which may not please them_.

“We have not made your people like this. Bear in mind that we have not
created this terrible state of barbarism which was let loose around that
Hall, and which has made my heart bleed many times in witnessing. Who is
responsible for this? We cannot be, for we have only been in your town a
few months.

“There are people also in Switzerland who have followed our meetings and
know that we are on the side of law and order, and that our only end is
to stop vice, to empty the prisons, and to save souls out of the grasp
of the devil. And it is those who are _interested in, and gain by sin,
who fight against us_!

“Although we have suffered terribly through misrepresentations that have
been wilfully circulated about us, we are not discouraged! we know that
truth and justice will soon triumph. I love Switzerland all the more for
what we have endured. A little while and Switzerland will love us. We
shall win thousands to righteousness, peace, and heaven. (Cries of
assent and dissent.)

“Further, we have broken no law since we entered your country. The
Prefect admitted to me that Capt. Becquet had all along strictly obeyed
his orders and counsels with reference to the Mont Blanc. We have
submitted and submitted again and again, until you have taken away from
us the right to meet to pray. This lands us on another platform
altogether. We have now no more any choice. There is but one course open
to us—we must follow that. We must obey God; we must be in harmony with
the laws of Heaven. We are not here to plead ‘Not guilty.’ No! Like
Daniel and Paul, and the early Christians, we weighed this matter. I
weighed it in my room on my knees. We have all told you that we did
violate the decree. We have no wish to hide it. I note, however, that
the enemy of souls has full liberty. He can do his work without
hindrance. He can lead to perdition thousands of victims. He is
permitted to drink, and shout, and sing, and dance, and make all the
noise he likes without the least interruption on the part of the
authorities, while we are _made prisoners for praying in a wood_! I was
struck the other night when in my prison by the howling and singing of
bands of roughs, which continued till past midnight, without any
interruption that I am aware of from the police, while our people were
followed home because they had sung a hymn, and strict watch was kept
that they should not do it again.

“I fear there is reason to believe that personal feeling and prejudice
have somewhat taken the place of justice in this matter. Permit me,
Messieurs, to say that on the ground we occupy to-day, where liberty and
truth are at stake, all prejudice and personal and vindictive feeling
should be banished; that justice, and justice alone, should have full
and fair play.

“I ask why the meetings were stopped at the Mont Blanc at the moment
when there was no noise? I was in Neuchâtel when the order came from the
Prefect to stop the evening meeting after a certain Friday night, at
which there had been _no disturbance_. The same thing occurred with
reference to the afternoon meeting. Why were the afternoon meetings
closed when there _was no noise_? And again with the Sunday morning
meetings. Neither is the law in execution to-day on account of noise,
since perfect order reigned on the 9th September at the Prise-Imer. The
meeting lasted from two to six, and hundreds of people, including
policemen and the Prefect of Boudry, witnessed the solemnity of that
occasion. And I ask further, is it just to shut up our hall on account
of the noise outside? If a _man took a shop for his business_ and people
chose to break his windows, _would the Conseil d’Etat shut his shop up
to satisfy_ the roughs outside—would they be guilty of such injustice?
Then why has it shut up the hall which we had taken, and which was our
property? They did not even offer to pay the rent. All we demand is that
which is guaranteed by your Constitution—religious liberty.

“M. le Procureur-Général referred to our Queen, saying that even she was
subject to the decrees of Parliament, but that I placed myself above her
in refusing to become subject to the decrees of the Grand Conseil. There
is no parallel between Her Majesty and myself. No Act has been passed to
forbid her praying in a wood, or I think her Majesty would have
something to say on the subject! (Sensation.)

“M. le Procureur-Général has spoken in a way that has astounded me on
public opinion, which, he says, they cannot afford to treat lightly. Let
me remind M. le Procureur-Général and all here that it was public
opinion that crucified Jesus Christ. The rulers and the people all
joined together to put him to death. Do you justify that action? That
was _public opinion_.

“The Government in England protects us. The Queen, John Bright, and
others of the most intelligent and influential men in our country, have
publicly expressed their sympathy. They appreciate and understand the
end and aim of the Army.

“One word in conclusion. You can punish us; you can imprison us; you can
persecute us (as long as you are permitted); but what you cannot do is
to stop this work—to suppress it. I warn you. Beware what you do, for
your country’s sake, for Jesus Christ’s sake; and as you value the peace
of your soul in a dying hour. Take care that in banishing us you don’t
banish the light, that you do not banish Jesus Christ, and in that great
day when you are called to give an account you be found guilty of
fighting against God.

“You can laugh at me; you can mock me; but you cannot deny what I have
said. God is with us!”

M. Convert, being asked if he wished to add anything, said:—

“The Procureur-Général has spoken in unsparing terms of our madness and
fanaticism. I shall not reply to this. History will judge between us.
The great future will show who is right—the Government accuser or the
Salvationist accused.

“When I heard of the coming of the Salvation Army I was extremely
anxious to assume the _true_ attitude towards them, and therefore I
immediately wrote to friends in London and other places who had
opportunity of seeing and studying the work and the workers. I had heard
many strange reports; among others that the work was full of
irreverence; but I wished to have exact information from people who had
seen, and heard, and studied for themselves, and not mere hearsay; and I
am glad of the opportunity of stating publicly that all the information
I thus received was such as to reflect the highest credit upon the Army
and its officers. I have since had the opportunity of seeing them
personally, and my experience has more than confirmed the information
received. I consider it an honour to be here to stand by them to-day,
whilst at the same time protesting as a Swiss and a Neuchâtelois,
against a decree which forces me to stand at this bar as an accused. It
is a post of honour; but I ask why was I chosen for this honour among
two hundred other Salvationists who attended the meeting? The two
hundred would have been happy to have been here at this bar to-day.”

The same question was then asked of Mme. Boillot, who said: “The
Procureur-Général had accused the Army of being a scourge to the
country. I reply, if you want to abolish something, abolish the real
scourges. Shut up the houses of ill-fame which are licensed by the
State, and the public-houses, and then you will have less need of your
prisons and of the Salvation Army!” (Sensation.)

Sergeant Cost then said: “All I have to ask, Mr. President, is that if
you punish our dear English Officers, you will give me the same sentence
as you give them!”

Sergeant Hess asks the same.

The Jury retire, and while the murmur of conversation rises all around,
the word is passed along the line of the accused, and among the
Salvationists in the audience, “Let us all pray!” In that moment of
crisis, from the midst of the excited throng, many prayers are rising to
the Eternal Throne.

During the whole of those two days, unseen and unknown to that earthly
tribunal, an appeal had been unceasingly going up to the Supreme Court
of Justice above, winged by the prayers of multitudes in other lands—

 “And thus the whole round earth
 Was every way bound by golden chains
 About the feet of God!”

After a time the President resumed his chair: “Silence! Messieurs! The
Jury is about to enter. I call upon this audience now to maintain the
attitude of respect due to the dignity of this tribunal. Upon the
slightest disturbance I shall have the hall cleared.”

A moment of breathless suspense followed.

The door opens, and the foreman of the jury advances to the table,
followed by his six colleagues.

With a manner full of courageous dignity, he reads:

_First Question_: Did the accused take part in a meeting?—Yes.

_Second Question_: Was this meeting in violation of the decree?—Yes.

_Third Question_: Did they act with culpable intention?—No.

The Judge pronounces:

“In consequence of this verdict, _the accused are acquitted_.”

Fervent “Amens;” burst spontaneously from the Salvationists in the
audience.

The following remarks on the events of those few days from Mlle. ——, may
be interesting.

  “On Saturday morning a carriage came to fetch Miss Booth and Mr.
  Becquet, who, accompanied by the jailer, drove to Boudry, a little
  country town at five miles distant from Neuchâtel, where the trial was
  to take place. We ourselves left the station at Neuchâtel at 7 o’clock
  in the morning; a great many Salutistes were gathered there, and as
  soon as they were in the train began singing their cheerful hymns of
  victory, and never ceased till the train arrived at Boudry. A crowd of
  Neuchâtel people marched in, and found this quiet little place already
  filled with people. The windows, doors, and streets were filled with
  faces looking eagerly at the anxious throng who flocked towards the
  Hôtel de Ville. It did require strength and patience to stand for two
  hours for the doors to be opened. But the subject was too intensely
  interesting to consider the fatigue or the inconvenience of being
  crushed in the crowd. Meanwhile a few friends gathered together in a
  little upper room in a friendly house opposite, and many fervent
  prayers were offered concerning this most important day. This little
  room became quite a sanctuary during the two days of the trial; many a
  time we saw it filled with earnest Christians, several of the
  _prévenus_ joining us to gather strength from on high. Luther’s hymn,
  “_C’est un rempart que notre Dieu_” was a favourite one, and brought
  comfort to many; we sang, however, also a good many Salvation songs in
  spite of the judges opposite who might have heard us! The jury
  assembled at 9.30 a.m.; then the prisoners were called in. Miss Booth
  entered first; she looked very pale and suffering. Many saw her then
  for the first time, and no doubt many thought she looked more like an
  angel than a criminal! Captain Becquet next entered; he, too, bore on
  his face traces of his hard work, and of the privations of the prison;
  he looked worn and very pale, but as joyful as ever. His peace is
  unalterable; loss of comfort, trials, persecution, difficulties, are
  nothing to him. When I asked him during the day how he was, he
  answered cheerfully: ‘Very well indeed; there is only one thing which
  can make me unhappy, that is _sin_.’ Some hearing this said: ‘One must
  confess these people do not look unhappy.’ It was indeed a very
  striking sight to observe the faces of the six ‘culprits.’ The perfect
  calm and peace which shone on their faces was in itself eloquent
  enough. They seemed quite unconcerned and perfectly at their ease in
  the midst of the crowd of people glaring at them. No nervousness, no
  agitation; they knew that the Lord Himself would speak through their
  lips; yea, that He was in the midst of them—what then could they fear?
  they were not their own, they had acted in obedience to their Master,
  and He had called them to be witnesses for Him before this Court, and
  here they were for His sake. M. Convert sat beside Miss Booth. He is a
  worthy Christian, of mature age, and a faithful follower of Christ.
  His father many years ago had also had to appear before the tribunals
  for his faith, and was sentenced to three days of prison for having
  held a religious meeting. Mme. Boillot is a young lady only 25 years
  old. Although a Christian woman before, she had been greatly blessed
  through the Salvation Army in Neuchâtel, and had been very bold and
  determined in holding meetings at her own house, and attending as many
  others as she could, without neglecting her family duties. Cost is a
  gardener who was converted in one of the meetings at the Mont Blanc
  Hall. He, too, has been a good faithful soldier, holding meetings
  every week at his house without fear. Hess, a good-looking young man,
  carpenter, had been a drunkard for many years, had signed and broken
  the pledge many a time, but only since the Army came to Neuchâtel did
  he obtain sufficient strength and blessing to keep it, and to be a new
  creature. His testimony was very remarkable.

  “So much for the criminals. I will not enter into the details of the
  debates which you will be able to read fully in the papers. A few
  things were particularly striking. The Procureur-Général is a man who
  professes to be a Freethinker, far, far above the narrow-minded people
  who call themselves Christians; yet, in his pleading against the
  accused, his _strongest_ argument was, that almost all the pastors and
  ministers of religion had expressed their feelings and opinions,
  either in the press or pulpit, _against_ the Salvation Army. ‘An
  eminent _Christian_ lady, also a famous authoress, had written such
  revelations against this association that there could be left no doubt
  in the minds of every thoughtful person that this Army was nothing but
  a lot of _Saltambanques_, who, instead of promoting religion, did only
  injure and profane it, &c., &c.’ Then the Procureur quoted several
  pages of Mme. de Gasparin’s brochure. It made everybody shudder, who
  loved the name of Jesus, to hear that blessed name uttered in such a
  tone, quoting the disgraceful passages where Mme. de Gasparin
  represents Jesus as the bitterest judge of the Salvation Army, tearing
  their banners, crushing their platforms, &c., &c. (Awful language.) I
  was ashamed for all our pastors to have to have their names quoted in
  the cause of the unbelievers against the true followers of Christ.

  “The powers of darkness were certainly well represented in this
  tribunal; we could _feel_ the conflict between the two powers very
  strikingly; but, thank God, our faith never faltered, and we knew that
  the Lord was the strongest and that the victory would be His.

  “A few illustrations come to my memory of this fact. At the end of the
  first day a few roughs had climbed on the high brick stove at the end
  of the room, when the President declared that the prisoners would be
  released until Monday, when the debates would be continued. One of
  these men shouted at the top of his voice: ‘Au clou,’ which means in
  slang, ‘To the prison with them.’ At this very moment there was a
  great crash and the man fell down the stove, breaking off a whole
  corner of it. He was himself much hurt by his fall, so that he stopped
  shouting, and the accused could freely leave the hall without
  molestation.

  “Another incident I noticed;—a rough-looking woman in the crowd who
  seemed all rage and anger against the _Maréchale_, swearing and
  threatening to kill her with her pitchfork if she was released; she
  kept following the debates eagerly. To our great surprise we noticed
  that a considerable change took place in her when the _Maréchale_
  spoke; at last her face changed completely—she was quite silent and
  grave looking—tears rolled down her checks, and it was exactly as if a
  tiger had been changed into a lamb. To God be all glory.

  “I must not forget to tell you that very few persons, in fact nobody
  expected the acquittal of the accused. The advocate whom I had
  questioned a few days before said that it would be a _miracle_ if they
  were acquitted, that everything was against them, the fact even of
  having to go to Boudry was most unfavourable—all these country people
  were rough, and opposed to religious questions, and that they were
  determined to put an end to these manifestations, &c. But God
  overruled all! Our advocate suddenly recollected that petition of the
  10,000, and he remembered that the jury which was to judge the
  Salutistes could not be composed of men who had signed this petition;
  this would be enough to make them _unfit_ to judge impartially, having
  expressed their opinion _against_ them in signing the petition. The
  accused are allowed to choose their jury, therefore when the members
  were proposed, the advocate went to the chateau where the
  petition-lists were examined, and there more than half the names were
  found of the proposed jurors, as signatories. The debates of the first
  day bore on these preliminary questions, which ended in the
  composition of a new jury, none of whose members had signed the
  petition, and were in fact all _good_ men. This was God’s hand. Still,
  even on Monday when the question _de fond_ really began to be treated,
  people did not know what the end would be. It was very interesting to
  observe the different faces in the crowd. Some anxiously hoping and
  fearing, others waiting like wild beasts to devour their prey. The
  crowd was much greater than on Saturday, and the air so bad, so
  intensely hot, that it was almost impossible to bear it. Of course,
  the great attraction was to hear the defence of the prisoners, and it
  was a remarkable thing to hear from these lips the confession of the
  name of Christ made without fear. I could not stay the whole time, and
  remained a little outside to get fresh air; a great many big fellows
  were assembled there drinking and using bad language. One
  rough-looking man quite drunk, lifted his hand shouting, ‘If you want
  an executioner, here I am, take me, we don’t want these English; I
  follow the advice of our pastor, we have our pastors!!’ Some others
  went to the Temperance Hall, emptied the chocolate cups on the floor,
  and took out of their pockets bottles of _eau-de-vie_, which they
  began drinking in that place! They said, ‘In these times of trouble,
  nobody knows what poison is in this chocolate, we must drink better
  stuff!!!’ Altogether there was a very uncomfortable presentiment of
  some outbreak in the evening. The suspense was very great. We kept
  believing and praying, but I must confess not without fear. When at
  last, towards 8 o’clock at night, the jury gave the verdict of
  acquittal, and the words ‘_sans intention coupable_,’ were pronounced,
  there was a momentary dead silence like the calm before the storm. M.
  Cornaz, the director of police, then ordered his police to withdraw,
  so the poor accused were at the mercy of the mob. Miss Booth was
  protected by her advocate, M. Monnier, and Professor Kœnig, of Berne,
  and reached her carriage, but the shouts and shrieks were awful, worse
  than those of wild beasts. ‘A l’eau, à l’eau,’ they shouted, and
  followed the carriage _en masse_, trying to upset it. But the coachman
  succeeded in getting out of the crowd; then the roughs attacked the
  other Salvationists who had to walk, pelted them with mud and stones,
  howling and blaspheming, so much so that we could only pray and thank
  God that they were not more hurt. Oh! how we sang in our train coming
  back! such joyful songs of victory; we did praise the Lord, and made a
  joyful noise unto the Lord.”

Great assistance was rendered to the accused by M. Professor Kœnig, of
Berne, a distinguished Swiss lawyer, and legal adviser to the British
Embassy. Dr. Kœnig had had the case before him for some time previously,
had studied it thoroughly, and watched the trial on behalf of our
Foreign Office.

Much indignation was felt throughout Switzerland, and found its
expression also in the English Press, at the conduct of M. Cornaz in
ordering the police to leave at the moment the verdict of acquittal was
pronounced, the moment in which the disappointed roughs might be
expected to break out in vengeance against the acquitted persons.

The _Suisse Libérale_ had the following on the subject:—

“After the conclusion of the trial, the crowd, which disperses slowly,
appears tumultuous enough in the neighbourhood of the building, and
threats are heard against the acquitted. The Brigadier of Gendarmes
approaches M. Cornaz to take his orders for the protection of the
persons whom the jury have just acquitted, and receives this answer,
made in an exasperated tone by the Director of Justice and Police—an
answer with which I remain astounded, but which I give textually:—

“‘_We have nothing more to do; you may retire._’

“The brave gendarme does not understand. He has not even strength to
articulate the usual word, ‘Director, you are right,’ and goes silently
away with his comrades. I leave without comment this little episode to
the reflection of those who still think several things which I do not
think any more at all. Miss Booth, jostled and pressed in the most
painful way by a hostile crowd, gets into a carriage with her advocate
and M. Kœnig. The carriage drives off in the midst of hisses and
hooting. The crowd is very discreet on the whole, as it had just been
given _carte blanche_.”

M. Cornaz’s face was white with passion when he heard the verdict of
acquittal. During Miss Booth’s defence he had stood facing her with “an
_evil grin_” on his face, as one of the papers expressed it, and on one
occasion rudely interrupted her, saying, “Silence her; Shut her mouth!”
He subsequently distinctly and publicly denied having dismissed the
police at the moment when most required for the protection of the
accused. This led to a correspondence in the press. M. du Pasquier
stated that he had distinctly heard the words spoken. M. Kœnig also, and
many others, attested the fact. The astonishment of the honest policeman
under him when ordered to retire was also witnessed by many persons.
Further comment is needless.

In the turbulent rush from the Court-house of Boudry, a fierce-looking
man approached Capt. Becquet, and gave him notice, with a clenched fist,
that “his flesh and blood would pay for it,” if he persevered in holding
meetings.

The other Salutistes were followed by a crowd of roughs along the
country road, and those who brought up the rearguard were savagely
kicked and pelted.

The not unfriendly feeling of the Public Prosecutor towards those
against whom he reluctantly took part may be illustrated by a little
incident. At the close of the trial, Capt. Becquet went up to him, and
held out his hand, saying, “I have no bitterness against _you_, sir.”
The Prosecutor responded heartily; patting Becquet on the back, he said,
“Nor have I against you and your comrades.” Miss Booth and her English
friends left Neuchâtel the same evening for Paris.

On the 3rd of October, certain persons who had formed themselves into a
committee for the organization of a public meeting to demand the
expulsion from the Canton of the English Salutistes, issued the
following address:—

  “FELLOW CITIZENS,—The jury of Boudry has given its verdict. The
  Salvationists are acquitted. As signatories of the anti-Salvationist
  petition you demanded that measures should be taken to put a stop to
  the action of the Salvation Army in your Canton. Your Council of State
  and your Grand Council have granted the desire of the ten thousand
  petitioners. A decree was published forbidding the meetings of the
  Salvation Army upon the whole of the Republican territory of the
  Canton of Neuchâtel. This decree was violated. The popular will has
  not been respected. The decision of the jury of Boudry has rendered
  that decree a dead-letter. Fellow citizens, it is incumbent now on the
  people to demand of the Council of State a measure of Police and
  Public Order which would put an end to the whole scandal, namely, the
  expulsion of the foreign Salutistes. The resolute measure adopted by
  the Constituted Authority upon the petition of ten thousand citizens
  must not be allowed to be trampled under foot. We adjure you,
  therefore, to come together on Sunday, at 3 o’clock in the afternoon,
  on the Place du Gymnase, to discuss publicly the urgent measures which
  are required. We count upon the presence of all citizens who regard
  the dignity of the country and respect our laws.

 “Signed by the Committee of Organization of the Meeting.”

A novel spectacle presented itself at Neuchâtel on that Sunday, _i.e._,
that of a large meeting of people demanding the immediate expulsion of
certain foreigners who had already crossed the Jura, and were some
hundreds of miles away.

A special cheap train service had been organized to tempt people from la
Chaux du Fond and other towns to come. The Committee of organization,
followed by about two hundred citizens, with a military banner and
music, marched to the station to meet those who were to arrive. On
returning, the procession, it is said, numbered about two thousand. At
the head of it marched M. Schmidlin, proprietor of the Café of the
Trocadero, and a veterinary surgeon of Boudry. Several members of the
Grand Council are said to have been present. Having marched round the
town, they halted at the Place du Gymnase.

M. Gehrig addressed the meeting, and enjoined the order suitable for a
Republican assembly worthy of the name. He thanked the citizens who had
replied to the appeal of the committee, for having come together to
demand respect for the rights of the Government, and for the will of the
majority of the people. They could not, he said, be satisfied with the
position in which the verdict of Boudry had left them. They demanded
expulsion—(prolonged cheers)—the immediate expulsion—(renewed cheers)—of
the foreigners who had brought disturbance amongst them.

M. Max Diacon, advocate, then spoke:—That was the first time, he said,
in the history of their country that such an immense concourse of people
had assembled to demand that the Government should enforce respect for
the law. The meeting had assembled to claim from the Council of State a
measure which would restore peace to their country. That day, which
would be a marked one in the history of the country, would, he was sure,
pass off with that order and dignity from which their people had never
swerved. They could do there what could not be done under a monarchy,
namely, demand through the voice of the whole people, not violence, but
justice. (Cries of “Yes, yes, it is true.”) He could not say all that
was on his heart, but he must reply to the accusations which had been
brought against the 10,000 petitioners. They had been reproached for
giving too much importance to a few English men and women. But the
question was not that. The motive which urged them was the sentiment
that in a Republic the laws must needs be respected.

M. Cochard then spoke:—The question, he said, was whether the people
should continue to be masters or not. The Salvation Army, by planting
its flag in their midst, had stirred up the indignation of the people.
Those mountebanks—(vociferous cheers)—trampling on all religious
principles, had tried to introduce their new religion into their
country. But for themselves, they had their own pastors, their beloved
and respected pastors. They did not need any comedy-players to teach
them, and they repudiated the Salvation Army, whose grand principle was
“Pay down money, and you shall be saved.” The decree of the Grand
Council had been violated by the decision of the jury. They (the
meeting) had a right to expel those who had brought disunion into the
families of the people. It could not be allowed that a few English
people should violate their laws under the pretext that they had
consulted Swiss advocates. They had held a meeting in violation of the
decree, and this they did (he blushed for the Canton of Neuchâtel) under
the auspices of a Neuchâtel citizen. It was said that these English were
already gone; but, he said, they might come back again! (The speaker
concluded by reading a resolution as follows:) “The Council of State of
Neuchâtel is respectfully requested to satisfy the wish of the people
expressed in the popular assembly of the 7th October, and to decree the
immediate expulsion from our Canton by a measure of police and public
order, of the foreign Salvationists, who have been the cause of scandals
among us, and who have permanently troubled the public peace.

“This resolution shall be conveyed to the Council of State at the Castle
by a deputation from the meeting. All citizens are invited to accompany
the deputation in procession.”

About seven hundred men then ascended the hill to the Chateau, followed
by a crowd of spectators. The gate of the Court was closed. The terrace
of the College was crowded with spectators. When the procession reached
its destination the gate was opened, and then the crowd poured into the
narrow passage with a great rush. Women groaned, children screamed, and
at last the Court was filled.

A correspondent of the _Suisse Libérale_ wrote:—“When I had at last
entered the Court with great difficulty, having just missed the harangue
of the speaker who presented the resolution to the Government, M.
Comtesse, President of the Council of State had begun to speak, and was
complimenting the people. The people of Neuchâtel, he said, were the
friends of order. (Cheers.) They did not wish to be troubled in their
family life by foreigners who came to embitter them in the name of a
religion which they perverted and dishonoured. (Cheers.) They (the
Swiss) had always shown hospitality to foreigners who came peaceably to
enjoy their institutions; but those who came to speculate on their
hospitality and resist their laws they would drive out of their country.
The Council of State would show that it knew how to do its duty, and to
enforce respect for the popular will. It was well that that dignified
demonstration had been organized, in order that they who had calumniated
the country by predicting a disorderly meeting that day would have the
lie given to them. They were a people jealous of good order, and they
did not require any lessons from foreigners, and still less from haughty
advocates of their own country. (Prolonged cheers.)”

M. Cornaz, Chief of the Department of Justice and Police, then addressed
the people. He complained of having been calumniated in the matter of
his having withdrawn police protection from the accused at the trial.
His concluding words were, “This day it has been the people who have
spoken. The powerful breath of the people has dissipated all these
infamies and calumnies. Let us give three cheers for the Neuchâtel
Republic.”

M. Diacon said he would propose, in that Castle which their ancestors
had re-conquered on two occasions, one more cheer for the Neuchâtel
Republic and Government.

The procession then returned to the town, where speeches were again made
on the Place du Gymnase.

  A Zurich newspaper remarked: “The committee for the formation of this
  meeting was composed of an obscure post-office official and a couple
  of young lawyers strongly attached to the chief of the Department of
  Justice and Police (M. Cornaz). The head of the procession, it is
  said, was formed by a band of good-for-nothings, such as those who
  distinguished themselves in Geneva in the persecutions of the
  Salvation Army. M. Cornaz, surrounded by his friends and brethren,
  stood upon a balcony, in a position to be well seen by the people. On
  the two occasions when the procession was obliged to pass along the
  Rue du Chateau, in front of the house of M. Aimé Humbert, the grossest
  abuse was showered upon its occupant by the roughs in passing.”

  La _Nouvelle Gazette_ said: “This meeting has been pronounced a
  success. To us it does not appear so. It was an occasion for Sunday
  excursionists to amuse themselves, and the manifestations were
  absolutely wanting in everything like seriousness and dignity.”

A correspondent from Switzerland to the _Globe_ (London), wrote as
follows of the address made on the occasion of the popular meetings:—

  “Especially telling was the hit directed against the Salvationists on
  the ground that they were mountebanks, who were seeking to get money
  out of the pockets of the people. To the average Helvetian mind, this
  charge is quite sufficient to justify their expulsion. Never had the
  well-worn proverb—‘Point d’argent, point de Suisse’—a fuller
  illustration. In trying to get hold of some Swiss money, the Booths
  have committed an unpardonable sin. The ingenuous inhabitants of these
  valleys and mountains have so long been accustomed to relieve the
  English traveller of his gold, that they bitterly resent the
  introduction of the converse process....

  “Amid vociferous cries of ‘Au Chateau,’ it was announced that the
  _cortège_ would be reformed and would proceed to the Castle—the seat
  of the Cantonal Government—for the presentation of the demand.
  Hurrying there before the crowd, I secured a place in the inner court,
  and was presently witness to a scene which, _magna componere parvis_,
  would be paralleled if Mr. Gladstone and Mr. Chamberlain came out into
  Palace Yard on a Sunday evening to address congratulatory speeches to
  the leaders of a mob-meeting which had marched from Hyde Park to
  Westminster Palace. When the orators and the bands of music arrived
  they were warmly received—evidently in accordance with a
  pre-arrangement—by MM. Comtesse and Cornaz, leading Radical members of
  the Council of State. These distinguished gentlemen told the crowd
  that they had done perfectly right in organising a noisy demonstration
  in order to try to upset the decision of a properly constituted court
  of justice. There was much said by these responsible administrators
  about the necessity of making all foreigners as well as Neuchâtelois
  obey the law, which in this case, be it remembered, is an edict of the
  Council, which a court of justice has declared to be in opposition to
  the law of the land.”

The following circular to the Prefects of Police was issued by M. Cornaz
on the 10th October.

  “GENTLEMEN,—Now that we have expelled Miss Booth and Mr. Edmund
  Becquet, there is no longer, properly speaking, any Salvation Army in
  the Canton of Neuchâtel. This corporation has failed to strike any
  firm roots amongst our people. It was an exotic importation, and seems
  to me to be fated to disappear, together with those who introduced it.
  Any foreigner in Switzerland who may attempt to revive its existence
  will come under the decree of expulsion of the 8th October. This
  measure must be sternly enforced in regard to such persons. In order
  then that a religious assembly held by people of Swiss nationality
  should come within the application of the decree of 15th June, 1883,
  it must advertise openly and as in defiance, the name and exterior
  signs of the Salvation Army. Now, therefore, we have re-entered our
  normal state. The Canton of Neuchâtel is the country of religious
  liberty, where everyone possesses the right freely to carry out his
  own form of worship, and to unite with others under the protection of
  the Constitutional, Cantonal, and Federal guarantees. All that has
  recently passed in relation to the Salvation Army testifies to the
  serious interest which our people take in spiritual questions. In
  demanding its expulsion the Neuchâtel people have not manifested any
  intolerance toward any religious opinion, seeing that the Salvation
  Army, in its dogmas, does not essentially differ from the Protestant
  sects which enjoy among us complete liberty. But public sentiment was
  shocked by the jugglery and shows which the Salutistes mix up with
  religion.[37] An irresistible current of opposition arose amongst the
  population. There is every reason to hope that the spirit of justice,
  of moderation and liberty which distinguishes our people, and that the
  most complete liberty will continue to be respected, as it has ever
  been since 1848. Liberty of conscience is a sacred thing in the
  Republic, and the opinions of a minority have the same title to
  respect as those of a majority. The public authorities rely on the
  good will of all the citizens in carrying out this respect. So far
  from interfering in any fashion whatever with meetings which are not
  directly interdicted by the late decree of the Grand Council, our duty
  is, on the contrary, to defend them against all attacks, as certainly
  the Grand Council and the Council of State desire should be the case.

  “Accept, gentlemen, the assurance of my distinguished consideration,
  in the name of the Department of Police.

 “CORNAZ.”

There appears in this address to his subordinates a rising fear in the
mind of M. Cornaz, lest he and his adherents should be on the verge of
becoming the minority whose rights he now calls upon the police to
respect. Possibly he also fears that his subordinates, (often, probably,
men of greater dignity of character than himself), might be too ready to
imitate the imprudence (to call it by a very gentle word) of which he
himself was guilty at the close of the Boudry trial, and that they might
carry out once too often in their official acts the principle of
non-interference in scenes of violence which may take place in future.

The _Continental Times_ of the 20th October contained the following
observations on M. Cornaz’s circular to the Police:—

  “Having exercised the law in an arbitrary manner and expelled two
  inoffensive English subjects from a country that is remarkable for
  harbouring insurgents of all nations, the Neuchâtel Government applies
  a salve to its conscience by publishing the above letter, signed by
  Councillor of State Cornaz, and addressed to the Prefects of the
  Canton:—It would be difficult to find a circular more abounding in
  lame excuses, in unreasonable reasons, in poor attempts to explain
  away tyranny and hide a cowardly subservience to the compulsion of a
  party that objects to any religious body which seeks to empty cafés
  and brasseries, or of one which accustomed to live on the money
  brought into Switzerland by foreigners, has a quaking in the pockets
  at the idea of subscriptions being made among the Swiss by foreigners
  who may carry the proceeds away with them. No! M. Cornaz, your Canton
  has not returned to its normal state. Such oppressive acts as have
  been performed in Neuchâtel leave their mark on men’s minds. The
  attempt to excuse a tyrannical decree while boasting of religious
  liberty, is a sign of weakness, and the day is not far distant when
  the same party that urged you to the expulsion of Miss Booth and Mr.
  Becquet may demand of you acts more difficult to condone than the
  persecution of a few helpless members of the Salvation Army.”

In the _Courrier de Neuchâtel_, which published this circular of M.
Cornaz, there stood, rather strikingly, side by side, in the next
column, the following earnest appeal from certain Christian men of the
Canton of Neuchâtel, following immediately upon the departure of the
acquitted Salvationists:—

  “DEAR FELLOW CITIZENS,—Not being able to present ourselves personally
  to give you this invitation, we do it by means of this advertisement.
  On Sunday, the 14th October, at eight o’clock in the morning, there
  will be a meeting to which we invite all persons who love the truth.
  At this meeting the subject treated will be that of the slavery to sin
  which is eating into the heart of our dear Helvetia. We shall treat of
  the youth of our country, which is given up, bound hand and foot, to
  prostitution, a vice which is practised in our country on such an
  enormous scale. We shall take into consideration the ways and means
  which may be employed in order to prevent the hope of our country from
  being polluted and corrupted throughout, soul and body. Above all will
  be brought forward the one efficacious remedy, through which, thanks
  be to God, already many among us have been saved. It is in the name of
  Jesus that we invite you. Come in great numbers—fathers and young
  men.”

 CHAPTER VIII.


The _Times_ of September 26th, 1883, remarks, concerning the scene which
I am about to describe: “There is a curious old-world look in a telegram
which we publish this morning from Geneva, relating how a citizen of
that Canton has braved the authorities in vindicating the principle of
religious liberty.”

M. Audeoud, one of those citizens of Geneva who hold fast all the good
traditions of liberty and of justice, has played a prominent part in the
late conflict, and will probably continue to do so; for the present
history of the events I have been describing must close shortly after
his challenge has been given to the illegal decrees of the Geneva
Government, and before the record can reach us of the succeeding acts of
the drama which will have to be played out. On Sunday, 23rd September, a
large meeting of Salvationists and others was held in the house of M.
Audeoud, who has a country residence about two miles from Geneva, with
extensive wooded grounds. A report had been current during the previous
week that such a meeting would take place; it had, indeed, been
announced at several private meetings of the Salutistes. The police in
consequence made their plans. At half-past two in the afternoon a number
of Salutistes were seen gathering in the grounds at Conches, or pursuing
the road from Florissant in that direction. There were many young men
among them, most of them having the “S.” on their collars. The
drawing-room and dining-room on the ground floor of M. Audeoud’s house
were occupied by this assembly, which, however, afterward became so
numerous as to form an audience also in the grounds immediately around
the house.

The proceedings had scarcely begun when the Mayor of Chêne Bougeries, M.
Aubert (the same who had arrested the Salutistes at the cemetery, at the
funeral of Wyssa), arrived with a company of his agents at the house of
M. Audeoud. The latter, with his wife, Mme. Audeoud, were in the
drawing-room. The Mayor presented himself, and explained to the master
of the house the motive of his visit, demanding, in a judicial manner,
that the meeting should be dissolved. M. Audeoud persisted in declaring
his intention to show hospitality to the members of the Salvation Army.
A long discussion ensued. M. Audeoud then opened the Penal Code, which
he held in his hand, and read before the Mayor and his attendants
several of the articles concerning religious liberty and freedom of
meeting. The only reply which the Mayor made was to open and display a
placard, bearing the decree of the Council of State, of the 2nd February
of the same year, forbidding the Salutistes’ meetings. He then asked M.
Audeoud if this was a public meeting, and received the following
reply:—“I shall admit to this meeting all who present themselves with a
proper motive, while I maintain my right to dismiss from this meeting
all who come with the purpose of disturbing our proceedings.” Upon this,
the Mayor and his companions retired. During the hour which followed the
attendance increased greatly, and now numbered about three hundred
persons. At three o’clock, while they were on their knees in prayer,
hasty footsteps were again heard upon the gravel walk outside; the Mayor
and his attendant again presented themselves, accompanied by three
gendarmes, an agent of the rural police, and an agent _de la Sûreté_.

M. Audeoud now stood surrounded by several friends, prominent citizens
of Geneva, MM. Alfred and Edmund Pictet, jurists, M. Lenoir, banker, &c.
The Salvationists did not cease their singing, but continued the joyous
chorus of one of their hymns—

 “_Les Salutistes sont toujours en fête._”

The Mayor again addressed M. Audeoud, and said: “M. Audeoud, you are
aware why I have come here. I have already told you my object an hour
ago. I come to dissolve this meeting.” In a voice much more calm, and
quite as firm, M. Audeoud replied: “Sir, in the name of the three
following rights, I forbid you to dissolve this meeting: The right of
freedom of assembly; of liberty of worship; and of the inviolability of
domicile.” Standing before that large and quiet concourse of people,
animated by one steady principle and fixed purpose, M. Audeoud, taking
off his hat in token of respect for the law, again opened the Penal
Code, and slowly and clearly read out to the magisterial and police
forces before him as follows: “Article 108 of the Penal Code. Every
magistrate, administrative or judiciary, or agent, or officer of justice
or of police, every agent of the public police force who, acting in his
official character, shall enter the house of any citizen against the
will of him who occupies it, except in cases clearly defined by the law,
and without all the formalities prescribed by the law, shall be punished
by imprisonment of from eight days to six months, and by a fine of from
thirty to three hundred francs.” He also read Article 107: “Whosoever by
threats or violence shall in any way attack the freedom of worship and
freedom of teaching or the liberty of the press or of meeting, shall be
punished with the same penalty.” “Therefore,” said M. Audeoud, looking
at the Mayor, “I request you to retire.”

The Mayor then read aloud the decree of the Council of State of the 2nd
February, which suspended the exercises of the Salvation Army, adding:
“In pursuance of this decree I must dissolve this meeting, which is a
public meeting as you yourself have allowed.”

M. Audeoud: “Pardon me, I did not allow that. I am free to admit to my
house all persons who wish to take part in this meeting, but I reserve
the right of refusing entrance to the meeting to those who come here
with the manifest intention of interrupting it.”

The Marshall attending the Mayor: “This a public meeting.”

M. Audeoud: “I forbid you to go a step further!” So saying, M. Audeoud
placed himself resolutely in front of the Mayor to prevent his further
entrance, and added, “I call these gentlemen around to witness. I have a
legal case against you.”

The Mayor: “Under these circumstances I will force my way in.”

M. Audeoud: “You mean to push me aside?”

A policeman: “Yes, we shall force an entrance.”

M. Audeoud: “Then you will have to walk over my person.”

The Mayor, who had taken off his scarf, his badge of office, waved it so
as to display it to the citizens assembled, and said “With this can I
not pass?”

At this moment Madame Audeoud came to the side of her husband, the
brothers Pictet and M. Lenoir being also around him, filling the
entrance. The Mayor, however, pushed his way through the group, followed
by his chief agent, and made an attempt to approach the door of the
salon whence the sounds of prayer were proceeding. M. Audeoud and his
wife, however, slipped past them and reached the door before them,
stationing themselves upon the threshold. M. Audeoud said in a loud
voice, “You will not proceed a step further; I am master of my own
house!”

The Mayor confronting M. Audeoud shouted with a loud voice which could
be heard by all present: “In the name of the decree of the Council of
State I declare this meeting dissolved.”

It was a remarkable scene. At the moment when the Mayor with agitated
countenance called out these words looking over the shoulder of M.
Audeoud, the assembled worshippers, led by a young man of Neuchâtel,
were singing one of the Salutiste’s hymns:—

 “_Les Salutistes sont toujours vainqueurs._”

Their voices sounded in joyous and harmonious accord, contrasting
strangely with the harsh, troubled tones of the Mayor. Several could be
heard praying aloud, asking the presence of God in the midst of this
strange scene, and expressing confidence in Him.

When the Mayor had made his proclamation sufficiently heard, M. Audeoud
replied firmly: “I resist your authority” and then the whole police
force retired. Some of those present believed they heard the Mayor say
to the agents as they left the garden, “We are not sufficiently
numerous, we must return in force.”

In dealing with this strange scene the _Times_ again remarks:—

  “The Mayor, who entered that gentleman’s house, seems to have been
  disconcerted when M. Audeoud told him that he was liable to be sued in
  damages for having violated the domicile of a Swiss citizen; and the
  scene was rendered additionally impressive by M. Audeoud removing his
  hat in token of respect for the law, as he read the articles of the
  Constitution which guarantee to every citizen liberty of conscience.
  Geneva is the last place in which one could have expected to hear of
  such an episode as this; and it must be hoped that now the question of
  religious liberty has been mooted, the Swiss will seriously reconsider
  the position which they have taken up as regards the Salvationists.”

The Mayor having retired, the meeting continued as if nothing
extraordinary had happened. Until half-past five in the evening there
was a succession of addresses, testimonies, prayers and hymns. Great
calmness and a feeling of confidence prevailed. Not one word was heard
against the public authorities. On the contrary, in their prayers
petitions were addressed to God for a blessing upon the police, the
Council of State, the Mayor, &c.

The only consequence which immediately followed was a proclamation
against M. Audeoud for non-compliance with the decree of 2nd February.

During a former meeting at his own house, M. Audeoud discovered a member
of the police force lying upon the ground behind a tree in a copse, at a
point from which he could watch the house and all who approached it.
When discovered he pretended to awake out of a heavy sleep, but M.
Audeoud recognised him, and seeing that, the spy ran off, scrambling
rather ignominiously through a hedge, and joined the rest of the “armed
force,” who were waiting outside the grounds, ready to come down upon
any meeting suspected of containing Salutistes.

The Evangelical Alliance of Switzerland having been awakened, though
somewhat tardily, to the full significance of the attacks made in
Switzerland on religious liberty, addressed an appeal to the Swiss
people, from which the following are extracts:—

  “DEAR FELLOW-CITIZENS,—For some time past events have been passing in
  Switzerland which we cannot but regard with much regret. Religious
  meetings have been violently disturbed. Those who attended them,
  sometimes defenceless women, have been the butt of insults and even of
  personal assaults. Private houses, the refuge of every citizen, have
  not always been respected. The occasion of all these outbreaks of
  violence has been, as all are aware, the arrival of the Salvation Army
  in Switzerland. We can understand that its methods of action displease
  some, but this is no justification whatever of persecution in a free
  country which glories in its liberty. We are convinced that the great
  majority of the Swiss people deprecate persecution as only worthy of
  an age of gross ignorance and brutality. It is with confidence
  therefore that we address you, free citizens, counselling you to hold
  firmly the religious liberty which the Federal Council has so
  distinctly guaranteed. Claim it for yourselves, claim it for
  foreigners, claim it for all.... Has not our country been a refuge
  where those persecuted for their religion or for their politics have
  found safety? Have we not opened our doors to thousands of fugitives?
  Let us recall our past history. It is because our fathers have so
  freely opened their doors to fugitives to our land, that Switzerland
  has received the blessing of God, and the esteem and gratitude of
  other nations.... Many times already the Evangelical Alliance, whom we
  have the honour to represent, has raised its voice in favour of
  religious liberty. In Spain, in Italy, in Sweden, in Russia, in
  Bohemia, &c., it has intervened in favour of unprotected men
  persecuted for their religious convictions. It cannot, therefore, be
  silent when in Switzerland itself religious liberty is threatened. It
  does not, however, desire to instigate the general intervention of
  this vast association; but we esteem it is the imperious duty of the
  Swiss Branch of the Alliance to protest solemnly against this
  violation of the most sacred rights; and that which binds us to such a
  course of action is not only the obligation imposed on every free
  citizen to watch with jealous care that no one lays hands on his
  liberty, but it is still more the higher law, which is binding on
  every Christian, that law of grace and love which Jesus Christ came to
  bring into the world, and for which he shed his blood on the cross. We
  earnestly deprecate, therefore, all violence, and every attempt to
  extinguish by brute force the liberty of those who think differently
  from ourselves. We may strive against error, but only with a spiritual
  arm. We must respect all religious convictions so long as they do not
  offend against morality or the law.

  “Freedom of conscience is one of the noblest acquirements of
  civilization, and the nation which allows it to be curtailed is in the
  position of seeming to doubt the power of truth. Let us arouse
  ourselves to the work as fellow citizens; let us unite our efforts in
  order that in every part of Switzerland religious liberty shall be
  respected and enjoyed by all and for all without distinction.

  “May God continue to protect our beloved country, and preserve to us
  the most precious of gifts—justice and liberty.

 “Signed for the Swiss branch of the Evangelical Alliance.”

According to the usual custom, the authors of this appeal prepared to
placard it in the conspicuous places used for advertisements, having
only to go through the formality of giving notice of the same to the
Department of Police. To their great astonishment they received from M.
Héridier the following message forbidding them to publish their appeal:
“You attack us in it. The public pillars (advertising stations) are
ours; we cannot grant them to you in order to attack us.”

About the same date the following petition was addressed to the Council
of State of the Canton of Geneva:—

  “We, the undersigned, all Genevese or citizens of other Swiss Cantons,
  electors, and domiciled in the Canton of Geneva, come before you
  respectfully to submit the following:

  “Possessed of our right to the free exercise of our judgment, ... we
  have become members or adherents of the Salvation Army, whose doctrine
  and practices have appeared to us conformable to the truth, and
  responding to the aspirations of our souls. We possess, consequently,
  the right of following their methods of worship, and of uniting
  ourselves together toward this end, without being hindered by any
  restriction beyond that which can be legally imposed on the exterior
  exercises of our worship. The State owes us not only tolerance, but
  protection. This obligation is formally expressed in our
  Constitutional Law of the 26th August, 1868, Article 2.

  “Individual liberty is guaranteed to us as it is to all other citizens
  by Article 2 of the Constitution, and that liberty has always been
  considered amongst us to carry with it the right of association, and
  consequently of meeting.

  “All Genevese are equal before the law, as proclaimed by Article 2 of
  our Constitution. We come to claim this equality in practice, and we
  refuse to admit that the right of meeting and of association, for
  every kind of object which is not illegal, can be limited in what
  specially concerns ourselves.

  “Since the time when we began to follow the religious assemblies of
  the Salvation Army up to the present moment, we have been disturbed,
  and hunted from place to place, by enemies whom we need not specify,
  and even by the agents of authority themselves. Such a fact can only
  arise from a false application of your decree of the 2nd February
  last. It is impossible that this decree could take upon itself to
  deprive citizens of their constitutional rights; nevertheless, since
  that time we are not allowed to unite, are constantly disturbed, and
  our constitutional rights violated in the most serious manner.

  “Under these circumstances, we, the free citizens of a free country,
  address ourselves to the Government of our country. We now have
  recourse to you gentlemen, praying you publicly to recognise that we
  ought not to be deprived of the rights guaranteed to every citizen,
  and in consequence to take such measures as may be needful in order to
  assure to us the free exercise of these rights, and in particular to
  cause to be removed every prohibition against uniting to worship
  together, and for that end to make use of buildings and places which
  may appertain to us or be placed at our disposal.

  “Whatever you do, do justice.

  “Geneva, 4th October, 1882.”

 (Here follows a long list of signatures of citizens of Geneva.)

Many persons of Geneva and other parts of Switzerland had waited in
anxious suspense for the reply of the Genevese Government to this
appeal, and also for its appreciation of the address of the Evangelical
Alliance.

The _Journal de Genève_ remarked:—“If we may judge by the past, the
Government will give no reply whatever, and the petitioners will be
forced to carry their appeal to the Federal Tribunal. The right of
meeting is now in Geneva nothing more than a favour to be accorded on
the permission of M. Héridier, if it pleases him to grant it, as was
done in France under the Second Empire.”

The same journal, of a later date, speaks of certain ideas opposed to
Constitutional principles, borrowed from Imperial France by M. Héridier,
which are gradually accumulating in Switzerland. “The protest of the
Evangelical Alliance,” it goes on to say, “is drawn up with great
firmness, but also with great moderation, and contains not a single word
which could be taken exception to. It is purely defensive; the
Governments of Geneva and Neuchâtel are not even mentioned by name. It
is simply a question of principles, and an appeal to citizens to uphold
those principles.”... “The authors of the appeal of the Evangelical
Alliance never doubted for a moment that they would be able, according
to custom, to placard their bills on the walls. To their extreme
astonishment, the Chief of the Department, after having perused the
document, refused the authorization desired; and yet this was not the
case of an anonymous placard, but one bearing some of the best known and
most honourable names of the City of Geneva, among whom M. Héridier
might have observed that of a member of the National Council. It
contained nothing insulting, no attack on any one at home or abroad,
nothing whatever to cause its suppression in this manner, M. Héridier,
however, gave as his reason for its quasi-suppression, ‘You attack us in
it, and as the public advertising stations _belong to us_, we do not
permit you to make use of them in order to attack us.’”...

“‘The public advertising stations belong to us!’ says M. Héridier. Who,
then, is this ‘us’ who take upon themselves to monopolise in their own
interests the walls of houses which abut on the streets by calling them
public advertising stations? Is it the Council of State, the police, or
the entire _radicale autoritaire_ party which so acts? Is it not simply
M. Héridier himself?”... “The right to discuss the public acts of the
Government by means of placards has been long enjoyed by the citizens of
Geneva. The only right the police has to suppress such is when they
contain slanders against private persons, or threats of public
disturbance or violence; but never in any other case has this right of
public criticism been denied. This, then, is a fresh step in the
downward path of arbitrary Government.” “We are now at the summit (says
another Geneva paper) of this frightful edifice in the law of terrorism,
which calls upon the tribunals to punish those who desire to be a little
more free than it suits his Majesty M. Héridier to allow them to be.
Certainly there is in all this a grotesque side which might excite
laughter; but it is we who have to pay the price of this hilarity, we
who have to submit to the humiliation of being ruled over by a chief of
police who does not even understand his own business. It is too grave a
matter for laughter.”[38]

Evidence has been given in the course of this narrative of the revived
faith and zeal of many individuals and of some of the churches of French
Switzerland during these troubled times. M. Vernier, a French pastor,
visited Neuchâtel subsequently to the division which took place in the
_Eglise Libre_, in the Place d’Armes, and on the invitation of members
of that Church, filled for a time the place of the minister who had
resigned his office as its pastor. M. Vernier wrote the following from
Neuchâtel to a friend in Drôme, the Editor of _La Chambre Haute_:—

“DEAR BROTHER,—A letter in your last number depicts the future of the
evangelisation of our dear Drôme in rather dark colours. It seems to
your correspondent that the work of revival would be compromised, if not
entirely ruined, by the appearance among us of the Salvation Army. Allow
me to express here an entirely different opinion. I have been present in
several religious revivals in Switzerland, but none has seemed to me
more thorough and more likely to be durable than the revival which we
now see. Three precious elements are prominent in it—the joy of pardon,
an ardent thirst for holiness and constant communion with Christ, and a
fervent desire to bring perishing souls to Him. I wish you could have
been present when more than two hundred Christians of our neighbourhood
were re-baptized by the Holy Spirit, their faces illuminated by the Sun
of Righteousness, their hearts filled with love to God and man. It would
have shown you that we have no reason to give way to fears and
discouragements; but on the contrary, that we have a thousand reasons to
praise God. From my heart I pity those who cannot see in the present
movement an accomplishment of the prophesy of Joel, which we are told
will be fulfilled in the last days. Our theologians may dispute other
texts of the Word of God, but it is impossible for them to annihilate
this. This promise is now being realised, and we ourselves are witnesses
of it. And this is just the moment, unhappily, when several amongst us
who are regarded as servants of God have chosen to protest, in season
and out of season, against the ministry of women, discouraging to some
extent the zeal of God’s servants, and alienating the sympathies of
others who might have gone forward in their ranks. In my humble opinion
it is neither the work of revival nor of evangelisation which is
imperilled—for the Lord has given the word, and great is now the company
of the preachers—but that which I think are imperilled are our
organisations, our forms, our official rules. The Church of God, that
company of faithful people, has never had before it so glorious a
future. The true Church does not shut its doors to the servants and
handmaids of the Lord, nor command silence to the children who shout
Hosannas. On the contrary, it says, ‘Come, ye blessed of the Lord of
Hosts, let us rejoice together, for our Redeemer cometh to judge the
earth, and to be glorified in his saints.’”[39]

In order justly to appreciate the present condition of Switzerland, and
the difficulties which the Constitutional party there may have to
encounter in the future, we must look a little below the surface of the
social life of that country. An intelligent inhabitant of Eastern
Switzerland said to me last summer, “the old Swiss stock is dying out,
or rather it is being thrust out by the perpetual and increasing influx
of foreigners pressing in on every side. What is happening to us is in
fact what happens when a country is overrun by an invading and
conquering army, the process only being much slower. We are being
invaded and gradually superceded by an army of foreigners perpetually
immigrating towards this centre. The old traditions are passing away;
the old stock is less pure, or dying out, and if it continues in this
way we shall soon have no nationality of our own.” At Basle the railway
station is periodically crowded with Germans entering Switzerland. Many
Germans leave home through their dislike of the conscription, and on
account of the present poverty-stricken state of a portion of the
population resulting from the dominant influence of the military spirit
since the Franco-German war, an influence opposed to industrial
prosperity and to liberty. From the southern side of the Alps there
continues to arrive an unceasing stream of indigent Italians, who form a
considerable proportion of the working class in the departments of
terrace and bridge builders, road repairers, etc. France, Alsace,
Bavaria, and Austrian Hungary contribute also their stragglers, for the
most part leaving their country from reasons of discontent, ill-success,
or even worse. A calculation has been made with regard to the Canton of
Neuchâtel which shows that if the present inundation of foreigners
continues for seven years longer at the rate at which it is at present
going on, the result will be that in the year 1890, only one-third of
the whole population will be natives of the Canton. This state of things
must enlist our sympathy and respect for that band of true Swiss leaders
of thought and of action, who see with sorrow the good traditions of
their country passing into oblivion. The men of 1848, trained to a real
strength and nobility of character by the severe discipline of the
troubled times which preceded that epoch, are dying out, and there
remains now little that is homogeneous amongst the people whom we call
Swiss. There is, in fact, little to which to appeal, for those who
desire to train the better instincts of the masses. For such a
conglomeration of international elements, (and by no means always the
best elements of the different nations) there is no national tradition
possible, no common principle of action, no past to point to, and, in
fact, no knowledge for the most part of the great principles which
formerly inspired and guided the men who made the country what it was.
It is a problem of great difficulty, and not without danger, which the
present generation of the true Swiss have to solve.

That Switzerland has been for some years past socially and politically
sick, to a dangerous degree, has been well-known to those who have
watched carefully the signs of the times. What is the meaning of the
fact of the frequent suicides, the record of which has startled us in
recent years, amongst those called to offices of responsibility? Does it
arise from a sense of their own unworthiness to fill such offices, or of
a certain despair in encountering the conflict which conscience tells
them is approaching for those called to judge and act publicly in
accordance with principles which have been secretly violated in their
own inner being and life? Or does it point to a loss of moral faith, of
hope, of the sense of human dignity; or perhaps sometimes to a secret
and unconquerable feeling of disgust and disappointment in having missed
the true aim of life? It is scarcely two years since we heard of the
suicide of M. Anderegg, the elect of the people, called to fill the
