highest place in the Republic—that of President of the Confederation. It
was on the eve of the day of his installation when, walking up and down
the promenade by the side of the Bernerhoff and Federal Palace, he sat
down on a bench, and apparently overcome by some strange and deep
melancholy at the thought of what awaited him on the morrow, or perhaps
by some thought of pain unshared by any human friend or counsellor, he
put a pistol to his head, and blew his brains out. M. Lambelet,
President of the Grand Council of the Canton of Neuchâtel, ended his own
existence by taking poison. M. Vuithier, his successor, and President of
the same Grand Council, a man of wealth and apparently possessing every
outward advantage which the world can bestow, disappeared from his home,
and after a long search his body was found in the deep waters at the
foot of the cascade of the Doubs in the Jura. We recollect, too, Colonel
Ott, National Councillor at Berne—the great engineer—who drowned himself
in the river at la Matte, at Berne.[40]

The Swiss patriot of to-day may thus have serious grounds for sadness or
apprehension in considering the actual state of his country, in the
centre of Europe, as it is, and surrounded by nations, whose
overflowings, so to speak, are continually invading his land. How is
anything like nationality to be preserved? The difficulties of this
subject have urged some of the best men of Switzerland towards the idea
so well enunciated by some of them, that their future hope must lie in
_international action_. Already that country has the honour of having
been the cradle of useful, progressive, international ideas, such as the
Universal postal system, the Croix Rouge, Arbitration, &c. This fact may
indicate perhaps the path of the future. We live in international times.
In England, insularly situated as we are, and of a character still
exclusive, we are less conscious of this than are the inhabitants of
Continental nations. The energy which is wanting to combat the surging
tide of demoralising influences partly derived from other lands, which
is now invading Switzerland, may be evoked by co-operation with those in
other countries who still remain faithful to the highest principles. I
do not here in any sense imply a necessity on the part of the
constitutional and law-loving portion of the Swiss population to have
recourse to external help in the re-establishment of respect for law and
for liberty in their own nation. All that is aimed at is that moral
force derived from a sense of a community of interests of the highest
kind and in the highest degree, among persons of all lands, from the
constant interchange of ideas, from the propagation and popularising in
many languages of true and living thoughts and principles. Principles
are of world-wide application, and those members of the present Cantonal
Governments of Switzerland who have shown themselves so unworthy of
their great traditions of the past, cannot, even if they would, repel
from their soil the steady, silent, irrepressible inroads of the truth,
though they may persecute and expel certain individuals of their own or
other nationalities who have brought home to their consciences the
disagreeable fact of their own degeneracy.

I cannot conclude the present record without an expression of the grief
and dismay with which I have observed, during the past few years, the
decay amongst educated—and unhappily amongst Christian—people, both at
home and abroad, of the sense of justice. I speak of justice pure and
simple, justice in the abstract. The idea of justice in this sense
appears to be a thing past the comprehension of many persons. I may
illustrate what I mean by the following quotation from the _Standard_ of
Feb. 10, 1883. It is a case brought before Lord Granville in a
correspondence which passed between the Foreign Office and Messrs.
Raphael, Tuck and Son, in reference to the expulsion of their
representative from St. Petersburg on the 15th August previously.
“Messrs. Raphael and Tuck ask the favour of a definite reply as to
whether it was in the power of Her Majesty’s Government to secure a
permit for their representative from the Russian Government, such permit
authorising him to prosecute his journey southward _en route_ for the
Bosphorus, or ‘_whether they must abandon all hope of securing for Mr.
Marcuse, a British subject, the protection of Her Majesty’s Government,
because of his profession of a faith, the disqualification attending
which an advanced civilization was supposed to have swept away_.’” We
have here the astounding fact of a man’s religious opinions publicly
suggested as a possible hindrance to the success of his appeal for legal
justice from our Government. I may further illustrate my meaning by
recalling an incident of my stay in Switzerland during the past summer.
In the presence of several jurists and others, who were consulting
together concerning the treatment of the Salvation Army in their
country, one of these gentlemen said, “if England has begun to deal out
legal justice in proportion to her sympathies and antipathies, she has
entered upon the fatal, downward path.” Another gentleman said to me,
with some indignation, “It is very strange to us to see the utter
inaction of your people in England, and of your Government, in the case
of so gross an injustice as Miss Booth has suffered. We suppose that
this is owing to the absence of sympathy for her religious opinions. It
is generally felt here that if it had been Mr. Charles Bradlaugh who had
suffered such an injustice—or, indeed, a much less one—there would have
been no lack of English defenders to come forward on his behalf.” My
reply to this was not, perhaps, such as the speaker expected. “You give
my countrymen too much credit, Sir—(I was obliged, with much regret, to
say). Indeed I do hope that they _would_ have arisen in defence of Mr.
Charles Bradlaugh in any case of his having suffered a legal injustice;
but I fear that a large party would have been as indifferent to any
injustice inflicted on him as at the present a large party is
indifferent to an injustice inflicted on Miss Booth, simply for the
reason that our people in England have, unhappily, begun to slide into
the downward path which has been designated by one of yourselves just
now—that of allowing justice to be dealt forth in proportion to their
sympathies or antipathies, religious, social, or political.” I was
grieved to find that the truth of what I feared had been so quickly
appreciated by those Swiss gentlemen, that England has forgotten to some
extent the sound traditions by which we were taught to apply to all
alike the great principles of justice and of the common law. But, I seem
to hear you say, “Such an one being anti-Christian is the enemy of the
Cross of Christ, and therefore my enemy! ought I to incur trouble and
effort on his behalf in case he were denied justice before the law?” Do
I need to remind you—Christian—of the words; “If thine enemy hunger,
feed him; if he thirst, give him drink?” Perhaps you assent, however, to
that; you would supply his bodily needs, were he in extremities. But
hear again;—“Man does not live by bread alone.” Stronger than all bodily
needs, deeper even than the love of kindred and country, and of freedom
itself, lies buried in the heart of man the _desire for justice_. The
cry for justice is rising on every hand. You must give to your greatest
enemy that which his soul craves.

It is especially disastrous that religious and Christian people should
have allowed this sense of justice to have become enfeebled; for from
them above all others the maintenance of the sternest principles in this
respect should be expected. I do not like to appeal in addressing
Christian people to so low a motive as that of self-interest; yet it is
impossible not to remind them that while through an enfeebled sense of
justice, and the letting go of the principles of legality, they are
allowing wrongs to be inflicted on persons with whose religious opinions
they have no sympathy, (they standing by as indifferent spectators); the
time may come—nay it assuredly will come—when they themselves will be
bereft of the safeguards of justice, in like manner, and on account
perhaps of the very opinions which they hold as Christians; and then
with what force can they appeal to the great idea of abstract justice,
they themselves having cast it under their feet? A lady to whom I had
recited recently some details of the outrages against the great
principles of justice and law which had been perpetrated in Switzerland,
naïvely asked the question: “But had not Miss Booth and her friends done
some things in Switzerland which are contrary to _good taste_—had they
not shown themselves wanting in _tact_ in their mode of procedure?”
Astonishment prevented my replying. I could only say to myself: “Is it
possible that we have come to this, that not only the holding of
religious opinions, or the observance of religious practices from which
we differ, weakens our sense of responsibility towards our fellow-men in
the matter of legality, but that even a want of good taste or of tact is
to be a excuse in our eyes for the relaxation of the great principles of
justice?” Have we forgotten that still in our own country the law, in
theory at least, is so strict, so absolute in its declarations of the
inviolability of the person, and the sacredness of individual liberty,
that if we dared to touch the person of a criminal on his way to
execution or in his prison cell in an insulting or injurious manner, we
should be considered ourselves criminal. The person of the lowest felon
condemned to death is before the law sacred even to the last moment. It
is not for the virtuous nor for the orthodox, nor for those who never
offend against “good taste,” that the common law guarantees its
protection, but for all alike, of whatever character.

To those among us who have studied the history of our own country, the
growth and expansion of its liberties, the various attacks upon those
liberties, and their re-establishment by the sufferings—even unto
death—of those who defended them, the last year’s events in Switzerland
have a great significance. We have had the opportunity of observing the
acting out before our eyes, on a comparatively small national platform,
that which we always knew, from our deep study of those principles, must
occur. So much so, that as one violation of liberty took place, we
could, almost with certainty, prophecy what the next would be, and in
what manner it would come about. We have seen that when the evil
passions of men—in all ages and all countries the primary and intimate
cause of the destruction of liberty—increase and run riot in the
community, political freedom becomes an impossibility. We have seen
surging up from underneath the elements which have brought about the
ruin of peoples, namely, the wide-spread drunkenness, increasing in all
classes of the community, leading to delirium, to the casting off of all
self-restraint, and to every murderous and evil impulse;—moral impurity,
expressing itself in the grossest forms, the repudiation of family ties,
contempt for women, and the acceptance of the public official patronage
of organised prostitution. Arising out of this, also, we see political
corruption in every department, beginning with the miserable necessity
which short-sighted aspirants for office believe themselves to be under,
of relying for success on the votes of the most degraded and brutalised
amongst the people, bribery in its worst forms, and flatteries of the
most disgusting kind interchanged between the vicious and drunken class
and their representatives. We have seen Governments chaining up liberty,
and giving the reins to license. When the conflict for Governmental
offices is surrounded with such a miasma of immoral elements as this, it
is not surprising that the best men, the true Swiss, those who have not
forgotten their past history, should hold back and refuse to enter into
such an arena. From this point, those who understand something of the
great principles now at war, will watch, with deep anxiety, the efforts
made by those persons who constitute the leaven to keep their country
from corruption. There is a saying that “unfinished questions have no
mercy for the peace of nations.” Swiss citizens are in the presence of a
great unfinished question at the present time. Every principle dear to a
free people has been violated in their midst. These principles will
avenge themselves sooner or later, but through whom, and by what means?
There can be no peace in Switzerland while these “unfinished questions”
are allowed simply to lapse. They must be fought out to the end.

What is our hope? It is in the revival among the masses of the people of
the fear of God, of the belief in the vital truths of Christianity, and
of those habits of virtue and self-restraint which grow up as the
inevitable fruits of the Spirit of God, in those who are enlightened by
it. We have heard it remarked: “It is not surprising to hear of such
things happening in Switzerland. Democracy will, of course, always
produce such results.” Such judgments are exceedingly childish. He must
be a very poor historian or observer of men and principles, who can
attribute to the _outward forms_ of government alone all the complicated
movements which we see among the mass of men in all lands, the suffering
and restlessness, and the rebellion, more or less repressed now, but
which is preparing for a future manifestation. The greatest instance in
modern history—perhaps in the world’s history—of the horrors of mob
rule, the French Revolution, was not the natural outcome of Democracy.
The Reign of Terror followed swiftly upon centuries of monarchical rule,
aristocratic privileges and the suppression of popular liberties? In
Germany and in Russia the same fierce elements are at work;—the same
dissatisfaction, the same rebellion against the religion of those who
monopolise all earthly advantages. These elements are only restrained
for the present by the heavy hand of Imperial power. The inward
transforming influence which alone can truly restrain, is wanting. It is
only a question of time. The longer these elements—call them what you
will, Socialism, Nihilism, &c., are violently held in check, without the
application of the true remedy, the greater and more terrible will be
their ultimate expression. The outward repression having been slight in
Switzerland, the overflowings of the evil have come early, and have been
more rapid and evident. But under every form of Government there are,
more or less, the same elements at work. The only true solution of the
problem is to be found in the revival of the true life of the immortal
beings who compose this volcanic substratum of humanity. If this
judgment of the case is true, in how grave and awful a light must appear
the deliberate rejection of influences which tend towards the revival of
that spiritual life. The Gadarenes prayed our Lord to depart out of
their coasts; Jerusalem would have none of Him; and speedy and swift was
the vengeance which fell on those nations who judged themselves unworthy
to receive the message of eternal life.

But we hope better things for Switzerland. The following testimony is
borne by a correspondent of “Evangelical Christendom” (Nov. 1):—“The
appearance of the Salvation Army in Switzerland has brought out the
hidden, or smouldering enmity against vital, vivid Christianity as
strongly as did Vinet and the Free Church movement in 1845–7, and the
visit of the Haldanes and the quickening of religious life and doctrine
twenty years before that.... Neuchâtel has the reputation, I believe
justly, of being the most pious and gospel-loving city in
French-speaking Switzerland, in which the National Church and the Free
Church vie with each other (with little exception) as to which shall
give the clearest gospel. Yet beneath all those who attend church,
chapel, and school, there seems to have lain, in darkness and the shadow
of death, one of the most compact masses of unknown or untouched
ruffianism, that could be found anywhere. It is well that this be
brought to light. It is turning serious minds, in all the churches,
whatever may be their opinion about the ‘Army,’ towards the undiscovered
fields of mission labour that lie beneath their very feet.”

During the publication of this record, the “Bitter Cry of Outcast
London” has sounded through the length and breadth of our land, and we
are reminded that not from Switzerland alone, or from London alone,
there is arising an exceeding bitter cry, and that in the midst of the
greatest activity applied to outward reforms, in the matter of
dwellings, wages, education, &c., the “heart-leverage,” as it has been
called, is still the first and the only efficient remedy.

As watchmen on their watch-towers, as the “King’s remembrancers,” those
who have faith in God must cry night and day to Him that He will send
forth the Mighty Spirit to “breathe on these slain that they may live.”


 THE END.

 APPENDIX


I. The persecutions are continued in Switzerland, not now against the
English alone, but against the Swiss Salvationists. The events which
have transpired since the trial at Boudry would themselves till a small
volume, as a sequel to the above narrative; but it is necessary to close
the first act of this drama, already too lengthy perhaps, even in order
to resume with deliberation the study of the conflict which is going on,
and which will probably not be quickly ended. There has been a trial of
Salvationists at Rolle, and another at Morges, the details of which are
significant. The harmless and unoffending young wife of Capt. Becquet,
whom he had left behind him for a time in Switzerland, and who is not an
officer in the Salvation Army, was brutally attacked in the street of a
Swiss town, while passing with her baby in her arms. A friend,
witnessing this attack, came forward to defend her, and drive off her
assailants. He was arrested and made prisoner for a night, and fined
eighteen francs for doing so. Lieutenant Thonger is in prison, and
others are fined, or expelled.

(III.) The following is a statement which will sufficiently prove the
absurdity of the assertions which have appeared in the Press, that there
is a mystery about the disposal of the finances of the Salvation Army.
The balance-sheets may be seen at Gresham Buildings by those who desire
to see them.

“The income of the Salvation Army is arranged in two distinct
divisions:—

“1st.—There are the amounts sent direct to head-quarters by subscribers
and others.

“The particulars of this amount and of the expenditure of the same, with
summary of the trade accounts, are published in the annual
balance-sheet, which may be had on application to us, or to the
head-quarters of the Army.

“The books at head-quarters are not only audited by us, but are under
our direct and continuous control, so that we have the full knowledge of
all income and expenditure of every kind whatsoever relating to this
division.

“From none of these sources of income does the General or Mrs. Booth
draw any moneys for their own personal income, nor have they done so for
the fourteen years during which we have had the audit of the accounts.

“2nd.—The second division of income of the Army consists of the local
contributions.

“These amounts are raised and spent locally by the duly appointed
treasurers and secretaries of each separate corps for their own local
expenses, under the direction of the officers, they are examined by
local auditors, and are under the direct supervision of head-quarters.

 “(Signed) JOSIAH BEDDOW & SONS, _Chartered Accountants_.

 “2, Gresham Buildings, Basinghall Street, London, E.C.
                                  _March 8, 1882._”

 ENDNOTES


Footnote 1:

  Lettre à Madame la Comtesse de Gasparin.

Footnote 2:

  “Brief Sketch of the Evangelical Alliance.” By Mr. Arnold, Secretary
  to the English Branch.

Footnote 3:

  I do not intend to enter upon the great question of the public
  ministry of women. It is too large a question for the compass of this
  paper. Its solution is at hand. It is one of the questions of the day
  which are pressing at our very door. I will only say here one word,
  namely, that God has evidently, and in the face of the whole world,
  signally blessed and honoured the public ministry of women in the
  Gospel, and no doubt _He will know how to justify Himself in so
  doing_.

Footnote 4:

  I find no question so frequently asked in England in reference to this
  matter as the following: “but have not the Salvation Army given ground
  in Switzerland for the attacks made on them, by their processions,
  obstructions in the streets, noisy music, &c.?” I think it well,
  therefore, to further emphasize my reply to such enquiries by giving
  the following testimony, in the original, of a gentleman, an
  independent observer, in Geneva. He says, “Un des principaux reproches
  qu’en Angleterre on adresse à l’Armée du Salut ce sont des processions
  dans les rues, avec accompagnement de musique ou de chants. _Jamais_,
  à Genève, l’Armée du Salut ne s’est montrée sur la voie publique; et,
  en ce qui concernait, soit ses affiches, soit sa musique dans
  l’interieur des locaux, elle s’est promptement conformée aux
  observations qui lui ont été adressées à ce sujet.”

Footnote 5:

  A Swiss lawyer writes:—“The words spoken by M. Héridier in the sitting
  of the Grand Council on the 27th of January, in reply to a question
  asked by Deputy Odier, were the expression of the most direct
  encouragement to mob disturbances which has probably ever been heard
  from the lips of a magistrate.”

Footnote 6:

  Extract from the official reports of the sittings of the Grand Council
  of Geneva.

Footnote 7:

  See Article 50 of the Federal Code

Footnote 8:

  Constitution de la Republique et Canton de Genève; Titre II., Article
  4, page 4.

Footnote 9:

  In the Appendix I give the balance-sheet for that three months, handed
  to me by Miss Booth, who further gave me proofs that all the monies
  collected in Switzerland were also _spent_ in Switzerland, with the
  exception of 1,000 francs (£40) which was a gift from a gentleman
  deeply interested in the work in France, and which he asked her to
  dedicate specially to that work. I append also a statement concerning
  the accounts in England which it would be well that readers should
  mark.

Footnote 10:

  M. Cuenod, the chief of the Cantonal Police of Geneva, has on several
  occasions acted a very kind part towards the English officers of the
  Salvation Army.

Footnote 11:

  In its report of these events, the _Journal de Genève_ remarked: “As
  the following day was Sunday, Mdlle. Charlesworth in true English
  fashion believed and said that she could not go to the Police Court.
  The police, _proud of the success of its little stratagem_, at once
  seized upon this ground in order to expel her from the Canton at six
  hours’ notice.”

Footnote 12:

  The motto of the city of Geneva.

Footnote 13:

  Colonel Clibborn, who was a member of the Society of Friends, speaks
  thus further on these points:—“My attachment to our Society remains as
  deep and strong as ever, while at the same time the conviction of
  being in the place where the Lord would have me is equally strong. The
  grand characteristics of early Quakerism will prove to be again the
  power of this work upon the Continent—the belief in and the enjoyment
  of a full salvation; the baptism of the Spirit; the ‘annointing’ of
  that Spirit, the one qualification for service; opportunity for all to
  ‘prophesy’ in the Spirit; liberty for the exercise of all gifts; open
  and avowed separation from the world, and confession of Christ; the
  right of women to preach. Almost all the best known Evangelistic
  movements on the Continent refuse women this right, or at most barely
  tolerate women’s ministry; and here is just one of the great
  advantages of the Army work. It seems to me strange that so many are
  blind to the fact that any day a great war may break out, which will
  claim all the French men evangelists. Our perfect liberty for all,
  calls out all the gifts of all the converts at the earliest moment,
  and thus raises a band of workers which no war can drain, and no
  persecution exterminate. And now, when the cruel war laws of these
  nations claim the bodies of all their young men from three to five of
  their most vigorous years, and maintain the right to send nearly all
  their able-bodied men to be food for powder, God is raising up a band
  of women in our Army who can carry on the work without interruption,
  come war or peace. Mrs. Booth’s pamphlet on Women’s Ministry is now
  being sold all over the land, and has already produced a marked
  effect. The prejudice against women’s ministry was one of the chief
  sources of the ‘religious’ opposition which our work met with in
  Switzerland.”

Footnote 14:

  For readers who are not familiar with that great prophetical word, the
  fulfilment of which is now beginning to be accomplished before our
  eyes, I give it in full:—“And it shall come to pass afterward that I
  will pour out my Spirit upon all flesh; and your sons and your
  daughters shall prophesy, your old men shall dream dreams, and your
  young men shall see visions: and also upon the servants and upon the
  handmaids in those days will I pour out the Spirit.”—Joel II., 28, 29.

Footnote 15:

  I have seen the originals of a considerable number of the testimonies
  which are quoted in the course of this narrative. All are well
  attested, and it has been carefully ascertained that those who uttered
  them are standing firm in the faith, and in the new life.

Footnote 16:

  It was a religious paper in Neuchâtel which published that the
  Salvation Army work in Switzerland had cost 60,000 francs, making this
  the basis for a long article accusing the Army of draining the
  resources of other missions. This article furnished the Conseil d’Etat
  of Neuchâtel with one of its pretexts for closing the hall, viz., that
  the Salvationists were amassing a fortune. What was the _truth_? The
  Swiss work had not cost even 10,000 francs.

Footnote 17:

  Miss Booth had never made such a declaration; she admitted simply her
  title of _Maréchale_.

Footnote 18:

  This word _ressortissants_ which frequently occurs cannot easily be
  translated into one word in English. It signifies the persons who have
  recourse to the tribunals in either country.

Footnote 19:

  This is not true, no clear reply was ever given to Miss Booth on this
  subject.

Footnote 20:

  The reader is now aware that no such law exists.

Footnote 21:

  It is difficult to render in English the force of this word, the use
  of which implies an accusation against the Salvation Army of making
  capital out of the _credulity of weak-minded people_.

Footnote 22:

  We have already seen that no such regulation exists. The following is
  the text of the regulation made use of by M. Héridier in the expulsion
  of Miss Booth:—

 _Police Regulation upon House to House Collections, March 4th, 1879._

  The Council of State, upon the proposition of the Department of
  Justice and Police decrees:—

  Article I. That no collection from house to house or in the streets
  shall take place on behalf of a religious establishment, public,
  charitable or beneficent institution, or other, &c., &c., nor to the
  profit of a legal foundation, nor of any association whatever, without
  the authorization of the Council of State....

  Article II. Any private person desiring to make a collection from
  house to house, in his personal interest, must previously obtain in
  writing the authorization of the Department of Justice and Police.

  Article VII. Anyone contravening this regulation will be punishable by
  Police penalties indicated in the law 23rd October, 1876, &c.

 Signed by the Chancellor,
 ETIENNE PATRU.

Footnote 23:

  A number of legal authorities are here quoted to substantiate this
  statement. The same authorities have been studied and quoted by
  persons who hold a view opposed to that of the Federal Council.

Footnote 24:

  We have seen that only one such notice, and that a verbal one by the
  police before whom she was called, was ever given to her.

Footnote 25:

  The _Daily News_ remarked in reference to this, that “every English
  Chaplain in Switzerland must now consider himself liable to be called
  to account for any collection made in the churches among the Alps
  frequented by English and American tourists.” But beyond this, the
  inhabitants of every European country are now informed by the Swiss
  Federal Council that they are not free, as they imagined themselves to
  be, to ask for money in public meetings, for every kind of social,
  political, or religious enterprise. They must henceforth understand
  that the authorities of the State have “incontestibly” the right to
  send a policeman to look into the money-box or purse of the
  collectors, and count up the amount, limit, or prohibit, as the case
  may be!

Footnote 26:

  The following appears in the _Nouvelliste_, April, 1883:—“I read among
  your correspondence from Neuchâtel, published yesterday, that the
  whole population observes with pain the attitude taken by M. Aimé
  Humbert in the matter of the Salvationists. This certainly is so false
  and outrageous that I cannot let it pass without a protest. Certainly
  it is not my mission to speak for other people, and I will not say, as
  your correspondent does when speaking of himself alone, ‘the people of
  Neuchâtel;’ but I will say, in my own name, that I am very happy to
  see M. Aimé Humbert—one of the founders of our Republic, and one of
  the defenders of liberty of conscience in our Canton—once more
  supporting so good a cause, while the men now in power are
  endeavouring to stifle religious manifestations which are distasteful
  to them, after the manner of the ancient _régime_. M. Aimé Humbert is
  not, moreover, the only one of our ancient magistrates who continues
  to be the partizan of religious liberty. M. Eugene Borel, late
  Councillor of State and Federal Councillor, expresses the same
  sentiments. He recently wrote that he considered liberty of worship as
  one of the most precious conquests of the Revolution of 1848, and that
  there was no legal ground for depriving the Salvation Army of that
  liberty.”

Footnote 27:

  Persons meeting privately to take tea, _have_, then and at all times,
  a right to the protection of the police against violent assaults from
  persons in the streets.

Footnote 28:

  A number of respectable citizens of Neuchâtel had voluntarily proposed
  to act as special constables. There is no doubt that the result would
  in that case have been very different to that which actually took
  place. The proposal was rejected by the majority of the Council of
  State, to whom it was not pleasing, and it was especially distasteful
  to M. Cornaz, the head of the Department of Justice and Police.

Footnote 29:

  The influence of the drinking habits of the people on the fierceness
  of the opposition to Salutistes is illustrated by the following
  facts:—

  During the first week of the work at Neuchâtel two men were observed
  in the meetings who manifested extreme opposition. One evening while
  Mme. Chabal was speaking, one of them jumped up in a towering rage,
  and, shaking his fist at her, cried, “You old viper! I shall make you
  pay dearly for that!” and left the hall in a violent passion, still
  shouting his threats in the street. This poor man lost his place as
  bailiff through drunkenness a few days later, and on Sunday, 26th
  August, he fell from the second story of a house in Beaume, and died
  several hours after from the injuries received in his fall.

  A workman who had distinguished himself by his furious attacks upon
  the Salvationists and those who attended the meetings in the Mont
  Blanc Hall, several weeks after, when ascending a flight of steep
  stone steps in a state of intoxication, fell backwards, broke his
  neck, and was taken up dead.

Footnote 30:

  I am aware that other letters have been addressed by Mr. Charlesworth
  to the public journals, which seem to invalidate the above. I do not
  consider, however, that I am called upon to explain any apparent
  inconsistencies in this matter which may have come before the public.
  My duty is, in the course of this narrative, merely to present to the
  reader the grounds which Miss Booth had for believing that her young
  companion was in no way quitting the path of duty in remaining by her
  side.

Footnote 31:

  At the Annual Conference of the Federation held last year (1882) at
  Neuchâtel, an old and respected citizen of Geneva spoke as follows of
  the institution of legalised vice in Geneva:—

  “Even under the rule of the most honest and estimable of men, M.
  Cuenod, these evils go on, with all the horrible consequences which
  are involved in the principles of legalized prostitution. Let me tell
  you a fact which will show the truth of my assertions. M. Camperio,
  head of the Department of Justice and Police, promised our Committee
  to frame a Regulation expressly stipulating that every girl was free
  to leave a _Maison Tolerée_ at any time. This Regulation was printed,
  and a copy given to each keeper, with an order to placard it up in his
  establishment. A fortnight after, a member of our Committee obtained
  permission from M. Camperio to go into some of the houses with a
  Police agent, in order to see with his own eyes that the order had
  been carried out. He went into fourteen of them: not a single one of
  them had executed the order of the Department of Police. I am wrong!
  one of the keepers had placarded the new Regulation on the wall behind
  a picture, by which it was completely hidden!

  “During this month a Prussian woman wrote to me begging me to get her
  daughter out of one of these houses. This was at last contrived. That
  young girl said that she had frequently attempted to get out, but the
  door was always fastened by a solid padlock. Another girl had
  succeeded in escaping, by taking the impression of the lock with
  orange peel, and thus getting a false key made through the help of a
  visitor at the house. I have the conviction that a thorough search
  would discover terrible things in Geneva precisely similar to those
  discovered in Brussels.

  “Let me speak to you now of a thing which has deeply wounded my heart
  as a Swiss citizen. Recently a _fête_ took place at Geneva on the
  occasion of a musical contest which attracted a vast number of
  persons, foreigners, as well as Swiss, to our town. The town was
  illuminated and decorated with flags. Know you, ladies and gentlemen,
  what houses were decorated with the greatest taste and expense? which
  were the most splendidly illuminated, so splendidly as to eclipse all
  the others? They were two _Maisons de Tolérance_. Yes, throughout all
  those days of festival we beheld floating from the windows of these
  dens of infamy our Federal flag, the flags of our Cantons, the flags
  of Vaud, Geneva, your flag of Neuchâtel, these emblems of our
  Fatherland. The grand word LIBERTAS streamed along the front of these
  houses, attracting the eyes of all passers by. I will suppose you
  indifferent to the sufferings of the victims of vice; I will even
  suppose you guilty of vice yourselves, but no citizen should endure
  such things: You are Swiss citizens: respect the honour of your
  national flag!”

  At the period of which I am writing, M. Héridier fills the post of
  Minister of Justice and Police, and consequently administers the
  system of legalised vice in Geneva. M. Cuenod, assisted, perhaps, by
  M. Camperio had endeavoured to introduce some _alleviations_ (as
  indicated above) of the sufferings of the girls immured in these
  houses. M. Héridier, on the contrary, is responsible, for having taken
  a step backward; and, as Professor Hornung, a well-known jurist of
  Geneva, has said, has placed the matter in worse position than before.
  Is it to be wondered at that M. Héridier, with the consciousness of
  having thereby increased the darkness of the dark lot of these slaves,
  should have constantly avoided the sight of our girl evangelists,
  sitting “behind a curtain” when they were questioned. Those eyes
  directed to his would alone have said, without the aid of words, “Thou
  art the man.”

Footnote 32:

  _En Avant!_ published at 187, Quai Valmy, Paris.

Footnote 33:

  In justice to the jailer it should be stated that clean straw and
  better plates were provided for her.

Footnote 34:

  Miss Booth was not aware in the least that this was an infringement of
  the prison rules.

Footnote 35:

  “A Corporation has a separate legal existence not dependent on the
  legal position of its members. Every Corporation has a seal, and that
  is its signature. For if there are several individuals in the
  Corporation it boots not for each to sign their name; that is not
  considered by the law as the signature of the Corporation. But when
  the seal is affixed to a document no further writing is necessary, for
  the Corporation is deemed to have signed.”

Footnote 36:

  The Public Prosecutor.

Footnote 37:

  In the interest of accuracy it is necessary to repeat again here that
  the proceedings of the Salvation Army were conducted without any
  bands, or processions, or demonstrations of any kind. When, therefore,
  M. Cornaz speaks of “shows,” he _knows_ there were none. As to the
  “jugglery,” its only existence is in M. Cornaz’s own mind; by the aid
  of it he distorts the text of his country’s laws, his own utterances,
  and the transparent speeches and actions of the members of the
  Salvation Army.

Footnote 38:

  While quoting the above the Geneva journals come to hand which give
  the results of the elections for several of the Cantonal Governments.
  M. Héridier has not been re-elected. It is reported that even his own
  supporters have found some of his recent acts too authoritative, while
  his illegalities and tyrannies have been pronounced by the
  Constitutional party to be beyond endurance. This is followed by his
  election by the Grand Council of Geneva as one of the two deputies
  which the Canton returns to the Swiss _Conseil des Etats_ (a body
  answering to the American Senate). This election is in direct defiance
  of the previous popular vote, and proves that the governing persons
  now in office are more in favour of arbitrary government than the
  common people.

Footnote 39:

  Since the return of M. Vernier to France this little church has been
  without a pastor, “as sheep without a shepherd.” Its members had
  recourse only the more directly to the “Shepherd and Bishop of their
  souls,” and great blessing, it is said, has attended the gatherings
  there. The Elders of the church and others lead the devotions, and
  address the congregation. The Church is now crowded, as never before,
  in a great measure by the very poor, the “outcast” population, whose
  usual habit it is to abstain from church-going altogether. This fact
  gives rise to ridicule on the one hand, to rejoicing and thankfulness
  on the other; for these are the very persons whom it is acknowledged
  are the hardest to reach. In a recent meeting of members of that
  church a grave discussion took place on the question whether women
  should be allowed to pray in the church in the evening or “after
  meetings.” (It will be remembered that it was the prayer of a woman in
  one such after meeting which was the occasion of the rupture in that
  church, followed by the retirement of its pastor.) The question was
  referred to M. Aimé Humbert, whose brief and pregnant answer seems a
  prophesy of the future solution not of this alone, but of many other
  questions—“_Where the_ SPIRIT OF THE LORD _is, there is_ LIBERTY.”

Footnote 40:

  The drinking habits of the Swiss people extend unhappily much above
  the poorer and working classes. The fact that the Canton de Vaud is
  sometimes styled “_le pays des veuves_” (the country of widows), has a
  melancholy significance. It is said that the evil institution
  established by certain vineyard proprietors of the _Cave_, or cellar,
  well fitted up, to which the master of the house retires with his male
  guests after dinner to drink until a late hour, has found its way to
  Berne, and that the degrading custom is even encouraged by some
  official persons, as a help to the attainment of personal political
  ends.

Footnote 41:

  Pour ceux qui auraient ajouté foi au dire des journaux religieux, que
  l’Armée aurait depuis son arrivée à Genève reçu et dépensé fr. 60,000,
  il est bon de faire savoir que depuis l’expulsion les recettes ayant
  atteint le chiffre de fr. 660, le total des recettes (ventes de
  publications non comprises) s’élèvent à fr. 7370.

Footnote 42:

  Deux des officiers sont absolument indépendants de l’Armée quant à
  leurs frais personnels, les autres ne reçoivent aucun salaire, mais il
  est pourvu à leur strict nécessaire.

Footnote 43:

  Cette somme eût été plus forte, si quelques loyers de salles n’avaient
  été acquittés directement par des amis de notre œuvre.
